# ERTS Differences

PBeam follows the ERTS implementation model and observable behavior. This file lists each intentional difference from ERTS and OTP. Add a row in the same PR that adds a difference. Remove the row in the same PR that removes the difference.

| Area | PBeam behavior | ERTS behavior | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Scheduling | One scheduler thread. The run queue has one queue for `max`, one for `high`, and one that `normal` and `low` processes share. The scheduler runs the head of the `max` queue, else of the `high` queue, else of the shared queue. A `low` process goes into the shared queue with the count 8, and each other process with the count 1. The head of the shared queue first decrements its count, and goes to the end of the queue when the count is then more than 0 and it is not the last process of the queue. A process that a message or a signal wakes goes into the queue of its priority, and the current process continues its turn. An exiting process keeps its priority. An exit that continues after the process leaves the process table goes back into the queue of its priority. A request signal of `process_info/2`, `group_leader/2`, or `is_process_alive/1` does not raise the priority of a target whose priority is lower than the priority of the sender (any of `high`, `normal`, or `low` below the sender). Thus a target with other signals in its queue answers later than on ERTS, and while a process of the sender's priority or higher stays runnable, a sender that waits for the answer can wait without limit (for example a `high` sender of `process_info/2` to a `normal` target while another `high` process runs). For a `low` target and a `normal` sender, a probe that is not a committed fixture gives 5 turns of a `normal` process before the answer in PBeam and 1 in `erl +S 1`. This part is not implemented yet. PBeam runs no system task (`erts_internal:request_system_task/3,4` is not supported), so the permanent priority raise of an exit in a system task does not occur. At each schedule-in, a process gets a budget of `CONTEXT_REDS` (4000) reductions. The cost points are the points of the pinned ERTS JIT. A function entry costs 1, and the process switches out after the entry when the budget is then 0 or less. A `return` costs 1, and the process switches out after the return when the budget is then less than 0. A `call_ext` of a light BIF and the `send` instruction cost 1 before the call, and the process switches out before the call when the budget is then 0 or less. The call then runs again in the next turn. A `call_ext` of a heavy BIF, a BIF through its export (`apply`, `apply_last`, or a fun call), and a `bif` or `gc_bif` instruction cost 0. A tail call of a BIF through `call_ext_last` or `call_ext_only` then costs 1 for its `return`, and a tail call through the export costs 0. A local send then uses 4 reductions, and returns its value after a switch when the budget is then 0. A NIF call costs the 1 of the function entry. A BIF that traps to Erlang code switches out the process at the trap, and the entry of the target function costs 1 in the next turn. The signal queue has the ERTS parts (row "Signal queue"): a message that arrives is not fetched, and a receive reads only the inner part. `loop_rec` costs 0 when a message of the inner part is next. At the end of the inner part, it fetches and costs 4, also when no message arrived, or, when the middle part has signals, it handles them with a limit of 100 reductions. Thus a receive that waits costs 4 at the wait and 4 again when a message wakes the process. The process switches out before `loop_rec` when the budget is 0 or less, and `loop_rec` then runs again in the next turn; it also runs again in the next turn when the signal handling used up the budget. `loop_rec_end` costs 1. `process_info/2` of the caller, `recv_marker_reserve`, and `erlang:hibernate/0` fetch with no cost. Each handled signal counts 1 and the extra count of its kind, and the handling costs the count divided by 4, plus 1, reductions. A signal to a process that waits puts it in the run queue, except a monitor signal, which waits until its sender switches out. At schedule-in, a process with signals handles them before Erlang code, with at most 100 reductions when it has Erlang work, else with its budget; it switches out with no Erlang code when it has no Erlang work or no budget left. A process that a signal ends at schedule-in exits in that turn, and in `loop_rec` it exits in its next turn. `lists:member/2` and `erlang:length/1` take at most 16 elements for each reduction of the budget, `lists:reverse/2` and `erlang:'++'/2` take 60, `erlang:'++'/2` with `[]` on the right takes 32, and `lists:keyfind/3` and `lists:keymember/3` take 40000 with no cost. When elements remain, the BIF uses its budget and traps to its export. The process switches out, and the export continues the BIF with no cost in the next turn. The guard BIF `length/1` keeps its trap state and runs the instruction again. A process that switches out goes to the end of the run queue and continues where it stopped. `erlang:yield/0` costs 0 and always switches out the process. `erlang:bump_reductions/1` costs 1 as a light BIF, and then uses the given number of reductions, but not more than 4000, down to a budget of 0. Each switch out adds the reductions of the turn to the reduction total of the process, except the 4 virtual reductions of each local send. `process_info/2` with `reductions` gives this total, and for the caller also the reductions of its current turn. Each item of `process_info/2` costs the caller 1 after the read. `integer_to_list/1` and `integer_to_binary/1` cost 1 for a bignum, and return a runtime error for a bignum when no reductions remain. `integer_to_binary/2` costs from 0 to 4 for a bignum, by its base, and returns a runtime error for a bignum whose cost is more than the reductions left. `maps:merge/2` of two maps that are not both flatmaps costs one reduction for each 8 steps of the ERTS hashmap merge. At the end of the budget, it uses all reductions and traps to an internal export, which continues with no cost in the next turn. PBeam terms share no memory, so PBeam counts the steps of a subtree that both maps share, also for `maps:merge(M, M)`. `erts_internal:map_next/3` takes the pairs and the keys that the budget permits, and costs 4 for each pair and 32 for each key (row "Map iteration"). `ets:insert/2` with a list of two or more objects, or with an improper list, uses 8 units for each reduction: 1 for each object of the check, then 2 for each object of the insert when the units that remain are at least the number of objects, else 1 for each object of a copy and 2 for each object of the insert. A failed copy removes the copies with 1 unit for each object. At the end of the budget, it uses all reductions and traps to an internal export (row "ETS tables"). `persistent_term:get/0` costs 1 for each persistent term, and traps at the end of the budget (row "Persistent terms"). `iolist_size/1`, `iolist_to_iovec/1`, `iolist_to_binary/1`, and `list_to_binary/1` walk the iodata with 8 steps for each reduction: one step for each cell and for the end of each list, and in the copy one more step for each 32 bytes of a binary that the copy takes: a binary of at most 512 bytes for `iolist_to_iovec/1`, and each binary for `iolist_to_binary/1` and `list_to_binary/1`. A walk costs one reduction for each 8 steps, rounded up. When the steps end, the BIF uses all reductions and traps to an internal export, which walks the same cell again in the next turn. `iolist_to_binary/1` and `list_to_binary/1` with at most 100 reductions left use them as virtual reductions and run again in the next turn, and with fewer than 1000 reductions left they walk with a budget of 1000. `binary:match/2` and `binary:split/2,3` run the ERTS Boyer-Moore and Aho-Corasick searches with 10 search steps for each reduction: one step for each comparison and for each 8 bytes of the memchr skip of a Boyer-Moore search, one step for each byte of an Aho-Corasick search, and one step for each part of a global result. When the steps end, the BIF uses all reductions and traps to an internal export, which continues in the next turn and bumps only the steps of its last turn. The items of `process_info/2` that PBeam supports cost 1 each, as on ERTS. `maps:from_list/1`, `maps:from_keys/2`, and `maps:keys/1` count the work units of the ERTS YCF code: 40, 40, and 15 units for each reduction. A loop step uses one unit, a step of a loop in one loop two units, and a step of a loop in two loops four units, and the selection sort of a sort part of at most 16 items uses `n * bits(n) * 2` units. When the units reach the budget, the BIF uses all reductions and traps to an internal export, which continues with no cost in the next turn. A `badarg` of `maps:from_list/1` costs nothing. The sort of `maps:from_list/1` and of `maps:from_keys/2` takes its pivots from the address 0 and the seed 1, so its cost for more than 32 distinct keys is one of the costs that ERTS can give. The measurement of `maps:from_keys/2` uses at most two distinct keys, because the ERTS cost then does not change with the pivots. `persistent_term:put/2` of a new value costs one reduction for each 32 slots of a table copy, traps at the end of the budget, and switches out the process (row "Persistent terms"). `unicode:characters_to_binary/2` and `unicode:characters_to_list/2` run the ERTS chardata walks with 10 loop steps for each reduction: the `utf8_need` walk costs one reduction for each 50 steps, the conversion one for each 10 characters or bytes, and the list build of `unicode:characters_to_list/2` one for each 10 characters. At the end of the steps, the BIF uses all reductions and traps. The exit of a process bumps 100 reductions, uses 80 for each BIF timer to the process, and uses 50 more when it leaves the process table. Then each link, each monitor or alias that it holds, and each monitor of another process or of a TTY resource on it uses 1. Each walk of these entries and of the timers checks the reductions before each entry, and a walk that ends with no reductions left gives 1. The monitors of other processes and of TTY resources on the process go in the order that the process handled their monitor signals, and a TTY resource monitor signal that the process did not handle runs its TTY monitor when the exit answers the signals. The exit then answers the signals in its queue: each signal counts 1, and a monitor signal 5, with a limit of 4 counts for each reduction left. When the reductions end at a check, the exit switches out and continues in its next turn with a new budget and no new bump. Before it leaves the process table, the process keeps its name. After the timers, the exit deletes the ETS tables that the process owns, in creation order, from the reductions left in the turn: each segment of the ERTS hash table of a table uses 32, and 1 for each object in it, from the top segment down. The number of segments and the segment of each object come from the active buckets of the table and the ERTS key hash. The exit switches out when the reductions are less than 0 after a segment or 0 after a table, and the deletion does not change the count of the later exit phases in the turn. A table loses its name and its reference when its deletion starts. The exit cancels the BIF timers in the post-order of the red-black timer tree of the process. These parts are not implemented yet. A local send of a message of more than 64 heap words costs one reduction for each 64 words that ERTS copies, but PBeam terms keep no sharing and no literal origin, so PBeam charges no copy. The backlog entry "Model the process heap for GC reductions and the message copy cost" of `docs/backlog.md` owns this cost. `maps:from_list/1` that finds a bad element or tail after a trap returns a runtime error. Garbage collection costs no reductions (row "Garbage collection reductions"). An undefined function costs the reductions of the Rust result (row "Undefined functions"). `erts_internal:scheduler_wall_time/1` gives `false` for each term other than `true`, as ERTS does when the scheduler wall time is off: it sends a new reference to the caller one time, for the one scheduler, and traps to the pinned `erts_internal:await_sched_wall_time_modifications/2`, which receives it. `true` returns a runtime error: PBeam has no scheduler wall time. A run of `execute_on_schedulers` has one such run queue on each scheduler thread. A process starts on the scheduler that creates it, and only an idle scheduler takes a runnable process from another (row "Work stealing of a run with scheduler threads"), so one process never runs on two threads at the same time. A message or a control signal to a process of another scheduler goes into the inbox of that scheduler in the send order of its sender, and the owner moves it into the signal queue of the target in inbox order (row "Signal queue"). | Many scheduler threads. Each run queue has the priority queues `max`, `high`, and `normal`, and `enqueue_process` puts a `low` process into the `normal` queue with `schedule_count` `RESCHEDULE_LOW` (8) and each other process with 1 (`erl_process.c:3953-3971`; `erl_process.h:124-127`, `373`). `pick_next_process` takes the `max` queue, then `high`, then `normal`, where `check_requeue_process` decrements the count of the head and moves it to the end while the count is more than 0 and the head is not the last process (`erl_process.c:4039-4054`, `9993-10023`). The request signals of `process_info/2`, `group_leader/2`, `is_process_alive/1`, a sync suspend, and a priority RPC raise the priority of a target with a lower user priority to the priority of the sender for the signal handling (`maybe_elevate_sig_handling_prio`, `erl_proc_sig_queue.c:1275-1318`, `3012`, `3062`, `3183`, `3233`, `3423`). A process that exits in a system task of a higher priority keeps that priority (`exit_permanent_prio_elevation`, `erl_process.c:11061-11090`). At each schedule-in, a process gets `CONTEXT_REDS` (4000) reductions (`erl_vm.h:53`; `erl_process.c:10183`, `10435`). The JIT changes `FCALLS` at these points: `i_test_yield` at each function entry (`jit/arm/ops.tab:929-938`; `jit/arm/instr_common.cpp:3086-3119`), `return` (`jit/arm/instr_call.cpp:30-54`), `call_light_bif` (`jit/arm/instr_bif.cpp:378-400`, `583-592`; `jit/arm/ops.tab:687-714`), `send` (`jit/arm/instr_bif.cpp:612-625`; `bif.c:2741-2746`, `2861-2879`; `bif.h:69-96`, `445-452`), the NIF call (`jit/arm/instr_bif.cpp:924-952`), `loop_rec` (`jit/arm/instr_msg.cpp:106-229`), and `loop_rec_end` (`jit/arm/instr_msg.cpp:258-268`). `loop_rec` switches out before the peek when `FCALLS` is 0 or less. At the end of the inner message queue, it calls `erts_proc_sig_receive_helper`, which uses 4 for each fetch (`erl_proc_sig_queue.c:7771-7799`). `erts_proc_sig_fetch` moves the outer signal queue into the inner message queue, or into the middle queue behind a signal (`erl_proc_sig_queue.h:1997-2028`; `erl_proc_sig_queue.c:1332-1440`). With signals in the middle queue, the receive helper calls `erts_proc_sig_handle_incoming` with 100 reductions (`erl_proc_sig_queue.c:7730-7868`). The handling counts each signal and costs `cnt / 4 + 1` (`erl_proc_sig_queue.c:52`, `6968`, `7160`). A non-message signal schedules a process that is not in the run queue (`erts_proc_notify_new_sig`, `erl_proc_sig_queue.h:2063-2075`), and a monitor signal waits in `esdp->pending_signal` (`erl_proc_sig_queue.c:1101-1170`; `erl_process.c:9665-9668`). At schedule-in, `ERTS_PSFLG_ACTIVE_SYS` handles signals with `ERTS_SIG_HANDLE_REDS_MAX_PREFERED` (100) or the budget (`erl_process.c:10318-10352`, `10375`, `10397`). `process_info/2` of the caller (`erl_bif_info.c:1198-1268`), `erts_msgq_recv_marker_insert` (`erl_proc_sig_queue.h:2226-2236`), and `erts_hibernate` (`beam_common.c:1586-1587`) fetch with no cost. A heavy BIF and a BIF through its export do not change `FCALLS` (`jit/arm/instr_bif.cpp:631-674`, `721-839`). A BIF that returns `BIF_TRAP` always switches out the process through `context_switch_simplified`, and the target function costs its entry when the process runs again (`jit/arm/instr_bif.cpp:525-536`, `676-701`; `jit/arm/process_main.cpp:119-163`). The x86_64 JIT changes `FCALLS` at the same points. A process that switches out goes to the end of its run queue (`erl_process.c:6695-6851`). `i_yield` always switches out the process (`jit/arm/ops.tab:664-666`; `jit/arm/instr_common.cpp:3121-3124`). `bump_reductions/1` uses `BUMP_REDS` (`bif.c:5403-5415`; `bif.h:71-78`). A switch out adds `REDS_IN - FCALLS - virtual_reds` to `p->reds` (`erl_process.c:9678-9700`; `jit/arm/process_main.cpp:150-160`), and `ERTS_VBUMP_REDS` adds to `virtual_reds` (`bif.h:85-96`). `process_info/2` with `reductions` gives `p->reds + erts_current_reductions(c_p, p)` (`erl_bif_info.c:2114-2121`; `beam_common.c:2393-2409`), and each item costs 1 (`erl_bif_info.c:1424`, `1601`). The list BIFs read the budget (`ERTS_BIF_REDS_LEFT`, `bif.h:218-219`), and bump their cost or trap to their export (`erl_bif_lists.c:61-352`, `1108-1381`; `erl_bif_guard.c:210-313`). A BIF export costs no reduction (`jit/arm/instr_bif.cpp:721-789`). The guard BIF `length/1` runs as `i_length`, which keeps its trap state in the X registers above the live registers and runs again (`jit/arm/ops.tab:1329-1333`; `jit/arm/instr_bif.cpp:198-337`). `integer_to_list/1`, `integer_to_binary/1`, and `integer_to_binary/2` bump one reduction for each 16 digits of a bignum in their base, and run the BIF on a dirty CPU scheduler when the cost is more than the reductions left (`bif.c:3283-3299`, `3323-3336`; `binary.c:92-108`, `129-142`, `163-178`; `big.c:2589-2597`, `4087-4092`). `maps:merge/2` runs `hashmap_merge`, which takes 8 steps for each reduction, bumps one reduction for each 8 steps, or uses all reductions and traps to `maps:merge_trap/1` (`erl_map.c:1257-1284`, `1425-1466`, `1507`, `1558-1851`). It takes a subtree that both maps share in memory, and two equal arguments, with no step (`erl_map.c:1258-1263`, `1608-1612`). `ets:insert/2` with a list runs the YCF code of `ets_insert_2_list`: `ITERATIONS_PER_RED` (8) units for each reduction, 1 unit for each loop step and each `YCF_CONSUME_REDS(1)`, and a trap to `ets:insert/2` when the counter is 0 or less (`erl_db.c:1812-1949`, `2002-2116`, `2152-2336`; YCF flags in `erts/emulator/Makefile.in:716-728`). `persistent_term:get/0` takes one pair for each reduction, bumps one reduction for each pair, or uses all reductions and traps to `persistent_term:get_all_trap/2` (`erl_bif_persistent.c:324-356`, `805-900`). `iolist_size/1` and `iolist_to_iovec/1` walk with `ERTS_IOLIST_SIZE_YIELDS_COUNT_PER_RED` (8) steps for each reduction, bump `(steps - 1) / 8 + 1` reductions, or use all reductions and trap to `erts_internal:iolist_size_continue/1` or `erts_internal:iolist_to_iovec_continue/1` (`erl_iolist.c:57-63`, `76`, `113-290`, `305`, `354-649`, `1004-1310`). `list_to_binary/1` runs the same walks to `erts_internal:list_to_bitstring_continue/1` with a budget of at least `IOL2B_MIN_EXEC_REDS` (1000), and yields with `ERTS_BIF_YIELD1` at `IOL2B_RESCHED_REDS` (100) or fewer reductions left (`erl_iolist.c:34-35`, `53-55`, `706-914`; `bif.h:501-505`). `maps:from_list/1`, `maps:from_keys/2`, and `maps:keys/1` run YCF code through `erts_ycf_trap_driver` with 40, 40, and 15 units for each reduction (`erl_map.c:279-311`, `321-1182`, `629-642`, `1207-1253`, `2801-2834`; `utils.c:3499-3689`, `3733-3819`). YCF puts `YCF_CONSUME_REDS(1)` at the start of each loop body of a `-f` function, and again for each enclosing loop (`ycf_yield_fun.c:648-679`; `erts/emulator/Makefile.in:730-751`). `erts_qsort` picks its pivots from a hash of addresses and of a scheduler random number (`utils.c:3522-3526`, `3686-3687`), so the cost of `maps:from_list/1` for more than 32 distinct keys changes between runs: measured 7399 to 8298 for 10000 keys, and 17 to 20 for 40 keys, in 400 runs. `BIF_ERROR` in YCF code returns before YCF clears its trap state, so a bad element after a yield makes the driver trap again from the last yield forever, and the call never returns (`utils.c:3757-3773`; probe on `erl +S 1`). `persistent_term:put/2` bumps one reduction for each 32 slots of a table copy, traps at the end of the budget, and suspends the caller (`erl_bif_persistent.c:275-293`, `642-784`, `1034-1139`). The unicode BIFs use `allowed_iterations` and `cost_to_proc` with `LOOP_FACTOR` 10 (`erl_unicode.c:48-49`, `166-186`, `342-1169`, `1637-1912`). A local send bumps one reduction for each 64 copied words, at most 4000 (`erl_message.c:881-886`; `erl_message.h:33-35`), and the copy counts each shared subterm once and no literal (`SHCOPY_SEND`, `global.h:1053`). Each other BIF with a body in the PBeam table has no cost of its own for the measured inputs. `erts_do_exit_process` bumps 100 reductions, `ERTS_CONTINUE_EXIT_FREE` uses 50, and the exit yields when its reductions are 0 or less (`erl_process.c:14229`, `14315`, `14475`, `14543`). A BIF timer costs 80 (`erl_hl_timer.c:2401-2446`, `2455-2491`), and a link, a monitor, an alias, and a resource monitor cost 1 (`erl_process.c:13797-13977`, `14067-14165`). The tree walks check the reductions before each entry and give 1 when they end with none left (`erl_rbtree.h:1342-1452`; `erl_monitor_link.c:439-480`), and the list walk checks after each entry (`erl_monitor_link.c:517-548`). `erts_proc_sig_handle_exit` counts 1 for each signal and 4 more for a monitor signal, with a limit of 4 counts for each reduction left (`erl_proc_sig_queue.c:52`, `7259-7473`). Each switch out of the exit uses all reductions of the turn (`erl_process.c:14795-14833`). `erts_db_process_exiting` deletes the owned tables in creation order from `ERTS_BIF_REDS_LEFT`, bumps their reductions when it ends, and switches out the exit when the reductions are less than 0 after a segment or 0 after a table (`erl_db.c:628-650`, `5031-5144`, `5405-5426`). The local count of `erts_continue_exit_process` does not change (`erl_process.c:14276`, `14360-14366`). A hash table frees its segments from the top down, with `EXT_SEGSZ/64` (32) and 1 for each object for each segment (`erl_db_hash.c:3122-3149`, `3468-3530`). An insert splits one bucket when the objects are more than the active buckets, a delete joins buckets while the objects are fewer than the shrink limit, and a key goes to the slot of `MAKE_HASH` under the size mask (`erl_db_hash.c:153`, `164-209`, `279-282`, `757-762`, `1349-1354`, `3350-3407`, `3648-3818`; `atom.c:83-105`). With one scheduler, no table has fine locks (`erl_db.c:2656-2657`; `erl_db_util.h:388-389`). `enif_monitor_process` sends a monitor signal, so a resource monitor goes into `ERTS_P_LT_MONITORS` in handling order (`erl_nif.c:3957-3989`; `erl_proc_sig_queue.c:6544-6562`), and a monitor signal that the process did not handle runs the resource monitor at exit (`erl_proc_sig_queue.c:7347-7355`; `erl_process.c:13827-13833`). `erts_internal:scheduler_wall_time(true)` turns on the scheduler wall time and gives the old state (`bif.c:5306-5315`). The call waits in a receive for the reply of each scheduler (`erts_internal.erl:759-774`), and the reply comes from the aux work of the scheduler. A scheduler with an empty run queue steals a process from another run queue (`try_steal_task`, `erl_process.c:4640`, `9907`), and `check_balance` moves processes between run queues (`erl_process.c:4894`, `9813`). | #229 adds the budget and the costs of function entries, returns, BIF calls, sends, and NIF calls. #230 adds `erlang:yield/0`, `erlang:bump_reductions/1`, and the reduction total. #231 adds the switch at a BIF trap. #232 adds the fetch boundary and the receive costs. #221 adds the signal queue and the costs of the signal handling. #233 adds the costs and the traps of the list BIFs. #234 adds the costs of the other BIFs whose cost depends on their input. #245 adds the reductions of the process exit and the costs of `maps:from_list/1`, `maps:keys/1`, `persistent_term:put/2`, and the unicode BIFs. #250 adds the exit costs of BIF timers, links, monitors, and signals. #253 adds the exit cost of the ETS table deletion and the order of the TTY resource monitors. #391 adds the costs of `list_to_binary/1`. #699 adds the priority queues. The backlog entry "Model the process heap for GC reductions and the message copy cost" owns the message copy cost, the steps of `maps:merge/2` for a shared subtree, and the garbage collection reductions. The backlog entry "Follow the endless trap of `maps:from_list/1` at each OTP pin change" owns the endless trap of `maps:from_list/1`, and the backlog entry "Run `integer_to_list/1` and `integer_to_binary/1` of a large integer with no reductions left" owns the runtime error of `integer_to_list/1` (#258), of `integer_to_binary/1` (#268), and of `integer_to_binary/2` (#399). Evidence: `preempt_probe:sends/0`, `preempt_probe:calls/0`, `preempt_probe:traps/0`, `preempt_probe:bumps/0`, and `preempt_probe:yields/0` of `fixtures/preempt` give the same run lengths of two busy processes in PBeam and in `erl +S 1`, and `preempt_probe:reductions/0` gives the same `process_info/2` reductions of each operation (`tests/integration/preempt.rs`). `receive_probe:receives/0` and `receive_probe:orders/0` of `fixtures/preempt` give the same run lengths and message orders for receives that fetch, find no message, skip a message, switch out before `loop_rec`, wake the process, and read messages that `process_info/2` or `erlang:hibernate/0` fetched. `signal_reds_probe:all/0` of `fixtures/signal_queue` gives the same reductions of the signal handling (`tests/integration/signal_queue.rs`). `list_probe:costs/1` and `list_probe:longs/1` give the same run lengths for each list BIF and list length, and for a process that calls a list BIF on a long list (`tests/integration/list_reductions.rs`). `input_probe:costs/1` gives the same cost of `integer_to_list/1`, `integer_to_binary/1`, `integer_to_binary/2`, `maps:merge/2`, `erts_internal:map_next/3`, `ets:insert/2`, `persistent_term:get/0`, `iolist_size/1`, `iolist_to_iovec/1`, and the `process_info/2` items for small and large inputs, and `input_probe:longs/1` gives the same run lengths for a process that calls `maps:merge/2` with two maps of 40000 keys, `ets:insert/2` with 16000 objects, `persistent_term:get/0` with 10000 terms, `iolist_size/1` or `iolist_to_iovec/1` with 100000 bytes, or `iolist_to_iovec/1` with 1995 binaries (`tests/integration/input_reductions.rs`). `bif_cost_probe:costs/1` and `bif_cost_probe:longs/1` give the same costs and run lengths of `maps:from_list/1`, `maps:keys/1`, the unicode BIFs, and `list_to_binary/1`, `bif_cost_probe:lows/1` gives the same cost of `list_to_binary/1` with few reductions left, and `bif_cost_probe:puts/1` and `bif_cost_probe:put_trap/0` give the same table copy costs of `persistent_term:put/2`. No exact ERTS cost exists for distinct keys, so the sort inputs of `maps:from_list/1` are two keys in 9 and 73 pairs, whose sort cost is the same for each pivot. `bif_cost_probe:exits/0` gives the same order of the `DOWN` message and the turn of another process for each budget at the exit (`tests/integration/bif_costs.rs`). `exit_probe:exits/2` gives the same order of the `DOWN` and `EXIT` messages of an exit with BIF timers, links, monitors, monitors of other processes, and pending monitor signals and the turns of another process, at the budgets where the exit switches out (`tests/integration/exit_costs.rs`). `exit_owned_probe:tables/2` gives the same turns of an exit that deletes small and large tables, tables with deleted keys, and tables with atom keys, and `exit_owned_probe:ttys/2` gives the same `DOWN` messages of an exit with a TTY resource monitor between other monitors and in its signal queue, at the budgets where the exit switches out (`tests/integration/exit_owned.rs`). `bif_audit_probe:costs/1` gives the same cost of each BIF with a body in the PBeam table for a small and a large input, except the message copy of a send (`tests/integration/bif_audit.rs`). `priority_probe:orders/0`, `priority_probe:lows/0`, and `priority_probe:wakes/0` of `fixtures/priority` give the same run order of processes with each priority, `priority_probe:exits/0` gives the same turns of a `low`, a `normal`, and a `max` exit that continues after the process table, and `priority_probe:flags/0` and `priority_probe:spawns/0` give the same results of `process_flag/2`, `process_info/2`, and `spawn_opt/4` with priorities (`tests/integration/priority.rs`). The reference run checks that `erl` is the JIT flavor and that no busy loop collects garbage. With more than one scheduler, the ERTS interleaving changes between runs. `kernel_refc:init/1` calls `erts_internal:scheduler_wall_time(false)` (`kernel_refc.erl:52-54`, `141-142`). Only `erlang:statistics(scheduler_wall_time)` users turn it on, and no measured path does that. (#698) #842 adds the second scheduler thread and the inbox, and #843 adds the control signals across threads, and #11 adds work stealing. |
| Signal queue | One scheduler. Each process has one signal queue with the ERTS inner, middle, and outer parts, and the messages and signals of all senders go into it in send order. The signals are exit, link, unlink, unlink acknowledgment, monitor, demonitor, monitor down, group-leader, is-alive, process-info, alias message, and flush signals, and the PBeam revocation signal (row "Capability checks"), which costs the count of 1 of each handled signal. At exit, a process sends its link exit signals and its demonitor signals in the post-order of its red-black link and monitor trees, as ERTS does (`src/runtime/process/tree.rs`). It answers its pending signals at the end of its exit. The exit uses reductions and can switch out, as in ERTS (row "Scheduling"). A hibernated process that a signal handling gives messages becomes active until it runs Erlang code again. In a run of `execute_on_schedulers`, a link, unlink, or exit signal to a process of another scheduler goes into the inbox of that scheduler after the earlier messages and signals of its sender, and the owner moves it into the signal queue of the target in inbox order. `erlang:link/1` finds no process of another scheduler when that process exits, as `erts_proc_lookup` finds none with the `EXITING` flag (`erl_process_lock.h:1151-1157`). A signal to a process that exits before its owner applies the signal gets the answer of the exit: a link gets the exit signal `noproc`, an unlink gets its acknowledgment, and an exit signal is ignored. In a run of `execute_on_schedulers`, a monitor or demonitor signal to a process of another scheduler, and a monitor down signal to a watcher of another scheduler, go into the inbox of that scheduler after the earlier messages and signals of the sender, and the owner moves them into the signal queue of the target in inbox order. Thus a `DOWN` message comes after the messages that its target sent before its exit. A monitor signal waits in the pending slot of its scheduler until the switch out of the sender, a message or signal of the sender to the same target, or the next monitor signal of the sender, as in ERTS (`erl_process.c:9665-9668`, `erl_proc_sig_queue.c:1146-1166`, `erl_message.c:463-480`). While it waits, a message or signal of the sender to another process of another scheduler waits behind it and goes into the inbox after it. This gives the order of an ERTS run in which the other scheduler runs the receiver after the sender switches out. Thus a kill that the receiver of the message sends when it gets the message comes after the monitor signal. ERTS sends that message at once, so the other scheduler can run the receiver before the sender switches out, which gives `noproc`, but `erl +S 2` gave `killed` in each of 8000 kills of `kill_watch` in `fixtures/multischeduler/monitor_signals.erl`. `erlang:monitor/2,3` sends the monitor signal also to a process of another scheduler that exits, because `proc_queue_signal` finds an exiting process with `erts_proc_lookup_raw` (`erl_proc_sig_queue.c:1138-1144`, `1168-1173`). That process answers with the monitor down signal `noproc` at its exit (`erl_proc_sig_queue.c:7347-7355`), and a process that left before its owner applies the signal gets the same answer. `erlang:demonitor/2` with `flush` receives in `erts_internal:flush_monitor_messages/3` after the scheduler moved its inbox, so it removes a `DOWN` message of the monitor that arrived from another scheduler. | ERTS has the same queue parts (`erl_proc_sig_queue.h:43-74`) and handles signals as `erts_proc_sig_handle_incoming` (`erl_proc_sig_queue.c:6229-7176`). With more schedulers it uses in-queue buffers and dirty signal handlers. `erts_continue_exit_process` and `erts_proc_sig_handle_exit` use reductions and can yield (`erl_process.c:14270-14833`; `erl_proc_sig_queue.c:7259-7473`). `F_HIBERNATED` stays until the next garbage collection (`erl_gc.c:934`, `1014`; `erl_proc_sig_queue.c:7152-7154`). ERTS also has priority signals, `ERTS_SIG_Q_OP_ADJ_MSGQ`, and signals of code purge, RPC, suspend, tracing, NIF select, and distribution. | #221 adds the signal queue on one scheduler. #10 adds the parts for more schedulers. PBeam has no priority messages (rows "Monitors" and "Unimplemented BIFs") and no sender of the other signal kinds. PBeam has no garbage collection (row "Garbage collection reductions"). Evidence: `signal_queue_probe:all/0`, `signal_reds_probe:all/0`, and `exit_order_probe:all/0` of `fixtures/signal_queue` give the same results in PBeam and in `erl +S 1` (`tests/integration/signal_queue.rs`). The pairs of roots of `fixtures/multischeduler/link_signals.erl` give the same results on two PBeam scheduler threads and in `erl +S 2` (`tests/integration/multischeduler_links.rs`). The pairs of roots of `fixtures/multischeduler/monitor_signals.erl` give the same results on two PBeam scheduler threads and in `erl +S 2` (`tests/integration/multischeduler_monitors.rs`). |
| Atom table | Each run holds one copy of the atom table of the program, and the schedulers of a run with more than one scheduler share that copy. Any scheduler of the run creates an atom, and an atom that two schedulers create has one index. A new atom of a run does not enter the table of the program or of another run, and the host result of the run names its atoms with the table of the run. The limit is 1048576 atoms, as in ERTS. | One atom table holds each atom of the emulator, and any scheduler creates an atom (`erts_atom_put_index`, `atom.c:231`; the table is `atom.c:38`). | PBeam can load more than one program in one OS process, and each program and each run of `BeamProgram::execute` or `BeamProgram::execute_on_schedulers` keeps its atoms apart. The shared copy is safe Rust: a read-write lock guards the name index, and fixed chunks of set-once slots hold the names, so a name read needs no lock. (#842, #929) |
| Work stealing of a run with scheduler threads | A scheduler of `execute_on_schedulers` with no runnable process marks itself hungry at the node while it waits. At its next scheduling step, a scheduler with two or more runnable processes gives processes from the head of its queues, in priority order, to the first hungry scheduler after its own index in cyclic order. The first queue that is not empty gives `len / 2` (at most 100), because it holds the process that the giver runs next, and each later queue gives `(len + 1) / 2` (at most 100); a queue that gives no process lets the search go on to the next queue. The first stolen process runs next on the receiver, and each other stolen `low` process keeps the count that it had left. The thread of the giver makes the handoff, because only that thread reads the state of its processes: the process state and the owner in the process table move together, under the shard lock that each cross-thread sender holds while it reads the owner and writes the inbox, and the deliveries for the process that wait in the old inbox follow it in their order. The hungry mark and each steal use the lock of the idle count, so a scheduler that leaves its wait takes each process that it got before it runs a process. A process moves only when it is in the run queue and does not wait, does not exit, has no dirty call, has no receive timer, is not a system process, and did not use a service that stays with its scheduler: a port (also as the new owner of `erlang:port_connect/2`) or a terminal. Such a process stays with its scheduler for the rest of its life. The resources of the node, which are the `prim_file` files, the `prim_buffer` buffers, the zlib streams, the zstd contexts, the compiled regular expressions, and the compiled binary patterns, resolve on each scheduler, as an ERTS resource or magic reference does (`enif_get_resource`, `erl_nif.c`), so a use of one does not pin the process (#938, #934). The ports and the terminals keep their poll state on one scheduler thread until M15 changes it. A run with a policy process does not steal. A process that holds the reference of such a service of another process and did not use it yet can move, and its next use then gives the unsupported error of that service (rows above and below). No scheduler moves a port, and nothing moves processes for balance. | An empty run queue takes the processes itself: it locks the run queue of a victim with work, from the queues after its own index, and takes `(len + 1) / 2` (at most 100) of the highest priority queue that gives a process, from its head, and goes on to the next queue when a queue gives none (`erl_process.c:4489-4592`). It runs the first one and links the others into its queue with their `schedule_count`, while the victim runs its current process (`try_steal_task`, `try_steal_task_from_victim`, `erl_process.c:4470-4720`). A process bound to its run queue stays (`erts_try_change_runq_proc`, `erl_process.h:2722-2723`). A process changes only its run queue: the process table, the ports, the timers, and the other services are node-wide, so any process can move. A steal can also take a runnable port (`erl_process.c:4603-4613`), and `check_balance` moves processes between run queues (`erl_process.c:4894`, `9813`). | PBeam keeps the state of a process in the slots of its scheduler, and the services in this table stay with each scheduler until the backlog entry "Share the node services of a multi-scheduler run" moves them to the node. The handoff at the next step of the giver is a departure in timing only: the giver counts the process that it runs next as the process that the ERTS victim runs. With two schedulers, the cyclic order is the ERTS order. The handoff depends on a PBeam property: only a scheduler thread writes an inbox, and it does so under the shard lock of the target (row "Run end"). Evidence: `work_stealing:busy/0` and `work_stealing:idle/0` of `fixtures/multischeduler` (`tests/integration/work_stealing.rs`). (#11) |
| ETS tables of a run with scheduler threads | The node keeps the ETS tables, and each scheduler of `execute_on_schedulers` reads and writes them. Each table has one lock, also with `write_concurrency`, so a hash table keeps the traversal order of a one-scheduler run. `ets:info/2` gives the `write_concurrency` and `decentralized_counters` values of ERTS for such a run. `ets:delete/1` of a table returns the runtime error `unsupported ets:delete/1 of a table that a trapped insert uses` only when the trapped insert runs on the same scheduler; a trapped list insert of another scheduler that did not take the table yet is not seen. | A fine-locked table has a lock for each group of buckets, and the order of `ets:first/1` and `ets:next/2` can differ (`erl_db_hash.c`). ERTS checks each trapped insert (`erl_db.c:2077-2107`). | M20 owns the lock-group order. Only the scheduler of a process can read its pending BIF. (#931) |
| Ports of a run with scheduler threads | The node keeps one port table, and a BIF of any scheduler of `execute_on_schedulers` reads and changes each port: `erlang:ports/0`, `erlang:port_info/1,2`, `erlang:port_command/2`, `erlang:port_close/1`, `erlang:port_control/3`, `erlang:port_connect/2`, `link/1`, and `monitor/2` give the result of `erl +S N`. The scheduler that opened a port serves its I/O: its poll waits for the fds of the port, its owner stays pinned to it, and a message of the port to a process of another scheduler goes through the inbox of that scheduler. A close from another scheduler wakes the serving scheduler. Each port has its own lock, as in ERTS (`io.c:923-1002`): a BIF checks the state of the port and runs its operation under one lock of the port, and a close that another scheduler makes before that lock gives the result of a closed port. The blocking write of `erlang:port_command/2` (row "Ports") holds only the lock of its port, not the port table. A BIF of another scheduler on a `tcp_inet` or `udp_inet` port, and an `erlang:port_connect/2` of such a port to a process of another scheduler, return the runtime error `unsupported: an inet port of another scheduler in a run of execute_on_schedulers`, because the receive and accept timers of the port stay with the serving scheduler. | ERTS keeps one port table for the node (`erts_port`, `io.c:76`, an `ErtsPTab` of `erl_ptab.c`), and a port task runs on any scheduler (`erl_port_task.c`). | The poll of the port fds and the port timers stay with the serving scheduler until the node poll thread (#983) and the port tasks on any scheduler (#985). (#982) |
| Atomic arrays of a run with scheduler threads | The node keeps one store for the atomic arrays, and a process of any scheduler reaches an array by its reference. Each operation is a sequentially consistent `std::sync::atomic` operation, so `atomics` and the default `counters:new/2` give the ERTS results on any scheduler. `erts_internal:counters_new/1`, the `write_concurrency` form of `counters:new/2`, stays an unsupported runtime error in each run. | The node keeps one store for the atomic arrays, and a reference reaches the array on any scheduler (`erl_bif_atomics.c:90-110`). `counters:new/2` with `write_concurrency` makes a counter array with one slot for each scheduler (`erl_bif_counters.c`). | The `write_concurrency` form waits for M20. (#842, #933) |
| BIF timers of a run with scheduler threads | A run of `execute_on_schedulers` keeps the timer wheel with each scheduler: the scheduler of the caller of `erlang:send_after/3,4` or `erlang:start_timer/3,4` owns the timer, also for a process or a name of another scheduler. The node timer table holds the owner and the end of each active timer, and the timer tree of each receiver process. `erlang:read_timer/1` and `erlang:cancel_timer/1,2` on any scheduler read or remove the node entry and give the result at once. A cancel from another scheduler then puts a cancel into the inbox of the owner, which removes the wheel entry. The expiry takes the node entry, so a timer that another scheduler canceled first sends nothing, and the message goes to a process of another scheduler through the inbox of that scheduler. A cancel or an expiry on any scheduler removes the timer from the tree of a live receiver at once. The exit of a receiver takes its tree from the node and cancels each timer through its owner, in the post order of the tree. The alias owners are in the node: a message to an alias goes to the owner on any scheduler (#928). | Each scheduler has its own timer wheel, and a BIF timer of a later end is a high-level timer of the node (`erl_hl_timer.c:2617`, `2774`). A read or cancel of a timer of another scheduler that is not a timer of the caller goes as a request to the owner, and a sync call waits for the reply (`access_bif_timer`, `erl_hl_timer.c:2236-2339`). The state of the timer decides between expiry and cancel (`erl_hl_timer.c:1155-1162`). | The values are the ERTS values. PBeam gives the result without the request, so the `{cancel_timer, TimerRef, Result}` message of an async cancel comes at once, which is one of the ERTS orders. The time left uses the clock of the scheduler of the caller, and each scheduler has its own time correction until #936. Evidence: the timer probes of `tests/integration/multischeduler.rs` give the values of `erl +S 2`. (#842, #935) |
| Code loading in a run with scheduler threads | The node keeps the published code table and the NIF library records with a generation number. Each scheduler keeps a working copy with its own import caches. `erts_internal:prepare_loading/2`, `erlang:finish_loading/1`, `erlang:finish_after_on_load/2`, `erlang:delete_module/1`, and `erlang:load_nif/2` take the lock of the node, install the newest copy, run as in a one-scheduler run, and publish the result. Another scheduler installs the newest copy before each external call, `apply`, call of a fun, and spawn, and when it drains its inbox. The install empties the import caches of that scheduler in place, so a call after a code operation that returned finds the new code, as in ERTS. `erts_internal:purge_module/2` is also such a code operation. `erts_internal:request_system_task/3` with `check_process_code` for a process of another scheduler puts the request into the inbox of that scheduler, which installs the newest code, checks the process when it drains the inbox, and replies (`erts_code_purger.erl:342-344`, `erl_process.c:11309-11492`). A process with signals to handle gets the request as a signal behind them and runs the check when it handles it, as ERTS sends the request as a signal then (`erl_process.c:11459-11480`). ERTS runs the check as a system task when the process next runs, at the requested priority or higher. Thus a failed `-on_load` function, `code:purge/1`, and `code:soft_purge/1` give the results of `erl +S N`, and the purge kills a process of another scheduler that runs the purged code (#959). The replacement of a loaded module stays the runtime error of a one-scheduler run (row "Code loading"). | ERTS commits a new code index and the caller of `erlang:finish_loading/1` waits for thread progress, so each scheduler sees the new code at its next external call (`code_ix.c`, `beam_bif_load.c:381-507`, `555-566`). | A later code replacement or purge of old code changes only the published table and the operations, not the publish and install path. (#842, #937, #959) |
| Compiled regular expressions of a run with scheduler threads | The node keeps the serialized code of each compiled regular expression (`pcre2_serialize_encode`, the bytes of `re:compile/2` with `export`) by its reference. Each scheduler decodes the bytes once, at the first use of the expression on that scheduler, into a cache of its own, as `re:import/1` decodes them (`erl_bif_re.c:761-789`). `re:run/2,3` with an expression that another scheduler compiled, also through `persistent_term`, gives the ERTS result, and a use does not pin the process. | A compiled expression is an ERTS magic reference, and any scheduler reads the one compiled code (`erl_bif_re.c:581-615`). | A `pcre2_code` that PBeam owns is a raw pointer, so it is not `Send`, and the project rules forbid a new `unsafe impl Send`. The cache costs one decode for each scheduler that uses an expression, and one copy of the code for each such scheduler until the run ends. Evidence: `ping_pong:regex_run/0` and `ping_pong:term_patterns_use/0` of `fixtures/multischeduler` (`tests/integration/multischeduler.rs`). (#934) |
| Compiled binary patterns of a run with scheduler threads | The node keeps the compiled binary patterns in its resources. `binary:match/2,3`, `binary:matches/2,3`, and `binary:split/2,3` with a pattern that another scheduler compiled, also through `persistent_term`, give the ERTS result, and a compile or a use does not pin the process. | A compiled pattern is an ERTS magic reference, and any scheduler resolves it (`erl_bif_binary.c:1004-1101`). | No difference in results. A call reads the pattern under the lock of the node resources and releases the lock before the search. Evidence: `ping_pong:pattern_match/0`, `pattern_split/0`, `pattern_matches/0`, and `term_patterns_use/0` of `fixtures/multischeduler` (`tests/integration/multischeduler.rs`). (#934) |
| Timers | PBeam has one timer queue on the run clock. The run clock is the ERTS internal monotonic time: one second at the run start, in nanoseconds, from `std::time::Instant`. A receive timer of `wait_timeout` and a BIF timer of `erlang:start_timer/3,4` or `erlang:send_after/3,4` end at a clock tick of 1 ms, as in ERTS. Timers with the same end always expire in start order. PBeam expires each due timer on the last time read before it takes the next process, and it reads the clock after `ERTS_CHECK_TIME_REDS` reductions and after each wake, so a timer never expires while a process runs. When no process can run, PBeam sleeps until the first timer end, or waits for fd 0 until then when fd 0 has a read select (see "Input select"). The run ends when the root process exits, also while a timer is active (see "Run end"). | Each scheduler has a timer wheel, and a timer with a time of `ERTS_TIMER_WHEEL_MSEC` or more is a high-level timer in a tree of the scheduler (`erl_time.h:156`, `erl_hl_timer.c:2617`, `2774`). A scheduler reads the clock and bumps its timers after `ERTS_CHECK_TIME_REDS` reductions and before it sleeps (`erl_process.c:9797-9810`, `3452-3620`). A timer that ends less than 2¹⁴ clock ticks (16384 ms) after the wheel position goes to the tail of its soon wheel slot (`time.c:647-668`, `1256-1300`), so these timers expire in start order. A timer with a later end starts in the later wheel. ERTS moves it to the tail of its soon wheel slot about 8 to 16 seconds before its end (`time.c:95-113`, `1030-1130`), so it can expire after a timer with the same end that started later (measured: a timer `a` of 20000 ms and, 3700 ms later, a timer `b` with the same end expire as `[b,a]`). A timer can expire while its receiver runs on another scheduler. ERTS corrects the Erlang monotonic time, and the node does not end when no process can run. | PBeam has one scheduler thread. Each scheduler reads the clock after its processes used `ERTS_CHECK_TIME_REDS` (4000) actual reductions and before it sleeps, as ERTS does (#987). No measured path observes the ERTS time correction. The `supervisor` hibernate timer is 1000 ms, the `logger_olp` idle timeout is 100 ms, and the `logger_h_common` file sync timer is 5000 ms. The one measured timer of 16384 ms or more is the `erl_prim_loader` idle timeout of 360000 ms, which the loader starts again for each message that it receives (`erl_prim_loader.erl:117`, `211`, `503`). No measured run lasts 360 s, so this timer does not expire in a measured run. The file sync timer runs only for a `logger_std_h` handler of type `file`, because the default `standard_io` handler gets `filesync_repeat_interval => no_repeat` (logger_std_h.erl:368-371). (#150) (#258) |
| Clocks | `erlang:monotonic_time/1` reads the run clock of "Timers" and adds the ERTS offset, so the Erlang monotonic time at the run start is -576460752000000000 in `native`, as at the ERTS emulator start. In a run of `execute_on_schedulers`, the schedulers share the run start and one time correction state of the node, as ERTS keeps one `time_sup` (`read_corrected_time`, `erl_time_sup.c:385-412`), so `erlang:monotonic_time/0,1` and `erlang:time_offset/0,1` agree across the scheduler threads also after a check. ERTS has one check timer in the wheel of one scheduler (`erl_time_sup.c:913-950`, `1305-1322`). PBeam does the check on the first scheduler that reaches the check tick, and the other schedulers take the next check tick of the node (#936). The run clock starts at each `BeamProgram::execute`. `os:system_time/1` reads the host `CLOCK_REALTIME`, as ERTS does. `erlang:system_time/1` adds the time offset of the run to the run clock and does not change the offset, as `system_time_1` does (`erl_time_sup.c:2527-2535`). The offset at the run start is `CLOCK_REALTIME` minus the run clock (`erl_time_sup.c:1240-1250`). PBeam does the time correction of the pinned defaults, time correction on and `multi_time_warp`, from an internal timer of "Timers" on the ERTS schedule (`late_init_time_correction`, `erl_time_sup.c:913-950`). The internal timer expires in the end and start order of the other timers. A wait of an idle run ends at the internal timer, but the internal timer alone does not keep a run alive. PBeam selects the check as the ERTS build does. On Linux, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` is corrected by the OS (`make/autoconf/otp.m4:945-958`), so the check is `check_time_offset` (`erl_time_sup.c:789-831`): the first check is 1.5 seconds after the start, each next check is 60 seconds later, and a check moves only the offset. On each other Unix host, for example with `CLOCK_UPTIME_RAW` on macOS, the clock is not corrected, so the check is `check_time_correction` (`erl_time_sup.c:450-777`, `835-885`): the first check is 15 seconds after the start. This check moves the offset at a step of more than `2 * small_diff`, 100 microseconds, and corrects the run clock for the drift of the host monotonic clock and for the error to `CLOCK_REALTIME`. After a change, it keeps the 15-second interval for 10 checks. The constants of the drift correction come from the clock resolution of the pinned macOS host, 1000000 Hz (measured: `os_system_time_source`): 5 drift intervals, the average of the intervals, and a drift error of 2133 nanoseconds (`erl_time_sup.c:1156-1212`). On a host that is not Linux and has a different resolution, ERTS calculates other constants, and PBeam keeps these. A change of the offset sends no `CHANGE` message, because `erlang:monitor(time_offset, clock_service)` is not supported. PBeam does not stop the run when the host monotonic clock steps backward (`erl_time_sup.c:475-477`), because `Instant` is monotonic. PBeam does not check the Linux kernel version, and ERTS uses a different clock before Linux 2.6.33 (`sys_time.c:283-310`). `erts_internal:perf_counter_unit/0` always returns 1000000000. | The clock starts at the emulator start. The Erlang monotonic time at the start is `ERTS_MONOTONIC_TIME_START_EXTERNAL`, -576460752000000000 (`erl_time.h:318-321`, `331-340`; measured: `erlang:system_info(start_time)`). With time correction, the default, ERTS adjusts the Erlang monotonic time to the OS system time (`check_time_correction` in `erl_time_sup.c:450-777`). The pinned default time warp mode is `multi_time_warp` (`erl_init.c:226`; measured: `erlang:system_info(time_warp_mode)` gives `multi_time_warp`). In this mode, ERTS moves the time offset when the OS system time steps (`erl_time_sup.c:497-504`, `807-817`). `erts_internal:perf_counter_unit/0` returns `ERTS_HRTIME_UNIT`, 1000000000 (`sys_time.c:1032-1041`; measured on the pinned aarch64 host). On x86, ERTS uses the measured TSC frequency only when bits 8 and 23 of the `cpuid` leaf `0x80000007` `edx` are set (`have_rdtscp` in `sys_time.c:989-1007`, `ethr_aux.c:167-168`). Intel and AMD define no bit 23 in that register, because the bit numbers come from the Linux software flags `constant_tsc` and `tsc_reliable`. Thus ERTS returns 1000000000 also on a current x86_64 host. | One `BeamProgram::execute` is one run, as one emulator is in ERTS. No measured path observes the time correction: `logger` reads the clocks only for timestamps (`logger.erl:728-729`, `logger_olp.hrl:75`, `logger_std_h.erl:850-851`). No measured path reads the `perf_counter` unit. (#154) `erlang:universaltime/0` gives the UTC date of the POSIX seconds of the clock with the ERTS algorithm `seconds_to_univ`, and `erlang:localtime/0`, `erlang:date/0`, and `erlang:time/0` read the local time with `localtime_r`. A time zone that holds leap seconds differs: the `gmtime_r` of glibc subtracts the leap correction there and PBeam adds none, and the `posix2time` path of `erlang:universaltime_to_localtime/1` is the only one that adds the leap seconds of macOS. (#756) `global:init/1` reads `erlang:system_time(nanosecond)` only to seed `rand` (`global.erl:865-867`), so no measured path observes the offset change. (#502, #691) |
| Heap limit | `process_flag/2` and `system_flag/2` accept `min_heap_size`, `min_bin_vheap_size`, `fullsweep_after`, and `max_heap_size` with the value checks of ERTS, store the value on the process or as the node default that a new process takes, and return the old value. `process_info/2` reports the four values, and the `garbage_collection` item of `process_info/1` holds them with `minor_gcs` 0. PBeam does no garbage collection and does not measure the heap, so the values change only what the BIFs report: `max_heap_size` with `kill => true` does not kill a process, and with `error_logger => true` it writes no report. A minimum heap size at or above the largest heap size of ERTS returns the runtime error `no next heap size found: N`. `system_flag/2` keeps the node minimum heap sizes as a 32-bit `int`, as ERTS does, so a large size wraps. A wrapped negative node minimum becomes a 64-bit unsigned value on an ERTS process (`erl_process.c:12568-12569`); PBeam keeps the negative value there and does not reproduce it. | `max_heap_size` can stop a process when its heap gets too large. Messages count toward the heap only when `message_queue_data` is `on_heap`. The heap flags change the heap sizes and the collections of the process (`process_flag_2`, `bif.c:2101-2166`; `system_flag_2`, `bif.c:5037-5105`). A minimum heap size at or above the largest heap size stops the emulator (`erts_next_heap_size`, `erl_gc.c:335`). | PBeam uses Rust memory management, not a per-process heap (owner decision of 2026-10-23). #1066 and #1065 narrow the difference. `garbage_collection` of `process_info/2` and the heap flags of `system_info/1` stay unsupported. (#1062) |
| Garbage collection reductions | PBeam does no garbage collection, so a process that allocates uses no reductions for it. `lists:reverse/2` and `erlang:'++'/2` always cost one reduction for each 60 elements that they copy. `erts_internal:garbage_collect/1`, which `erlang:garbage_collect/0` calls, returns `true` for `minor` and `major` and costs no reduction for a collection. | A garbage collection costs the process at least 1 reduction, more for a large heap (`erl_gc.c:218-230`, `966-981`; `jit/arm/beam_asm_global.cpp:121-124`). Thus a process that allocates switches out earlier. `lists:reverse/2` and `erlang:'++'/2` copy 60 elements for each reduction only while the heap has free words. Then they copy to a heap fragment, 40 elements for each reduction, and a garbage collection can follow the BIF (`erl_bif_lists.c:111-174`, `219-221`, `1144-1192`, `1235-1237`; `jit/arm/instr_bif.cpp:488-514`, `549-574`). Measured: `lists:reverse/2` of 10000 elements costs 166 with a heap of 2000000 words, and 2386 with the default heap. `erts_internal:garbage_collect/1` collects the heap of the caller and adds the reductions of the collection to the caller (`bif.c:4076-4090`; `erl_gc.c:984-993`). | PBeam has no process heap (row "Heap limit"). The fixtures of `tests/integration/preempt.rs` and `tests/integration/list_reductions.rs` allocate no heap that ERTS collects in their busy loops, and the reference run checks that each loop has no garbage collection. The reference run of `tests/integration/list_reductions.rs` gives the measured processes a heap of 2000000 words, so that ERTS copies on the heap. Owner decision in #7. The backlog entry "Model the process heap for GC reductions and the message copy cost" of `docs/backlog.md` owns this work. |
| Message copy | A send, an ETS insert and lookup, and `persistent_term` share a term: they increment a reference count and copy no part of the term. A receiver cannot see a difference, because terms are immutable, and PBeam reports no value that depends on a physical copy. PBeam has no `process_info/2` items `memory`, `heap_size`, and `binary`, no `erts_debug:size/1`, no `erlang:memory/1`, and no send copy reductions (rows "Heap limit", "Garbage collection reductions", and "Unimplemented BIFs"). `erts_debug:flat_size/1` computes the ERTS word count of `size_object` with a walk of the term (`src/term/flat_size.rs`). PBeam does not keep the ERTS form of a binary, so the walk counts a binary of at most 64 bytes as a heap binary and a larger one as a refc binary; ERTS gives 8 words also for a small writable binary that an append made. `erts_debug:size/1` needs `erts_debug:same/2` and the hashmap node BIFs of `erts_internal`, which PBeam does not have. A later implementation of `memory`, `heap_size`, `erts_debug:size/1`, or the send reductions reuses that walk. | A local send copies the message to the heap of the receiver and expands its shared subterms (`erl_message.c:834-861`). The default build has no sharing-preserving copy (`SHCOPY_SEND`, `erts/configure.ac:874-882`; `global.h:1054-1057`). A send to the process itself with the message queue on the heap does not copy (`erl_message.c:824-826`). An ETS insert copies the object into the table and expands its shared subterms (`db_store_term`, `erl_db_util.c:3559`, `:3579`, `:3613`), and a lookup copies the stored object out (`db_copy_from_comp`, `:3729`). `persistent_term:put/2` copies the term to a literal area with the sharing-preserving copy (`erl_bif_persistent.c:744-761`), and `persistent_term:get/1` copies nothing (`:380-388`). A copy of a refc binary increments its reference count (`copy.c:812-842`), and a copy of a binary of at most 64 bytes copies its bytes (`ERL_ONHEAP_BINARY_LIMIT`, `erl_bits.h:166-167`). | Owner decision R3 of [#450](https://github.com/fahchen/pbeam/issues/450) accepts physical sharing ([`copy-on-write-terms.md`](copy-on-write-terms.md)). Terms are immutable, and PBeam reports no value that depends on a copy. The row "Map merge reductions" records the effect on the steps of `maps:merge/2`. The backlog entry "Model the process heap for GC reductions and the message copy cost" of [`backlog.md`](backlog.md) owns the send copy reductions, the literal origin of a term, and the logical copy size. |
| Process and reference counters | Process identifiers and references come from `u64` counters. Counter overflow returns a runtime error. The root process is `<0.0.0>`. With a policy module, the policy process is `<0.1.0>` (row "Policy module"). A pid prints its counter value as the ERTS pid data: `<0.{value & 0x0FFF_FFFF}.{value >> 28}>`. A reference prints `#Ref<0.N2.N1.N0>` with the numbers that ERTS makes from a counter value of scheduler 1 (`erts_set_ref_numbers`, erl_bif_unique.h:117-131), so the first reference is `#Ref<0.0.1.0>`. A port number comes from a `u64` counter of the run, the first port is `#Port<0.0>`, and a port prints its counter value as the ERTS port data. An alias is a reference with three numbers. The high bit of its number `N2` marks it as a pid reference, also after its owner exits, so `erts_internal:is_process_alive/2` raises `badarg` for it. | The number of live processes is limited by `+P` (default 1048576). Process table slots are used again. The `-eval` process is `<0.10.0>`, and reference numbers change at each run (measured: `#Ref<0.1267164529.2489319425.102044>`). An alias is a pid reference with five numbers (measured: `#Ref<0.0.1283.4093163285.2220687365.65758>`). `ERTS_REF1_PID_MARKER_BIT__` of its number `N1` marks it (`erl_bif_unique.h:106-109`). The monitor tree of a process orders the references by the bytes of their numbers in memory, the low byte of `N0` first (`erl_monitor_link.c:167-181`). Thus the order of the demonitor signals at the exit of a process can change from run to run when the low byte goes past 255 between the references of its monitors. | A `u64` counter does not overflow in practice. The PBeam run starts no ERTS system processes before the root process. No current fixture compares an alias text. The monitor tree of PBeam uses the ERTS byte order on the PBeam numbers, with the pid mark in `N1`, so it gives the ERTS order of the demonitor signals at exit when the two counters give the same byte order. The two monitor cases of `exit_order_probe` give the same order for each counter value (`tests/integration/signal_queue.rs`). (#6) |
| Unique integers | `erlang:unique_integer/0` gives `MIN_SMALL + (1 bor (Counter bsl 1))` from one counter of the run that starts at 0: `-576460752303423487` for the first call, and 2 more for each later call, also in another process. A value above `MAX_SMALL`, after 2^59 calls, stops the run with the runtime error `erlang:unique_integer/0 needs a bignum`. `erlang:unique_integer([])` runs as `erlang:unique_integer/0`, also for the error above `MAX_SMALL`. `erlang:unique_integer/1` with `positive` and without `monotonic` takes the same counter and gives `2 + 2 * Counter`: 2 when it makes the first call of the run. With `monotonic`, it adds `MIN_SMALL`, or 1 with `positive`, to a second counter of the run that starts at 0, so the first call gives `-576460752303423488`. An element that is not `monotonic` or `positive`, and a term that is not a proper list, raise `badarg`. With `positive` or `monotonic`, a value above `MAX_SMALL` stops the run with the runtime error `erlang:unique_integer/1 needs a bignum` (`src/runtime/scheduler.rs`). | `unique_integer_bif` makes the value from the thread id of the scheduler and its counter: the counter goes after the bits of the largest thread id, which counts the schedulers and the dirty CPU and dirty IO schedulers, and `MIN_SMALL` is added (`erl_bif_unique.c:459-470`, `475-578`). A value above `MAX_SMALL` is a bignum. The boot uses the counter before the first call of a program. Measured with `erl +S 1` on a host with 4 bits of thread id: `-576460752303422911` first, and 16 more for each later call. `unique_integer_1` raises `badarg` for another element or a list that is not proper (`erl_bif_unique.c:840-871`). `unique_monotonic_integer_bif` adds `MIN_SMALL`, or 1 with `positive`, to one counter of the node (`erl_bif_unique.c:693-734`). Measured with `erl -eval`: the first `[monotonic]` call gives `-576460752303423488`. | PBeam has one scheduler with thread id 1 and no dirty scheduler that runs Erlang code, so its thread id has 1 bit. The value depends on the host and on the boot on ERTS, so no program can rely on it; it is unique, as in ERTS. `code_server` uses it only as a cache key (`code_server.erl:545`). Evidence: `tests/integration/unique_integer.rs`, `returns_the_erlang_unique_integer_sequences` (`tests/runtime/interpreter/bif/erlang.rs`), and the `unique_integer/1` values and `badarg` cases of `tests/integration/diagnostics.rs`. (#445, #786) |
| Term order | The `is_lt` and `is_ge` instructions and the BIFs `erlang:'>='/2` and `erlang:min/2` compare two terms in the ERTS term order, and `put_map_exact` sorts its keys in the ERTS flatmap key order. PBeam compares two references by their number words, from the highest word down (`Reference::numbers`), and its numbers carry the thread number 1 of the first ERTS scheduler. Thus two references that ERTS makes on different scheduler threads can order differently. A comparison that involves an alias reference raises the runtime error `unsupported term order of an alias reference`, because ERTS holds the pid of the owner in the higher reference words and a PBeam reference does not keep it. | ERTS compares the three number words of two references from the highest word down (`erts_cmp_compound`, `utils.c:2283-2286`). The words hold the counter of the scheduler thread that made the reference and the thread number (`erts_set_ref_numbers` in `erl_bif_unique.h`), so a reference that a process on scheduler 1 makes later can be less than a reference that a process on scheduler 2 made earlier. | PBeam has one scheduler, so each of its references carries the thread number 1. The fixture `event_bif_probe:refs/0` runs `erlang:min/2` on two references that one process makes, and both runtimes give a reference. A reference is also a map key, alone and inside a tuple or a list (`fixtures/map/reference_key_probe.erl`). (#77, #110, #184, #797) |
| Integer range | PBeam keeps an integer in the 64-bit range as an inline `i64` and each other integer as a shared bignum term (`Term::BigInt`, a `num-bigint` value); a result in the 64-bit range is inline again, so `=:=` and the internal hash compare values. The loader reads `SMALL_BIG_EXT` and `LARGE_BIG_EXT` literals and integer operands of 9 or more bytes. `+`, `-`, `*`, `div`, `rem`, `-/1`, `abs/1`, `band`, `bor`, `bxor`, `bnot`, `bsl`, `bsr`, the comparisons, the term order, `float/1`, `fconv`, `is_integer/1`, `is_integer/3`, `integer_to_list/1`, `integer_to_binary/1,2`, `lists:keyfind/3`, and a binary segment of at most 64 bits give the ERTS values, exceptions, and reductions (`tests/integration/bignum.rs`). `integer_to_list/2` of a bignum and `trunc/1` with a bignum result give the ERTS values (`tests/integration/erlang_bifs.rs`), and `term_to_binary/1,2` writes a bignum as `SMALL_BIG_EXT` or `LARGE_BIG_EXT` (`tests/integration/external_term.rs`, `tests/runtime/interpreter/bif/term.rs`). `round/1`, `floor/1`, and `ceil/1` with a bignum result, `binary_to_integer/1,2`, and `list_to_integer/1` of a text above the small integer range give the ERTS values: the text BIFs run the `big` path of the pinned `erlang` module (`erlang.erl:1056-1108`, `4111`). Measured on the `pbeam` binary and on `erl` with `round(-1.0e20)`, `floor(1.5e19)`, `ceil(-1.5e19)`, `binary_to_integer(<<"123456789012345678901234567890">>)`, `binary_to_integer(<<"ffffffffffffffffffff">>, 16)`, and `list_to_integer("-123456789012345678901234567890")`; no committed test covers them yet. Not implemented yet: the `integer` command of `bs_match` with a value outside the 64-bit range returns the runtime error `unsupported bs_match integer outside the 64-bit range: needs a bignum`, and `bs_get_integer2` returns `unsupported bs_get_integer2 integer outside the 64-bit range: needs a bignum`; a `bs_create_bin` integer segment of more than 64 bits of a bignum returns the runtime error `unsupported bs_create_bin integer segment of N bits of a bignum`; a bignum key in a hashmap (row "Map keys"); and `erlang:unique_integer/0,1` above `MAX_SMALL` (row "Unique integers"). | ERTS keeps an integer outside -(2^59) to 2^59 - 1 as a bignum of 64-bit digits, at most `BIG_ARITY_MAX` = 2^16 - 1 digits (`erl_term.h:465-466`); a larger result raises `system_limit`. Arithmetic is in `erl_arith.c:56-1359`, the order of a bignum and a float is exact (`utils.c:2392-2430`), `big_to_double` rounds to the nearest float and fails above the float range (`big.c:2411-2529`), and the hash mixes the digits (`erl_term_hashing.c:2101-2127`). | The `rand` seed path (`splitmix64_next/1`, `rand.erl:3400-3404`) and the kernel start through `global:init/1` need bignums (#500). |
| Integer division reductions | `-(2^59) rem -1` costs 1 reduction, also in a direct call of `erlang:'rem'/2` or an `apply` of it, because the BIF table runs one function for the instruction and for the call. | The `rem` instruction of the arm64 and x86_64 JIT calls `erts_int_div_rem` for `-(2^59)` and `-1`, which charges 1 (`jit/arm/instr_arith.cpp:759`, `:822`; `jit/x86/instr_arith.cpp:531`, `:612`; `erl_arith.c:1234`). A direct call runs `rem_2`, which divides the two small integers and charges 0 (`erl_arith.c:152-156`). | PBeam does not tell the call path of a BIF apart. The instruction path is the frequent path. |
| Float terms | A float term is an IEEE 754 double, and it is always finite, as in ERTS. The loader reads a float literal (`NEW_FLOAT_EXT`). `fmove`, `fconv`, `fadd`, `fsub`, `fmul`, `fdiv`, and `fnegate` (opcodes 96-102) run with float registers, so `float_test_probe:half(8)` returns `4.0`, and an overflow raises `error:badarith`. The arithmetic BIFs `+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, and `-/1`, the BIFs `float/1`, `abs/1`, `is_float/1`, `float_to_list/1,2`, and `float_to_binary/1,2`, the `is_eq` and `is_ne` tests, the BIFs `==` and `/=`, the term order, map keys, ETS keys, and `lists:keyfind/3` give the ERTS results (`fixtures/float`, #501). `list_to_float/1` gives the float that its byte list reads as for the text shapes of the scan of `sys_chars_to_double` (`sys_float.c:85-105`), a point or a comma as the radix character, `0.0` for a text that underflows to zero, and the `badarg` of `list_to_float_1` (`bif.c:3699-3725`) for a text that overflows an infinity and for a term that is not a proper list of bytes (`list_to_float_probe` of `fixtures/list_to_float`, #743). `io_lib:format/2` writes `~w`, `~f`, `~e`, and `~g` of a float that is not zero as ERTS does. `~f`, `~e`, and `~g` of `0.0` and `-0.0` read the sign with a 64-bit `float` binary segment (`io_lib_format:signbit/1`), which PBeam runs (row "Binary construction"); no test measures the format of zero yet. `~p` of the float `1.5` gives the ERTS text (`tests/feature/pretty.rs`). The BIFs `trunc/1`, `round/1`, `ceil/1`, and `floor/1` of a float (`tests/integration/erlang_bifs.rs`), `binary_to_float/1` (`tests/integration/binary_to_float.rs`), `math:exp/1`, `math:log/1`, `math:log10/1`, `math:pow/2`, and `math:sqrt/1` (`tests/integration/maps_math.rs`), and the other `math` BIFs except the five below (`tests/integration/math_bifs.rs`) give the ERTS results. `string:list_to_float/1`, `math:acosh/1`, `math:asinh/1`, `math:atanh/1`, `math:erf/1`, and `math:erfc/1` are `unsupported` entries of the BIF table and return the runtime error `unresolved external MFA` (row "Unimplemented BIFs"). A float operation with a bignum operand is in row "Integer range". | ERTS stores a float as a boxed double with the header `HEADER_FLONUM` (`erl_term.h:484-485`) and never makes an infinity or a NaN (`ERTS_FP_ERROR`, `erl_arith.c:487`; `emit_check_float_error` in `jit/arm/instr_float.cpp:33-53`; `dec_term` in `external.c:4666-4668`). `0.0 =:= -0.0` is `false` and `0.0 == -0.0` is `true` (`eq` in `utils.c:1422-1433`; `erts_cmp_compound` in `utils.c:2131-2137`). `float_to_list/2` with `short` writes the Ryu text (`bif.c:3427-3436`, `ryu/d2s.c`, `ryu/to_chars.h`). | The kernel starts `rand` through `global:init/1` (`global.erl:865-868`), and `rand` uses floats (#449). The measured Hello World and logger start paths run `fconv` only through `string` and `erl_scan`, which they load. (#167, #183, #393, #501) |
| Binary matching | The `bs_start_match3` test (opcode 166) jumps to its fail label for each term that is not a binary or a match context. For a binary, it makes a match context, which is a term in the destination register. For a match context, it writes the same match context to the destination register. Thus `binary_match_probe:kinds/0` gives 11 `other` atoms, `string:uppercase/1` of a list gives the uppercase list, and `binary_match_probe:first(<<195,169>>)` gives `{utf8,233}`. `bs_get_position`, `bs_set_position`, `bs_get_utf8`, `bs_skip_utf8`, `bs_get_utf16`, `bs_skip_utf16`, `bs_get_utf32`, `bs_skip_utf32`, `bs_match_string`, `bs_skip_bits2`, and `bs_get_integer2`, and `bs_match` with the commands `ensure_at_least`, `ensure_exactly`, `integer`, `binary`, `=:=`, `skip`, and `get_tail` run as in ERTS, for each size, unit, and flag. Thus `binary_match_probe:byte(<<7,8>>)` gives `7`, and the functions of `bs_match_probe` give the ERTS results. `bs_start_match4` with a fail label or `no_fail` runs as `bs_start_match3`, as the ERTS loader loads it. `bs_start_match4` with `resume` writes the match context to its destination register. `bs_get_tail`, and `bs_get_binary2` with the size in a register or a literal integer, run as in ERTS for each unit. `bs_match_string` compares the bits of a segment with a string of any size, at each position, and moves the position past them; it jumps to its fail label when fewer bits remain and when the bits differ, and keeps the position. Thus `bs_match_probe:match_string(<<"icrosoft.icon">>)` gives `<<>>` and `match_string(<<"icrosoft.ico">>)` gives `false`. `bs_skip_bits2` moves the position by the size times the unit, and `bs_get_integer2` reads an integer of that size with the flags `big`, `little`, `native`, `unsigned`, and `signed`; each jumps to its fail label when the size is not a small integer that is not negative, when the size times the unit overflows, and when fewer bits remain. An integer outside the 64-bit range is a bignum that `bs_get_integer2` does not build yet: the run stops with the runtime error `unsupported bs_get_integer2 integer outside the 64-bit range: needs a bignum` (row "Integer range"). `bs_skip_utf8` reads one character and drops it, as `bs_get_utf8` without a destination does. A `bs_get_binary2` size that is not a non-negative integer, or that is more bits than remain, jumps to the fail label. Thus `bs_get_probe:split/2`, `split_units/2`, and `nibbles/1` give the ERTS results. `bs_get_float2` runs as in ERTS for the sizes 0, 16, 32, and 64, for each unit, and for the big, `little`, and `native` flags. A `native` segment has the byte order of the host, as in ERTS. Another size, a size that is not a non-negative integer, fewer bits than the size, an infinity, and a NaN jump to the fail label. Thus the float functions of `bs_float_probe` give the ERTS results. A local call keeps a match context in its argument register. Thus `binary_match_probe:usascii/1` and `prim_tty:is_usascii/1` run as in ERTS. A match context term refers to one match context, as in ERTS. `move`, `bs_start_match3`, and `bs_start_match4` with a match context source and another destination register copy the term, so the two registers share the match context and its position. `byte_size/1` of a match context gives the bits from its position to its end in bytes, rounded up. Thus `match_copy_probe:parse/1`, whose `decode_codepoint/1` has the instructions of `Terra.Input.Parser.decode_codepoint/1`, gives the ERTS results. After a failed `=:=` command, the position is at the end of the JIT read that holds the command, as in ERTS. A fused `test_heap` after `bs_match` changes the reads as in ERTS. `swap` copies a match context, and `bit_size/1` of a match context gives the bits from its position to its end. The `binary` and `get_tail` commands, `bs_get_binary2`, and `bs_get_tail` give a bitstring of each size at each position, as in ERTS. Thus `bs_match_probe:bits(<<165>>)` gives `<<5:3>>`, `bs_get_probe:split_bits(<<1,2>>, 3)` gives `{<<0:3>>,<<8,2:5>>}`, and `bs_match_probe:bitstrings/0` matches `bits` segments and unit tails of bitstring literals as ERTS does. An `integer` command with a value outside the 64-bit signed range stops the run (row "Integer range"). `bs_get_binary2` with the size `all` runs the commands `{ensure_at_least,0,Unit}` and `get_tail`, as the ERTS loader loads it; thus `bs_all_probe:run/0` gives the ERTS result. Each other instruction that gets a match context stops the run with the runtime error `invariant error: the match context in <Operand> reached an instruction that is not a binary match`. | The `bs_start_match3` test jumps to its fail label for each term that is not a bitstring. For a bitstring, it reuses the match context or makes one with `erts_bs_start_match_3` and writes it to the destination register (emu `bs_instrs.tab:1074-1118`, `1199-1238`; JIT `emit_i_bs_start_match3` in `jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:134-189`; `erl_bits.c:81-105`). A literal source that is not a bitstring loads as `jump Fail` (emu `ops.tab:1266-1267`; JIT `jit/arm/ops.tab:1054-1055`). A match context is one `ErlSubBits` term on the process heap, so each register that holds it sees the same position. The JIT runs the `bs_match` commands in order (`gen.bs_match` in `jit/arm/generators.tab:532-604`; `emit_i_bs_match_test_heap` in `jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:2916-3205`).
