It reads the `integer`, `binary`, `=:=`, and `skip` segments up to 64 bits at a time, and writes the position after the read before it compares a `=:=` value (`beam_jit_opt_bsm_segments` in `jit/beam_jit_bs.cpp:366-521`; `jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:2995-3045`). Thus after a failed `=:=` the position is at the end of that read. `native` is little-endian (`bs_get_flags` in `jit/beam_jit_bs.cpp:255-285`). An integer that is not a small integer is a bignum (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:2829-2875`; `erl_bits.c:170-398`). The `binary` and `get_tail` commands make a bitstring of each size, so `bs_match_probe:bits(<<165>>)` gives `<<5:3>>`. The BEAM validator lets `move` and `swap` copy a match context (`beam_validator.erl:403-407`, `2690-2693`, `2783-2785`), and lets `bit_size/1` and `byte_size/1` get one (`validate_bif_sources` in `beam_validator.erl:1649-1654`). `move` copies the term (`emit_i_move` in `jit/arm/instr_common.cpp:516-519`), and `bs_start_match3` writes a match context source to the destination (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:158-159`, `187-188`). `byte_size/1` gives `end - start` of the `ErlSubBits` in bytes, rounded up (`emit_bif_byte_size` in `jit/arm/instr_guard_bifs.cpp:458-509`). The loader makes `bs_start_match3` from `bs_start_match4` with a fail label or `no_fail`, and with `resume` it makes nothing or `move` on a 64-bit system (JIT `jit/arm/ops.tab:1036-1045`). With the fail label 0, the JIT does not test the source (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:142-148`). `bs_get_binary2` fails for a size that is not a non-negative small integer, and for a size above the bits that remain. Else it gives a sub-bitstring and moves the position (`emit_bs_get_field_size` and `emit_bs_get_binary` in `jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:38-116`, `506-576`; `erts_bs_get_binary_2` in `erl_bits.c:400-424`). `bs_get_tail` gives the bits after the position and keeps the position (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:407-454`). With the size `all`, `bs_get_binary2` loads as `i_bs_get_binary_all2` (`jit/arm/ops.tab:999-1000`), which runs `{ensure_at_least,0,Unit}` and `get_tail` (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:386-405`). The loader fuses `bs_match` and a `test_heap` after it (`jit/arm/ops.tab:962-963`); a heap need starts a new read at the first `integer`, `binary`, or `get_tail` command (`jit/beam_jit_bs.cpp:378-411`). | The measured latin1 output path of the boot runs only `prim_tty:is_usascii/1` (`prim_tty.erl:1316-1321`), which needs no copy of a match context. The measured log event path runs `bs_start_match3` only on lists: `string:uppercase/1` of a list in `logger_formatter` (`logger_formatter.erl:616`) runs it in `unicode_util:cp/1`. (#183, #219) The static gap scan of Terra Counter (#339) found the `integer`, `=:=`, `get_tail`, `binary`, and `skip` commands on 210 reachable sites, so #395 runs them. The scan also found `bs_start_match4`, `bs_get_tail`, and `bs_get_binary2` on the path, for example in `Terra.Input.Parser.do_parse/3` and `extend/3`, `unicode:ml_map/3`, and `Macro.inspect_atom/3`, so #396 runs them. The pinned compiler makes `bs_get_binary2` with the size `all` only with `no_ssa_opt`, and no measured path has it. The compiler restores the position before it reads a match context after a failed match (`bs_restores` in `beam_ssa_pre_codegen.erl:308-437`), so compiled code does not read the position after a failed `=:=`. The Terra Counter run of #339 copies a match context to a Y register with `move` in `Terra.Input.Parser.do_parse/3` and `decode_codepoint/1`, and gives a match context to `byte_size/1` in `decode_codepoint/1` and `first_grapheme/3`, so #421 runs them. No measured path has `swap` or `bit_size/1` of a match context. (#258, #395, #396, #421, #1008, #1015) |
| Binary construction | `bs_create_bin` (opcode 177) runs `string` segments, `binary` segments of size `all` with each unit, and `binary` segments of a size, as in ERTS. Elixir compiles string interpolation and `<>` to these segments. Thus `bs_create_bin_probe:prefix(<<"3">>)` gives `<<"count: 3">>`. A `binary` segment uses one reduction for each 1024 bytes that it copies. A `binary` segment with a term that is not a binary jumps to the fail label. With fail label 0, it raises `error:badarg` with the stack trace `[{Module, Function, Arity, [{error_info, #{cause => {Segment, binary, type, Value}, function => format_bs_fail, module => erl_erts_errors}}]}]` (see "Exception stack trace"). PBeam runs an `append` segment of size `all` as the first segment, as ERTS does. Elixir compiles `A <> B` with a variable `A` to this segment. Thus `bs_create_bin_append_probe:two(<<"ab">>, <<"cd">>)` gives `<<"abcd">>`. The `append` segment is checked after the `binary` segments. A term that is not a binary fails with the cause `{Segment, binary, type, Value}`, and a binary whose bit size is not a multiple of a segment unit larger than 1 fails with the cause `{Segment, binary, unit, Value}`, with the same fail label and `badarg` rules. When the other segments add no bits, the result is the appended binary and the append uses no reduction. Else the copy of the appended binary uses one reduction for each 1024 bytes. PBeam has no writable binary, so it also copies a binary that an `append` built and charges these reductions: `bs_create_bin_append_probe:cost_again(binary:copy(<<1>>, 100000), <<2>>)` gives 98 reductions in PBeam and 1 in ERTS. `bs_init_writable` (opcode 133) writes a new binary with no byte to `x0`, as ERTS does; PBeam does not keep the size hint in `x0` as a capacity. PBeam runs a `private_append` segment of size `all` as the first segment, as the compiler emits it for `<<Acc/binary, X:8>>` in a loop. Thus `bs_create_bin_writable_probe:bytes(5)` gives `<<5,4,3,2,1>>`, and `erl_parse:parse_term/1` of the tokens of `{short, <<1, 2:16, "xy", 1.5/float>>}.` gives `{ok,{short,<<1,0,2,120,121,63,248,0,0,0,0,0,0>>}}` through the pinned `eval_bits`. The segment has no check. The result is also the term of the source register, because ERTS changes the size of that term in place. PBeam takes the binary out of the source register, and writes the new bytes in its array when no other term holds the binary. A loop of appends then copies each byte a constant number of times on average, as ERTS does. When another term holds the binary, PBeam builds a new binary that shares the old arrays, as for an `append` segment; the values are the same. PBeam charges no reduction for a `private_append`, as the pinned ERTS on arm64 does: `bs_create_bin_writable_probe:reds(N)`, a loop that appends 1024 bytes N times, gives N + 3 reductions on both. On x86_64, the pinned ERTS also charges one reduction for each 8192 bits of the old binary when it grows the buffer, so PBeam charges fewer reductions there (not measured). A `private_append` of a bitstring writes its bits, so `bs_create_bin_writable_probe:bits(2)` gives `<<"!">>`, as ERTS does. When the old bitstring does not end on a byte boundary, PBeam copies it at each append, so such a loop copies O(N^2) bytes where ERTS appends in place; the values are the same. PBeam runs `float` segments of 32 and 64 bits as ERTS does. The size checks are the size checks of an `integer` segment, with the cause `{Segment, float, size, Size}`. A small integer converts with one cast to the float of the size, a bignum converts to a double first, and a value too large for 32 bits gives an infinity with no error. A size other than 16, 32, or 64 fails with the cause `{Segment, float, invalid, Bits}`, a term that is not a number with `{Segment, float, type, Value}`, and a bignum too large for a double with `{Segment, float, no_float, Value}`. A `float` segment of 16 bits stops with the runtime error `unsupported bs_create_bin float segment of 16 bits`, so `bs_create_bin_writable_probe:float_size(1, 16)` stops, where ERTS gives `<<60,0>>`. PBeam runs `integer` segments with each size, unit, and byte order (`big`, `little`, `native`) that the compiler writes, and `utf8` segments, as ERTS does. Thus `bs_create_bin_integer_probe:little12(16#ABC, 16#D)` gives `<<16#BC, 16#AD>>` and `bs_create_bin_integer_probe:code_tail(233)` gives `<<195,169,33>>`. An `integer` segment writes the value modulo 2^Size and uses no reduction. A size that is not a small integer that is not negative fails with the cause `{Segment, integer, size, Size}` and the reason `badarg`, or `system_limit` for a positive bignum. A size of 2^52 or more in a register with a unit other than 1 fails with `system_limit`, as the arm64 JIT of the macOS arm64 target does. The x86_64 JIT has no such check: it fails with `system_limit` only when the size times the unit overflows 64 bits, else it stops the runtime at the binary allocation (`binary_alloc: Cannot allocate`). PBeam uses the arm64 check on each host. A term that is not an integer fails with the cause `{Segment, integer, type, Value}`. A `utf8` segment with a term that is not a code point from 0 to 16#10FFFF outside 16#D800 to 16#DFFF fails with the cause `{Segment, utf8, type, Value}`. The checks have the ERTS order: first the size of each `integer` segment, each `utf8` code point, and each `binary` term in segment order, then the `append` segment, and then the term of each `integer` segment. A group of adjacent little-endian `integer` segments of a fixed size checks its terms in reverse order, so `bs_create_bin_integer_probe:little_pair(other, one)` fails with the cause `{2, integer, type, one}`. A result whose size is not a multiple of 8 bits is a bitstring, so `bs_create_bin_integer_probe:nibble(1)` gives `<<1:4>>`, as ERTS does. A `binary` segment of a size copies the first Size times Unit bits of its bitstring, and uses one reduction for each 8192 bits. The size checks are the size checks of an `integer` segment, with the cause `{Segment, binary, size, Size}`. At the construct pass, a term that is not a bitstring fails with the cause `{Segment, binary, type, Value}`, and a bitstring shorter than the size with `{Segment, binary, short, Value}` (`bs_create_bin_bits_probe:cases/0`). PBeam runs `utf16` segments with each byte order as ERTS does, so `bs_create_bin_utf_probe:utf16(16#41)` gives `<<0,65>>` and `utf16(16#1F600)` gives `<<216,61,222,0>>`, and `utf32` segments as an `integer` segment of 32 bits does, so `bs_create_bin_utf_probe:utf32(16#1F600)` gives `<<0,1,246,0>>`. A term that is not a code point from 0 to 16#10FFFF outside 16#D800 to 16#DFFF fails with the cause `{Segment, utf16, type, Value}`, or `{Segment, utf32, type, Value}`. ERTS checks a `utf32` code point at the size pass and a `utf16` term at the construct pass, so `bs_create_bin_utf_probe:ordered(other, -1)` fails with the cause `{2, integer, size, -1}` and `ordered(other, 8)` with `{1, utf16, type, other}`. The other segments load: an `append` or `private_append` segment that is not the first segment or has a size other than `all`. When one of them runs, the run stops with the runtime error `unsupported bs_create_bin segment <Type>`. The `Alloc` operand loads as a heap word count or as an allocation list, such as `{alloc,[{words,1},{floats,0},{funs,1}]}` of `bs_create_bin_alloc_probe:wrap/1`. The loader rejects each allocation list that the ERTS loader rejects. PBeam has no process heap, so it does not keep the word count. | The JIT runs each segment type (JIT `emit_i_bs_create_bin` in `jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:1442-2300`; emu `i_bs_create_bin` in `bs_instrs.tab:359-411`). It checks the term of each `binary` segment of size `all` in segment order before it builds the binary, and gives the failure to `Fail` or to `emit_bs_create_bin_error_shared` (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:1002-1019`, `1588-1638`). `beam_jit_bs_construct_fail_info` makes the `error_info` (`jit/beam_jit_common.cpp:911-1010`). `erts_bs_put_binary_all` uses one reduction for each 8192 bits (`erl_bits.c:1311-1332`, `erl_bits.h:41`). A `binary` segment of a size has the size check of an `integer` segment (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:1639-1689`), and `erts_bs_put_binary` fails for a term that is not a bitstring or is shorter than the size, and uses one reduction for each 8192 copied bits (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:2048-2071`; `erl_bits.c:1285-1308`). The JIT runs an `append` segment only as the first segment, after the size pass, through `erts_bs_append_checked` (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:1584-1586`, `1811-1864`; `erl_bits.c:1724-1925`). That function checks the type and then the unit, and returns the binary when the other segments add no bits. It appends in place to a writable binary, which an earlier `append` built, and uses reductions only when it reallocates the binary (`erl_bits.c:1773-1849`). For another binary, it copies the binary and uses one reduction for each 8192 bits (`erl_bits.c:1855-1924`). `erts_bs_init_writable` reads the size hint from `x0`, 1024 bytes when it is not a small integer that is not negative, and keeps no other register live (`erl_bits.c:2020-2049`; `jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:985-1000`). The JIT runs a `private_append` segment as the first segment through `erts_bs_private_append_checked`, which cannot fail on a 64-bit host (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:1865-1881`). That function sets the size of the source term in place and returns that term; it grows a writable buffer to twice the used size and calls `BUMP_REDS` with one reduction for each 8192 bits of the old size, and it copies a binary that is not writable (`erl_bits.c:72-73`, `1928-2018`, `1974`, `2010`). The arm JIT enters the runtime for this call without `Update::eReductions` (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:1876-1880`), so the bump is lost and no reduction is visible. The x86 JIT enters the runtime with `Update::eReductions` (`jit/x86/instr_bs.cpp:1578-1584`, `2116-2131`), so the bump stays. The JIT checks a `float` segment size as an `integer` segment size (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:1639-1689`) and writes the term with `erts_bs_put_float`, which fails with the `fvalue` info (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:2085-2115`; `erl_bits.c:1340-1452`). The JIT checks the size of each `integer` segment that is not a literal from 0 to 2^56 - 1 and each `utf8` code point in the size pass (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:1493-1505`, `1639-1750`), and the term of each `integer` segment in the construct pass (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:2116-2386`). The construct pass joins adjacent `integer` segments of a fixed size into groups of up to 64 bits, and visits a little-endian group in reverse order (`beam_jit_bsc_combine_segments` and `push_group`, `jit/beam_jit_bs.cpp:106-232`). `native` loads as `little` (`jit/beam_jit_bs.cpp:255-285`). `erts_bs_put_integer_be` and `erts_bs_put_integer_le` use no reductions (`erl_bits.c:926-1167`). A size failure is `system_limit` for a positive size and `badarg` for each other size (`BSC_REASON_DEPENDS`, `jit/beam_jit_common.cpp:968-975`). The arm64 JIT fails a size of 2^52 or more with a unit other than 1 (`jit/arm/instr_bs.cpp:1685`). The x86_64 JIT fails only on an overflow of the size times the unit (`jit/x86/instr_bs.cpp:1942-1943`). `bs_create_bin_probe:utf16(7)` gives `<<98,0,7>>`, and `bs_create_bin_integer_probe:nibble(1)` gives `<<1:4>>`. The loader changes an allocation list into a `u` heap word count (`beam_file.c:2384-2395`). `marshal_allocation_list` rejects more than three items, a kind other than words, floats, and funs, a number larger than `i32::MAX / 3`, and a sum larger than `i32::MAX` (`beam_file.c:2234-2280`). | The examples of #262 need only the `string` and `binary` segments that Elixir makes for `<>` (#269). The Terra Counter example of #339 needs the `append` segment (#383), and the `integer` and `utf8` segments in `Terra.Input.Parser`, `String`, and `Access` (#397). A writable binary needs a new binary term with a shared, growable buffer; no measured path needs its reduction cost. `eval_bits:expr_grp/3`, which `erl_parse:normalise/1` calls for each binary of `sys.config`, needs `bs_init_writable`, `private_append`, and the `float` segment of 64 bits (#681). No measured path needs a 16-bit float or the x86_64 reductions of a `private_append`. The other segments are an independent instruction feature with no measured path. |
| Unimplemented instructions | The loader accepts the instructions `get_record_elements` (188) and `put_record` (189). When one of them runs, the run stops with the runtime error `unsupported BEAM opcode N`. Because `put_record` stops the run, PBeam has no native record term. The test `is_any_native_record` (opcode 186), the guard `is_record/1`, thus jumps to its fail label for each term, and `erlang:is_record/1` gives `false` for each term. `erlang:is_record/2,3` give `false` for each test of a native record. Under the PBeam boot, with the pinned OTP modules that the format calls, `io:format/2` writes a format without a term argument, `~w` of an atom, an integer, a float, a list, a tuple, a map, or a binary, and `~s` of a string as ERTS does. `~p` of an atom, an integer, a float, a list, a tuple, a map, or a binary writes as ERTS does: `~p` of `<<1,2>>`, `<<"text">>`, and `{a,[b,#{c => {d,<<"e">>}}],2.0}` gives the ERTS text on the `pbeam` binary under `pbeam_boot` (measured). | ERTS runs each of these instructions (genop.tab:734, 740). `is_any_native_record` passes for a boxed term with the record header (emu `instrs.tab:1323-1327`; JIT `jit/arm/instr_records.cpp:30-45`; `erl_term.h:139`, `167`). `erlang:is_record/1,2,3` also test a native record (`erl_bif_op.c:279-349`). `io:format/2` writes each term. | No measured path of #25 runs these instructions. The backlog entry "Run the remaining test instructions for `io:format/2`" records the work. The result of `is_any_native_record` depends on the absence of a native record term: when PBeam runs `put_record`, the test must pass for a native record. |
| Bitstring terms | The loader loads a `BIT_BINARY_EXT` (tag 77) literal, such as `<<1:3>>`, as a bitstring whose size is not a multiple of 8 bits. `is_bitstr` passes for it, `erlang:bit_size/1` and `erlang:byte_size/1` read it, `erlang:binary_part/3` raises `badarg` for it, and its print and its term order are those of ERTS. `erlang:split_binary/2` of it keeps the tail bits, and the binary-match instructions make such a bitstring (row "Binary matching"), and `bs_create_bin` makes one (row "Binary construction"), `erlang:bitstring_to_list/1` gives its tail bits as the last element of the list, `erlang:list_to_bitstring/1` takes it as each element and as the tail of an iolist, and `erlang:term_to_binary/1,2` and `erlang:binary_to_term/1,2` encode and decode it as `BIT_BINARY_EXT` (row "External term format"). | The loader decodes the literal (`external.c:4895-4923`) and rejects a trailing bit count outside 1 to 8 or an empty byte list (`external.c:4913-4917`). `is_bitstring` passes for each boxed term with a bitstring header (emu `ops.tab:796`, `instrs.tab:945-949`; `erl_term.h:384-391`). `erlang:byte_size/1` rounds up to the next byte (`erl_bif_guard.c:376-393`), so `byte_size_probe:bits/0` gives `[1,4]`. `erlang:binary_part/3` raises `badarg` (`erl_bif_binary.c:1995-1997`). A bitstring prints its whole bytes, then its tail bits as `Value:Size` (`erl_printf_term.c:615-653`). Two bitstrings compare by their bits, then by their sizes (`utils.c:2322-2335`). `bitstring_to_list/1` follows `binary.c:413-474`, and `list_to_bitstring/1` walks the iolist with the unit 1 (`erl_iolist.c:176-184`, `930-934`). | (#359, #360, #1005, #1008, #1010, #1012) |
| Chardata conversion | `unicode:characters_to_binary/2` supports only the `latin1`, `unicode`, and `utf8` encodings. With `latin1`, each byte of a binary and each integer is one character from 0 to 255. It converts a binary or a list of integers, binaries, and nested lists, and a list can have a binary as its tail. Any other encoding returns a runtime error, also when `unicode:characters_to_list_int/2` calls the BIF for `unicode:characters_to_list/2`. A term that is not chardata raises `badarg`, as the `utf8_need` walk of ERTS decides. A code point that is not a Unicode scalar value, an integer above 255 with `latin1`, and a run of adjacent binaries that is not valid UTF-8 each return a runtime error. A valid binary gives an equal copy of the binary. `unicode:characters_to_list/2` gives the ERTS result for `latin1`, `unicode`, and `utf8`, also the error and incomplete tuples, and traps each other encoding to `unicode:characters_to_list_int/2`. It charges and traps as ERTS does (row "Scheduling"). | The BIF converts `latin1`, `unicode`, and `utf8` in C, and `unicode:characters_to_binary_int/2` converts the other encodings. Invalid input gives `{error, Converted, Rest}`, an incomplete UTF-8 sequence at the end gives `{incomplete, Converted, Rest}`, and a term that is not chardata raises `badarg`. A valid binary, or a list with only one valid binary, gives the same binary. A list can have a binary as its tail. `unicode:characters_to_list/2` stops and continues in a trap when the reduction budget of the process ends (`allowed_iterations`, `erl_unicode.c:166-174`), and `unicode:characters_to_list_int/2` converts the other encodings with `unicode:characters_to_binary/2`. | On the current fixture paths, `io:io_request/2` calls the BIF with `unicode`, `group:putc_request/3` calls it with `utf8`, and `user_drv` and the `prim_tty` reader call it with `latin1` for a latin1 request and for the latin1 encoding of `standard_io` (`io_device_probe`, #391), each with valid chardata. The error tuples come with their first consumer. `standard_error` calls `unicode:characters_to_list/2` with `unicode` for each `put_chars` request (#158). For inputs of 100000 characters, ERTS gives the same `Rest` as for short inputs, so the trap does not change the result. (#74, #77, #152, #158, #391) |
| Binary to list trap state | When `erlang:binary_to_list/1,3` traps after a chunk, it traps to `erts_internal:binary_to_list_continue/4` with the binary, the byte offset of the part, the bytes that remain, and the list so far (`src/runtime/interpreter/bif/erlang.rs`). The last chunk returns the list at once. The results, the reductions, and the switch-out points are the ERTS results (`b2l_probe:costs/1`, `b2l_probe:lows/1`). | `binary_to_list_chunk` keeps the state in a magic binary and traps to `erts_internal:binary_to_list_continue/1` with a magic reference to it. The trap disables the garbage collection of the process, and the last chunk can return through `ERTS_BIF_PREP_YIELD_RETURN` when a collection is pending (`binary.c:201-304`). | PBeam has no magic reference term and no process heap, so no collection is pending. The continuation raises nothing, so its arity is not in a stack trace. (#696) |
| Binary search trap state | When the search of `binary:match/2`, `binary:matches/2`, or `binary:split/2,3` uses all search steps of a turn, the BIF traps to `erlang:binary_find_trap/3` with the subject, the search state, and the pattern term of the call (`src/runtime/interpreter/bif/binary.rs`). The search state is a tuple of 10 terms: the flags, the end of the search scope, the next position, the current trie node, the node and the start of the candidate match, the next match and the part end of the result build, the list of the matches so far, and the list of the parts so far (a `binary:matches/2` build keeps its phase in the part end field and its tuples so far in the parts field). At each trap, PBeam makes this tuple from the search state. The trap reads the tuple back into a search state and compiles the pattern again. Thus each trap copies the matches and the parts so far, and makes the Boyer-Moore data or the Aho-Corasick trie again. Each trap also makes a new copy of the subject term and of the pattern term for the trap arguments, and each copy shares the bytes of its binary, so no byte of the subject or the pattern is copied (`src/term/binary.rs`). This work uses no reduction. The observable effect is none, except more work for each trap: the results, the reductions, the switch-out points, and the `badarg` raises are the ERTS results. Thus `binary_find_probe:costs/1` gives the pinned ERTS results and reductions of `binary:match/2` for the subjects whose search traps, and `iolist_split_probe:main/0` gives the pinned ERTS results of `binary:split/2,3` for the inputs whose search or result build traps. | At the first trap, `bf_context_export` copies the search context into a magic binary, and makes a magic reference to it and a magic reference to the compiled pattern (`erl_bif_binary.c:1186-1206`). The BIF traps with these two references (`erl_bif_binary.c:1567-1571`, `1625-1629`). `binary_find_trap` gets the context and the compiled pattern from the references and continues the search with them (`erl_bif_binary.c:1440-1448`, `1935-1952`). Thus a trap after the first copies no state and compiles nothing. The trap arguments refer to the subject binary and copy none of its bytes. A global result build can export the context before its first trap (`erl_bif_binary.c:1693-1697`, `1865-1869`). | PBeam has no magic binary and no magic reference term. A search state as a term needs no new term type, and the extra work for each trap changes no result and no reduction count. The `binary:match/2`, `binary:matches/2`, and `binary:split/2,3` results share the bytes of the subject, so a part keeps the offset of its bytes in the array of the subject (`do_split_single_result`, `erl_bif_binary.c:1753-1833`, and `do_split_global_result`, `erl_bif_binary.c:1835-1933`). The Terra Counter path calls `binary:split/3` and `binary:match/2` (`docs/terra-reference.md`). The owner confirmed this difference on 2026-10-11 (#436). (#390, #394) |
| Binary copy reductions | `binary:copy/1,2` copies the whole result in one turn and charges `min(total cost, remaining budget)` once, so it omits the charge of the resumed pass of ERTS. The difference in the charged reductions is therefore bounded by the next turn's budget plus the first slice, and not by the current turn's budget. The values, the `badarg` raises, the `system_limit` raise, and each byte of the result are the ERTS ones. | `binary:copy/1,2` copies at most `fcalls * 512` bits (256 KB at 4000 reductions), yields to `erlang:binary_copy_trap/3` with `trap_offset = target_size`, and the resumed trap copies the whole remainder in one pass and charges a clamped `BUMP_REDS(remaining / 512)` in the next turn (`erl_bif_binary.c:2427-2487`, `2472-2479`). | PBeam has no writable binary: a term that a trap receives keeps its bytes, so PBeam cannot fill the result of a copy across turns. `ProcessState::bump_reductions` stops the budget of the turn at 0 (`src/runtime/process.rs:601-603`), which is the clamp of the ERTS trap. No task owns this difference. |
| Printable range | `io:printable_range/0` always returns `latin1`, because PBeam takes no emulator flags. | `io:printable_range/0` returns `latin1` by default (`erts/emulator/sys/common/erl_sys_common_misc.c:62`). The emulator flag `+pc unicode` makes it return `unicode` (`erl_init.c:1371-1383`; `erl_unicode.c:2943-2950`). Measured: `erl +pc unicode -noshell` gives `unicode`. | The log path needs only the default (#184). |
| Local call to a BIF stub | A local call from a module to its own function whose export is a BIF runs the BEAM body of that function. For example, a local call from `erts_internal:term_to_string/1` to `term_to_string/2` runs the stub body. Only `call_ext`, `apply`, `apply_last`, the `bif` instructions, a `call_fun` or `call_fun2` of an external fun, and the calls of `erlang:apply/2` and `erlang:apply/3` go to the BIF. | The loader replaces the body of each function whose export is a BIF (`ops.tab`, `emu_load.c`). A local call also runs the BIF: `erts_internal:term_to_string/1` returns `"{a,1}"` for `{a,1}`. | No function on the current fixture paths makes a local call to a BIF stub. (#74, #76) |
| Spawn of a BIF | A process that `spawn/3`, `spawn_link/3`, or `spawn_opt/4` starts with a BIF as its MFA runs the BEAM stub body of that function when its module is loaded. When the module is not loaded, the spawn returns the runtime error `unresolved external MFA`. For example, `spawn_monitor(maps, keys, [not_a_map])`, which calls `spawn_opt/4` in `erlang.beam`, runs the `maps` stub, or fails when `maps` is not loaded. A process that starts at `erlang:apply/2` or `erlang:apply/3`, as `erlang:spawn/1` makes, runs its body in `erlang.beam`, which runs the `i_apply_fun` or `i_apply` instruction, as in ERTS. | The new process calls the export of the MFA, and the export of a BIF goes to the BIF (`emu_load.c`). `spawn_monitor(maps, keys, [not_a_map])` gives the `DOWN` reason `{{badmap, not_a_map}, [{maps, keys, [not_a_map], ...}]}`. | No process on the current fixture paths starts with a BIF as its MFA. (#74) |
| Hibernation | `erlang:hibernate/3` checks its arguments as ERTS does, drops the stack and its catches, and waits as `erlang:hibernate/0` does. PBeam has no garbage collection and no heap sizes, so the call does not shrink a heap, and `process_info/2` does not give `current_function`, `status`, or `total_heap_size` for a hibernated process (row "BIF arguments"). After the wake-up, the process applies the MFA with an empty stack, as `apply/3` does, so a BIF as the MFA runs the BIF. The process wakes up in the first frame of `erlang:apply/3` of `erlang.beam` (`call_ext_only erlang:apply/3`), so the run must load `erlang.beam`. A process that `spawn/3` starts at `erlang:hibernate/3` runs the BEAM stub (row "Spawn of a BIF"). The call charges no reductions for a hibernate garbage collection. Thus the wake-up after a wait charges fewer reductions than ERTS. From a `process_info(self(), reductions)` before the call to one in the applied function, after a wait of 50 ms, pinned `erl +S 1` gives 4, 5, 25, and 223 when the arguments hold a list of 0, 10, 100, and 1000 integers, and PBeam gives 2 for each. With a message in the queue before the call, both give 2. | `hibernate_3` checks the arguments, drops the stack, sets `catches` to 0, and traps to `beam_run_process` (`bif.c:1366-1427`). `erts_hibernate` does a hibernate garbage collection when the queue has no message, and the process waits (`beam_common.c:1568-1602`). That collection charges `gc_cost(final_size, final_size)` reductions, which is `final_size / 10 + final_size / 100` and at least 1, where `final_size` is the live heap words, `S_RESERVED`, and the stack words (`erl_gc.c:1125`, `erl_gc.c:1129-1133`, `erl_gc.c:219-230`). While it waits, `current_function` is `{erlang, hibernate, 3}` and `status` is `waiting`. | PBeam has no garbage collector and no heap accounting (rows "Heap limit" and "Garbage collection reductions"). (#716) |
| Node of a term | `erlang:node/1` returns `nonode@nohost` for a pid, a port, or a reference, and raises `badarg` for another term (dist.c:6219-6225, #1001). | `node/1` returns the node of a pid, a port, or a reference, and raises `badarg` for other terms. Without distribution the node is `nonode@nohost`. | Fixture `node_probe` checks each case against the pinned ERTS. (#74, #1001) |
| BIF arguments | With a pid and a valid item, `erlang:process_info/2` returns `undefined` for a process that is not alive, the items `registered_name`, `message_queue_len`, `group_leader`, `priority`, `links`, and `reductions` of each live process, and the item `current_stacktrace` of the calling process. Another process that exits or has signals or messages that it did not handle gets a process-info request, as on ERTS (`erl_bif_info.c:1478-1500`). The item `message_queue_len` counts the messages of the inner queue part and no receive marker (`erl_bif_info.c:1742-1754`). In a run of `execute_on_schedulers`, a read of a process of another scheduler, also of a process that exits, always sends the process-info request with one of the items above except `current_stacktrace`, because the thread of the caller cannot lock the process. The owner answers after the signals that the caller sent before, and a process that exits first answers `undefined`. `erlang:process_info/1` and the item `current_stacktrace` of a process of another scheduler return a runtime error. For a live process, a list of items, the item `{dictionary, Key}`, and each other item return a runtime error. `erlang:processes/0` gives the pid of each process of the run, also an exiting process, in the index order of the ERTS table: the pid number modulo 1048576. It reads the 1048576 indices of the ERTS table in chunks of 1000, charges 1 reduction for each 25 indices, and traps between chunks, as `erts_ptab_list` does. The result has each process that is in the table at the start of the read. A process that starts during a trapped read is not in the result. A process of the start that exits before the read gets to its chunk comes after the other pids, in the order of the exits. Each process that exits during the read costs 1 reduction for each 10, and 1 more in the oldest read that steps through it, as in ERTS. A process that is not a boot process gets the list without the policy process (row "Policy module"). `erlang:cancel_timer/1` runs as `erlang:cancel_timer/2` with `[]`. | `process_info/2` also returns the other items, accepts a list of items for a live process, and returns `current_stacktrace` of another process (`process_info_bif` in `erl_bif_info.c`). It gives `group_leader` (erl_bif_info.c:2107-2112). With more schedulers, a read of a process without signals that no other thread locks reads the process directly, and a busy or exiting process gets the request (`erts_try_lock_sig_free_proc`, `erl_process.c:14835-14883`; `erl_bif_info.c:1380-1413`). `erts_ptab_list` lists the process table in index order and charges reductions for the table indices that it inspects (erl_ptab.c:746-780, 1022-1023). `cancel_timer_1` calls `access_bif_timer` with `async` false and `info` true (erl_hl_timer.c:2644-2647). | On the `gen_statem` enter-loop path, `process_info/2` gets `self()` and `current_stacktrace`. On the group path, `group:server_name/0` gets `self()` and `registered_name`. The `prim_tty` writer gets the pid of its parent and `registered_name` (`prim_tty.erl:569-571`). The `logger_olp` overload check gets `self()` and `message_queue_len` (`logger_olp.erl:362, 483`). At the stop of the kernel application, the `application_master` of `kernel` calls `processes/0` and `process_info(P, group_leader)` to kill the processes of the application (application_master.erl:439-453), and the stop path calls `erlang:cancel_timer/1`. (#76, #77, #108, #184, #449, #859) |
| Unimplemented BIFs | Each entry of the BIF table (`src/runtime/interpreter/bif/table.rs`) that is `unsupported` returns the runtime error `unresolved external MFA M:F/A` when a process calls it. These entries include `erlang:read_timer/2`, `erlang:exit/3`, `persistent_term:put_new/2`, `persistent_term:info/0`, `maps:get/2`, and `maps:is_key/2`. The pinned compiler changes a direct call of `maps:get/2` to the `map_get` BIF and of `maps:is_key/2` to the `is_map_key` BIF, which PBeam runs. Thus only a fun call or an `apply` that the compiler does not change reaches the two `maps` entries. Measured: a call of `fun maps:get/2` returns `unresolved external MFA maps:get/2`. | `erlang:is_builtin/3` returns `true` for each entry of `bif.tab`, and each entry runs its BIF. | No measured path of #25 calls these BIFs. The backlog entries "Run the unsupported BIFs that OTP code calls" and "Load local fun literals and run the fun information BIFs" record the work. |
| Exception stack trace | PBeam builds the stack trace of an exception from the frames of the process, from the active frame to the first frame, and adjacent frames at the same return position give one frame, as ERTS `gather_stacktrace` records each continuation pointer once (`beam_common.c:817-834`). Each frame is `{Module, Function, Arity, Location}`, and each frame holds the arity in place of the arguments, except the first frame. The loader reads the `Line` chunk (`parse_line_chunk`, `beam_file.c:443-600`) into a line table of the module, keyed by instruction index. `Location` is `[{file, File}, {line, Line}]` of the last `line` at or before the instruction in the same function (`lookup_loc`, `beam_ranges.c:330-368`), and `[]` for a module with no `Line` chunk or an instruction with no line: the current frame at the instruction that raises, and a caller frame at its call, as ERTS looks up each continuation pointer one byte before it (`beam_common.c:817-877`). A module that `BeamProgram::load` loads with the name of a preloaded module of ERTS, such as `erlang` or `init`, keeps no line table, because ERTS builds each preloaded module without its `Line` chunk (`make_preload:143-157`). The error of `badmatch`, `case_end`, `try_case_end`, `badrecord`, and `if_end` gets the stack trace `[{Module, Function, Arity, Location} | CallerFrames]` with at most `backtrace_depth` frames, and one frame at the depth 0 (`beam_common.c:1011-1027`). The errors of each other instruction, for example `badarith` of a float instruction, `badmap` of `put_map_exact`, and `badfun` of a fun call, get the stack trace `[]`. `erlang:error/1`, `erlang:error/2`, `erlang:exit/1`, and `erlang:throw/1` give at most `backtrace_depth` frames, and one frame at the depth 0 (`save_stacktrace`, `beam_common.c:880-935`; `bif.c:1626-1637`); the first frame holds `Args` for `erlang:error/2`, and the arity for `erlang:error/1`, `erlang:exit/1`, `erlang:throw/1` (`bif.c:1536-1540`, `4132-4136`), or a term that is not a list. An uncaught `exit/1` exits the process with `Reason` alone. `erlang:error(Reason, Args, Options)` gives the stack trace of `erlang:error(Reason, Args)`. When `Options` is a proper list that is not empty and each element is `{error_info, Map}` with a map `Map`, the location of the first frame is `[{error_info, Map}]` of the last element, after the `file` and `line` of the call. For each other `Options` term, the location is `[]`, and the BIF does not raise `badarg`. Thus `io:format/2` to a device that replies `{error, foo}` raises `error:badarg` with the first frame `{io, format, [Format, Args], [{error_info, #{cause => {device, foo}, module => erl_stdlib_errors}}]}`. A tail call of the fun `fun erlang:error/1`, `fun erlang:error/2`, `fun erlang:error/3`, `fun erlang:exit/1`, or `fun erlang:throw/1` first removes the frame of the calling function, so the first frame is of the function before it. When no frame is before it, the first frame is `{Module, Function, Arity, Location}` of the initial call of the process, where `Location` is the `error_info` location of `erlang:error/3`, else `[]`. A tail call of `erlang:error/1,2,3`, `erlang:exit/1`, or `erlang:throw/1` through `apply/3` keeps the frame of the calling function, as ERTS `apply_bif_error_adjustment` does (`beam_common.c:1345-1431`). The frames of the caller of `error_handler:crash/1` make the list one frame longer than on ERTS in about one of four calls of an `undef` exception. The `undef` of a function that a loaded module does not export gets the stack trace `[{Module, Function, Args, []} | CallerFrames]` (see "Undefined functions"). The `function_clause` of a `func_info` instruction gets the stack trace `[{Module, Function, Args, Location} | CallerFrames]`, where `Args` is the list of the argument registers, `Location` is the location of the `line` before `func_info` (`beam_common.c:1231-1233`; `func_line`, `jit/x86/ops.tab:831-860`), and at most `backtrace_depth` frames (`save_stacktrace`, `beam_common.c:1011-1024`). The `badarg` or `system_limit` of a `bs_create_bin` segment gets the stack trace `[{Module, Function, Arity, [{error_info, ErrorInfo}]}]` with the ERTS `error_info`, with no `file` and `line`. The handler of a `try` or a `catch` gets the raw stack trace `{Class, Stacktrace}` in `x2`, and `build_stacktrace` gives its `Stacktrace`. The error of each other BIF gets the stack trace `[{Module, Function, Args, Location} | CallerFrames]` with at most `backtrace_depth` frames, as ERTS `save_stacktrace` gives it (`beam_common.c:903-1005`). `Args` is the argument list of the BIF. `Location` is `[{error_info, #{module => Format}}]`, where `Format` is `erl_erts_errors` for `atomics`, `counters`, `erlang`, `erts_internal`, and `persistent_term`, `erl_kernel_errors` for `code` and `os`, and `erl_stdlib_errors` for `binary`, `ets`, `lists`, `maps`, `math`, `re`, and `unicode` (`beam_common.c:939-991`). For each other module, for example `pbeam_cap`, the location is `[]`. The caller frames start at the function that calls the BIF: for a `bif` or `gc_bif` instruction with the fail label 0, and for `call_ext`, `call_ext_last`, and `call_ext_only` of a BIF, because the loader makes a body call of each (`jit/arm/ops.tab:709-714`). After a tail `apply/3` or a tail call of a fun of a BIF, they start at the function before it. The `badarg` and `system_limit` of `erlang:apply/2`, `erlang:apply/3`, and the `apply` instruction get the BIF frame `{erlang, apply, Args, Location}` (`beam_common.c:1434-1551`, `1734-1762`). A process that starts at a BIF or at `erlang:apply/3` of a BIF, and the wake-up from `erlang:hibernate/3`, get only the BIF frame. A process that starts at `erlang:error/1,2,3`, `erlang:exit/1`, or `erlang:throw/1` gets the stack trace `[]`. An error of a BIF that a trap to a BIF export continues gets the frame of the BIF call. The `lists` BIFs give the rest of the list in the arguments, and the other BIFs give the arguments of the call (#996). The `length/1` of a `bif` instruction gets the BIF frame with the list of the call. Thus `catch erlang:apply(1, f, [])` in `main/0` gives `{'EXIT', {badarg, [{erlang, apply, [1, f, []], [{error_info, #{module => erl_erts_errors}}]}, {Module, main, 0, []}]}}`, and a process that wakes up from `erlang:hibernate(erlang, apply, [1, f, []])` exits with `{badarg, [{erlang, apply, [1, f, []], [{error_info, #{module => erl_erts_errors}}]}]}`. `erlang:spawn_opt/4`, the `ets` BIFs (#968), the BIFs of `bif.c` that PBeam runs (#969), the timer BIFs (#971), and the BIFs of `erl_bif_re.c`, `erl_bif_os.c`, `erl_bif_port.c`, and `external.c` that PBeam runs (#972) add the ERTS `cause`: for example, an option that `erlang:spawn_opt/4` rejects gives `#{cause => badopt, module => erl_erts_errors}` (`bif.c:1040-1051`). The `bif.c` BIFs with a cause are `monitor/2,3` (`badopt`, `badtype`, `bif.c:966-967`, `991-995`), `register/2` (`none`, `notalive`, `registered_name`, `register.c:183-227`), `erlang:send/3` (`badopt`, `bif.c:2713-2730`), `process_flag/2` (`badopt`, `none`, `bif.c:1902-1912`, `2229-2233`), `halt/2` (`badopt`, `bif.c:4395-4397`), and `erlang:system_flag/2` (`badopt`, `bif.c:5297-5299`). The timer BIFs with a cause are `erlang:send_after/3,4` and `erlang:start_timer/3,4`: `badopt` for a bad option list and `time` for a bad time (`erl_hl_timer.c:2573-2597`, `2612-2636`). PBeam does not run `link/2` and `exit/3`, so their cause `badopt` (`bif.c:146-149`, `1739-1743`) is missing, and `erlang:display_string/2` does not give the `errno` cause of a failed write (`bif.c:4287-4297`). Thus `erlang:spawn_opt/2` with a bad option raises `badarg` with that `error_info` and its own arguments, as `error_with_inherited_info/3` does on ERTS (`erlang.erl:10751-10756`, `13485-13492`). The `error_info` map of each other BIF has no `cause`. The `raw_raise` instruction raises the exception with the stack trace of its raw stack trace. The `raise` instruction raises the exception again with the class and the stack trace of the raw stack trace. For the raw stack trace `[]`, it raises class `error` with the stack trace `[]`. Each other term gives a runtime invariant error. `erlang:raise/3` checks its stack trace argument, and the exception gets its frames, as in ERTS: at most `backtrace_depth` frames, and one frame when the depth is 0. A frame with a location keeps it, and each `{M, F, A}` and `{Fun, Args}` frame gets the location `[]`. `erlang:system_flag(backtrace_depth, Depth)` sets the depth of the run (see "System flags"). `erlang:process_info(self(), current_stacktrace)` returns the frames of the caller with their locations, at most `backtrace_depth` frames, without the frame of `process_info/2` (`erl_bif_info.c:2397-2460`), and `current_stacktrace` of another live process returns a runtime error. | ERTS records the frames, the BIF arguments, and the `error_info` location. `error_3` reads `Options` until an element that is not `{error_info, Map}`, keeps the `Map` of the last element, and drops it when the rest is not `[]` (`bif.c:1462-1503`). `save_stacktrace` puts `[{error_info, Map}]` in the location (`beam_common.c:1030-1051`), and `erts_build_mfa_item` puts `{file, File}` and `{line, Line}` before it (`utils.c:3430-3445`). After a trap, ERTS gives the BIF frame with the arguments in the registers: the rest of the list for `lists:member/2`, and the first list again for `length/1` (`erl_bif_guard.c:243-247`). ERTS also adds the `cause` of a BIF error with `EXF_HAS_EXT_INFO` to the `error_info` map (`beam_common.c:993-1002`) for the BIFs of other files than `bif.c`, `erl_db.c`, `erl_hl_timer.c`, `erl_bif_re.c`, `erl_bif_os.c`, `erl_bif_port.c`, and `external.c`, for `erlang:monitor_node/3` (`dist.c:6499`, `6503`), and for `link/2`, `exit/3`, and the write error of `erlang:display_string/2`. The stack trace of `erlang:nif_error/1` starts with the `erlang:nif_error/1` frame. `process_info(self(), current_stacktrace)` returns the frames of the caller, so `gen_statem:terminate/7` runs `erlang:raise(Class, Reason, Stacktrace)`. `current_stacktrace` of another process returns the frames of that process. `erlang:error/1` saves the current frame and at most `backtrace_depth - 1` continuation pointers (`save_stacktrace`, `beam_common.c:880-935`). | #926 added the line table and the locations, the stack trace of the `badmatch`, `case_end`, `try_case_end`, `badrecord`, and `if_end` errors, and `current_stacktrace` of the calling process. The backlog entry "Give the exact caller frames of an `undef` exception" of `docs/backlog.md` owns the extra frame of the error handler path. #446 added the caller frames of `erlang:error/1,2` and the frames that `erlang:raise/3` keeps. #680 added the `function_clause` stack trace. #677 added the `error_info` location of `erlang:error/3`. #692 added the stack trace of `erlang:exit/1` and `erlang:throw/1`. The `undef` path of #447 runs `error_handler:crash/1`, which adds the frame of its caller in about one of four calls. The `bs_create_bin` error path and `current_stacktrace` of another process are not implemented. #715 added the stack trace of a BIF error and the `cause` of `erlang:spawn_opt/4`. The backlog entry "Give the `error_info` cause of BIF errors" of `docs/backlog.md` owns the other causes. (#180, #400, #446, #447, #677, #692) |
| Exception raise sites | Only `erlang:nif_error/1`, `erlang:exit/1`, `erlang:error/1`, `erlang:error/2`, `erlang:error/3`, `erlang:throw/1`, `erlang:raise/3`, the `raise`, `raw_raise`, `badmatch`, `erlang:map_get/2` and the `put_map_exact` instruction, and the `badmap` exceptions of `maps:put/3`, `maps:merge/2`, `maps:remove/2`, `maps:find/2`, `maps:take/2`, `erlang:is_map_key/2`, Each of these sites returns a runtime error instead: `undef` for an external function or an external fun whose module is not loaded. `put_map_assoc` gives a runtime error for a source that is not a map: the compiler puts an `is_map` test and an `erlang:error({badmap, Map})` call before it, and ERTS has no exception for another source (beam_common.c:1981-1985). `erlang:raise/3` returns the atom `badarg`, and does not raise, for a class that is not `error`, `exit`, or `throw` and for a stack trace that is not valid, as in ERTS. The `raw_raise` instruction writes `badarg` to `x0`, and does not raise, for a class that is not `error`, `exit`, or `throw`, as in ERTS. | Each site raises an exception in the calling process. `raise` raises the original class again, and the other sites raise class `error` with the reason `{badmatch, V}`, `{case_clause, V}`, `if_clause`, `{badrecord, V}`, `function_clause`, `undef`, `{badmap, M}`, `{badkey, K}`, `badarg`, `timeout_value`, or `{try_clause, V}`. `erlang:error/1`, `erlang:error/2`, and `erlang:error/3` raise class `error` with their first argument as the reason. `erlang:raise/3` and `raw_raise` raise their class, also `throw`. `erlang:throw/1` raises class `throw`. `try` and `catch` can catch each of them. | Task #73 adds the exception model and the raises of `erlang:nif_error/1` and `erlang:exit/1`. | Undefined functions | A `call_ext`, `call_ext_last`, `call_ext_only`, `apply`, or `apply_last` instruction, an `erlang:apply/2` or `erlang:apply/3` call, and a `call_fun` or `call_fun2` of an external fun, to a function that a loaded module does not export, run the pinned `error_handler:undefined_function/3`. The export entry puts the module, the function, and the argument list in `x0`, `x1`, and `x2` and enters that export (`call_error_handler`, `beam_common.c:1294-1342`). The handler loads the module of the failed call through `code:ensure_loaded/1` or `init:ensure_loaded/1` (`error_handler.erl:85-98`, `182-198`), runs the function when the module now exports it, calls `Module:'$handle_undefined_function'(Function, Args)` when the module exports that function, and else raises `error:undef` with the frame of the failed call and the frames of its callers (`error_handler.erl:165-178`, `206-213`). `erlang:process_flag(error_handler, Module)` sets the module of the handler of the process, and `process_flag(error_handler, error_handler)` sets the default handler again. A custom handler answers the call itself: it runs the function of a module that it loads, and it can raise `undef` for a module that the run does not load (`error_handler_custom` of `fixtures/error_handler`). A BIF trap target that the run has no loaded export for enters the same handler in the next turn, after the switch out of the trap. When the process error handler has no `undefined_function/3`, PBeam raises `undef` with only the frame of the target function; ERTS adds the frames of the caller and the `error_info` cause (row "Exception stack trace"). A process that `spawn/3`, `spawn_link/3`, or `spawn_opt/4` starts at such a function, and the root process of a run, have no frame until they first run. The roots of `BeamProgram::execute_on_schedulers` still need an exported function of a loaded module, and else stop the run with `missing BEAM module M` or `missing export M:F/A`. Then it calls the handler, or exits with `{undef, [{Module, Function, Args, []} | CallerFrames]}`. A spawn with more than 1023 arguments exits with `{system_limit, []}` when the child first runs, also when the module is not loaded. A run without a code server and without the init process of `pbeam_boot` has no process that answers the load call of the default handler: `init:ensure_loaded/1` sends to the registered name `init` and waits for its reply (`init.erl:463-464`, `485-490`), and a send to a name that no process has raises `badarg` (`error_handler.erl:182-198`). Such a run stops with the runtime error `unresolved external MFA M:F/A` when the process uses the default handler and the call is for a module that the run does not load. A custom handler of such a run answers the call. A call of `error_handler:crash/1` gives one more frame than on ERTS in about one of four calls, because PBeam adds the frame of the caller of the handler to the snapshot of `erlang:error/1` for the depth of `backtrace_depth`. The reductions of the `error_handler` code match ERTS when the run has the live code server of ERTS: the fixture of `tests/feature/error_handler.rs` gives `[{plain,16},{handled,8}]` on the boot run of `pbeam_boot` with `--root` and on `erl +S 1`. A trap target that also loads the module charges other reductions: the first `re:run/3` call with `global` of `tests/feature/re_global.rs` loads `re`, and its charge differs from ERTS, while the global runs after the load charge 26 and 29 on both. A run of the library API has no code server, so `error_handler:ensure_loaded/1` takes the longer `init:ensure_loaded/1` path, and the `plain` case of the fixture of `tests/integration/error_handler.rs` costs 25 against 16 on ERTS. | The export entry of the function calls `error_handler:undefined_function(Module, Function, Args)` (`call_error_handler` in `beam_common.c:1294-1342`; JIT `emit_export_trampoline` in `jit/arm/beam_asm_global.cpp:164-232`). For a loaded module, it calls `Module:'$handle_undefined_function'(Function, Args)` when the module exports it (`error_handler.erl:85-98`, `206-213`). Else `crash/1` raises `error:undef` through `erlang:raise/3` with the stack trace `[{Module, Function, Args, []} | CallerFrames]` (`error_handler.erl:165-178`). For a module that is not loaded, `error_handler` loads it from the code path, or raises `undef`. The `error_handler` code costs reductions. Measured on `erl +S 1` with no garbage collection: a call of `undef_handler:missing(m)` costs 11 reductions, where a local call and its return cost 2, and `try undef_target:missing(a) catch error:undef -> ok end` costs 13. A new process starts with `i_apply_only` of its MFA (`erl_process.c:12616`; JIT `jit/beam_jit_main.cpp:258`), so it goes to the same export entry when it first runs. Before the export lookup, the `apply` of `i_apply_only` raises `system_limit` for more than `MAX_REG - 1` (1023) arguments (`beam_common.c:1501-1513`), with the stack trace `[{erlang, apply, [Module, Function, Args], ErrorInfo}]`. | PBeam runs the pinned `error_handler.beam`, so the value, the class, the reason, and the first frame of the `undef` exception match ERTS. The frames of the callers come from the frames of the process, with the locations of the line table (row "Exception stack trace"). A run of the library API has no code server and no init process, so a call of a module that the run does not load keeps the runtime error `unresolved external MFA`; `pbeam_boot` starts the init process that answers `{ensure_loaded, Module}` (`init.erl:730-750`). The backlog entry "Give caller frames and line locations in stack traces" of `docs/backlog.md` owns the depth of the snapshot of `erlang:error/1`. Evidence: `undef_probe:calls/0` and `undef_probe:down/3` of `fixtures/undef` give the same first frame in PBeam and in `erl -noshell -kernel logger_level none -pa fixtures/undef` (`tests/integration/undef.rs`). `error_handler_probe` of `fixtures/error_handler` compares the `undef` tuple, the frames, `process_flag(error_handler, Module)`, and the reductions (`tests/integration/error_handler.rs`). The boot loads a module that the run does not load (`tests/feature/error_handler.rs`). For 1024 arguments, the reasons match and only the stack trace differs (row "Exception stack trace"). Without `-kernel logger_level none`, ERTS can write the report `Error in process <pid> with exit value: {undef,[{undef_target,missing,[a,b],[]}]}` of the child to stderr (row "Error report"). (#166, #447) |
| Code loading | Before the run, PBeam loads the start set that the run gets into the code table of the run, and each other module loads on demand. With `--root`, `pbeam_boot` starts the pinned `code_server`, which holds the code path (row "Boot"). Without `--root`, the run has no code server. In the run, `erlang:load_module/2`, `erlang:prepare_loading/2` (through `erts_internal:prepare_loading/2`), and `erlang:finish_loading/1` add a new module to the code table with the ERTS results, and the atoms of the module go into the atom table of the run. These cases differ. `erlang:finish_loading/1` of a loaded module, or of a module whose code waits for its `-on_load` function (code replacement), stops the run with the runtime error `erlang:finish_loading/1 cannot replace the loaded module M`. For a module with an `-on_load` function (opcode 149), `erlang:finish_loading/1` returns `{on_load, [Module]}`, and a list of more than one module with such a module raises `system_limit`. The code of the module waits for its function: `erlang:module_loaded/1` and `erlang:function_exported/3` give `false`, and a call goes to the error handler. `erlang:call_on_load_function/1` runs the function as the ERTS `i_call_on_load_function` instruction, and raises `badarg` through `apply` and for a module with no waiting code. `erlang:finish_after_on_load(Module, true)` makes the code the loaded code of the module, with visible exports. `erlang:finish_after_on_load(Module, false)` keeps the code until a purge, so the function can run again, and the module stays not loaded. Both return `true`, and a `Keep` that is not a boolean or a module with no waiting code raises `badarg`. `erlang:finish_after_on_load(Module, true)` after `false` stops the run with the runtime error `erlang:finish_after_on_load/2 cannot keep the code of M after its exports were dropped`. The boot of `pbeam_boot` starts the pinned `erts_code_purger` (row "Boot"), so `code:ensure_loaded/1` and a call that loads on demand run the `-on_load` function through the pinned `code_server` and `erts_code_purger`, as on ERTS. When the function does not return `ok`, the pinned `erts_code_purger` purges the code, and the load gives `{error, on_load_failure}`, as on ERTS (#710). `erts_internal:purge_module(Module, prepare_on_load)` makes the waiting code the old code of the module, `erts_internal:request_system_task/3` with `check_process_code` replies `true` for each process with a frame in that code, so that `erts_code_purger` kills it, and `erts_internal:purge_module(Module, complete)` removes the code. A later load of the module runs its function again. A local fun of removed code raises `error:{badfun, Fun}` at its call when the loaded code of the module name of the fun holds no entry of the fun or holds an entry with another old unique value (#740). These cases differ. `erts_internal:purge_module/2` accepts the process registered as `erts_code_purger` as its caller, and raises `notsup` for each other caller. The operation `prepare` gives `false` for a module with no old code and raises `badarg` while a purge runs, as ERTS does (`beam_bif_load.c:2108-2110`, `2143-2144`), so `code:purge/1` gives `false` and `code:soft_purge/1` gives `true` for such a module. `prepare` of a module with the old code that `erlang:delete_module/1` leaves, and `abort`, stop the run with the runtime error `unsupported erts_internal:purge_module/2 operation O`, and `prepare_on_load` of a loaded module with no waiting code stops the run with the runtime error `erts_internal:purge_module/2 found no on_load code of M`. `erts_internal:request_system_task/3` checks a process of the scheduler of the caller at once and sends the reply at once; for a process of another scheduler, that scheduler checks the process when it drains its inbox, or after the signals that the process has to handle. A `garbage_collect` request whose first argument is not `minor` or `major`, or that has no first argument, raises `badarg` before ERTS schedules the task (`erl_process.c:11412-11423`). The requests `garbage_collect` and `system_task_test` stop the run with the runtime error `unsupported erts_internal:request_system_task/3 request R`. A call of a fun of the code after `prepare_on_load` stops the run with the runtime error `missing BEAM module M`. `erlang:system_info(outstanding_system_requests_limit)` gives `2`, and `erts_internal:processes_next/1` reads the process table as ERTS does, without the policy process for a process that is not a boot process. A start module with an `-on_load` function stops the load of the program with the error `the start module M has an -on_load function, which PBeam runs only for a module that loads in the run`. Bytes that are an IFF form with the given module name, but that the PBeam loader cannot decode, stop the run with the same runtime error and its cause, also when ERTS gives `{error, badfile}` for a corrupt chunk or loads the module, for example with bytes after the form. PBeam sends no error report to the system logger for bad bytes. A compressed module gives `{error, badfile}`, because the `zlib` NIF stubs raise (row "NIF stubs"). A prepared entry that does not finish stays in memory until the run ends. `erlang:finish_loading/1` puts the code into the table at once, and the caller switches out to the end of the run queue. `erlang:module_loaded/1` returns `true` for each module of the code table, `false` for each other atom, and raises `badarg` for a term that is not an atom. The example build (`scripts/example.sh`) gives the `pbeam` binary a start set and the code path of the pinned OTP and Elixir, as an interactive-mode ERTS run has: the boot modules of `pbeam_boot`, the example modules, `pbeam_boot.beam`, the preloaded modules of ERTS that PBeam loads from the ebin directory of erts (`init` and `erl_prim_loader`), and each consolidated protocol that the example calls. The run gets `--pa` with each directory of the code path and `--root` with the root directory of the pinned OTP. Each other module that the example and the boot call, directly or through other modules, loads on demand through the pinned `error_handler` and `code_server`, as it does in an interactive-mode ERTS run. The build writes the whole list of the build into `target/examples/<name>/pbeam.beams` and the start set into `target/examples/<name>/pbeam.start`. The PBeam loader does not load each called module of this list: `crypto` (a start module with an `-on_load` function, because a policy run gives the whole list as its start set), and `elixir_errors`, `shell_docs`, and `Elixir.IEx.Evaluator` (`bs_match_string`, opcode 132). The build writes each such module and the reason into `target/examples/<name>/pbeam.unloaded`. A call to one of them stops the run with the runtime error `unresolved external MFA`. `code:ensure_loaded/1` runs the pinned `code` module. For a loaded module, it returns `{module, Module}`. With `--root`, `code:get_mode/0`, `code:ensure_loaded/1`, `code:which/1`, and `code:get_path/0` run the pinned `code` and `code_server` modules and give the ERTS results: in interactive mode, `code:ensure_loaded/1` of a module that is not loaded loads it from the code path, or returns `{error, nofile}`, and in embedded mode it returns `{error, embedded}`. `erlang:pre_loaded/0` gives the fixed ERTS list of 22 modules, also when the run did not load one of them, and `code_server` marks each of them `preloaded`. The `init` request process answers `init:fetch_loaded/0` with `[]`, because the boot loads no module with `primLoad`. Thus a start module that is not in the list of `erlang:pre_loaded/0` is not in the module database of `code_server`: `code:which/1` gives the first file of the code path for it, not the file that the run loaded, and `code:is_loaded/1` and `code:all_loaded/0` do not give it. Without `--root`, `code:ensure_loaded/1` of a module that is not loaded calls `code_server:get_mode/0`, and the run stops with the runtime error `unresolved external MFA code_server:get_mode/0`, or `code_server:get_mode/0` raises `badarg` when the run loads `code_server.beam`, because no code server put the persistent term `code_server`. A call to a module that is not loaded loads it through the pinned `error_handler` and `code_server` (row "Undefined functions"), so a process of an example waits for a module load as on ERTS. Terra Counter sends its result to `main` after its terminal owner restores the terminal (Terra `30dea566` `lib/terra/runtime.ex:282-288`, `356-359`). The first `IO.inspect/1` call of `main` loads 11 modules in an interactive-mode ERTS run and in a PBeam run, and the terminal owner writes the restore sequence while `main` waits. Thus PBeam writes the restore sequence `\e[0m\e[?25h\e[?1049l` before the result `{:ok, 1}`, as an interactive-mode ERTS run does, and the result stays on the main screen (#428). | `module_loaded_1` returns `true` for a module with current code and no pending `-on_load` function (`beam_bif_load.c:738-755`). `erts_internal:prepare_loading/2` gives `{error, badfile}` for bytes that the loader cannot read and for a module name that is not the given name (`beam_load.c:125-240`), and it sends an error report such as `Error loading module queue_x: corrupt file header` to the system logger (`beam_load_report_error`, `beam_load.c:372`). `erlang:prepare_loading/2` first decompresses bytes that do not start with `FOR1` with `zlib:gunzip/1` (`erlang.erl:5436-5447`). `erlang:finish_loading/1` of a loaded module makes the current code old (`beam_load.c:258-262`). For a module with an `-on_load` function, it returns `{on_load, [Module]}`, and the module waits for its function (`beam_bif_load.c:484-504`; `beam_load.c:259-330`): its exports keep a deferred address (`jit/asm_load.c:1188-1195`). It raises `system_limit` for more than one module when one has an `-on_load` function (`beam_bif_load.c:395-408`). The JIT runs `erlang:call_on_load_function/1` as an instruction, and the BIF raises `badarg` (`jit/arm/ops.tab:630-641`, `jit/arm/instr_bif.cpp:994-1031`, `beam_bif_load.c:793-817`). `finish_after_on_load_2` makes the code current with `true`, and with `false` clears the deferred addresses and keeps the code until `erts_internal:purge_module(Module, prepare_on_load)` (`beam_bif_load.c:819-941`, `2088-2160`). `erts_internal:purge_module(Module, prepare_on_load)` moves the `on_load` code to the old code and returns `true`, or `false` for a module that does not exist, and `complete` removes the old code; a second `prepare_on_load` during a purge, and `complete` of another module, raise `badarg` (`beam_bif_load.c:2088-2337`). `erts_code_purger` sends a `check_process_code` request to each process of `erlang:processes_iterator/0` and kills each process that refers to the code (`erts_code_purger.erl:166-182`, `213-359`). `check_process_code` looks at the instruction pointer and at each continuation pointer of the stack (`beam_bif_load.c:1149-1212`), and the reply is `{check_process_code, RequestId, Result}`, with `false` for a process that does not exist or exits (`erl_process.c:11309-11492`, `10574-10620`). `erts_internal:processes_next/1` reads 25 indices of the table for each pid that it can give (`erl_ptab.c:1472-1534`). The default limit of outstanding system requests is twice the number of schedulers (`erl_init.c:1354`). Measured with `on_load_server_probe:failure/0`, `killed/0`, `survived/0`, and `system_tasks/0` of `fixtures/code_server` (#710). After `false`, `true` makes the code current with exports that call the error handler (`beam_bif_load.c:887-897`). ERTS starts `erts_code_purger` as a system process (`erl_init.c:2550-2557`), and `code_server` runs the function in a new process and calls `erts_code_purger:finish_after_on_load/2` (code_server.erl:1348-1403; erts_code_purger.erl:154-182). The ERTS boot runs the `-on_load` function of each module of the boot script (init.erl:1381-1403, 1857-1880). Measured with `load_probe:on_load_ok/1`, `on_load_error/1`, and `on_load_crash/1` of `fixtures/code` and `on_load_server_probe` of `fixtures/code_server` (#708). ERTS frees a prepared entry when the garbage collector frees its magic reference. The caller of `erlang:finish_loading/1` is suspended until a code barrier commits the code, and then it gets the value (`beam_bif_load.c:542-566`). `code:ensure_loaded/1` returns `{module, Module}` when `erlang:module_loaded/1` returns `true` (`code.erl:581-583`). Else it reads the code mode from the persistent term `code_server` (`code.erl:585`, `1278-1279`; `code_server.erl:84-85`). In interactive mode, the code server searches the code path and loads the module, or returns an error such as `{error, nofile}` (`code.erl:586-600`). In embedded mode, the call returns `{error, embedded}` (`code.erl:601-602`). Measured: `code:ensure_loaded(code_probe_missing)` returns `{error,nofile}`. `code_server:init/3` puts each module of `erlang:pre_loaded/0` and of `init:fetch_loaded/0` into its module database, makes the interactive path of the `lib` directories of the root, and merges it with the `erl_prim_loader` path (`code_server.erl:91-125`, `513-535`). `init:fetch_loaded/0` gives the modules that the boot script loaded with `primLoad`, with their files, and then clears the list (`init.erl:720-722`). `code:which/1` gives the file of a module of the database, `preloaded`, or else the first file of the path (`code.erl:1782-1789`). `erlang:pre_loaded/0` gives the 22 modules of the emulator (`erl_init.c:459-484`). Measured with `erl +S 1 -noshell -boot start_clean -pa fixtures/code_server`: `code:which(lists)` gives the `lib/stdlib-8.1/ebin/lists.beam` file of the root, and `code:get_path/0` gives `fixtures/code_server`, the `ebin` directories of `kernel` and `stdlib`, the other `lib` directories, and `"."` (`code_server_probe` of `tests/feature/code_server.rs`). In interactive mode, the first `IO.inspect/1` call of Terra Counter loads 11 modules through `error_handler` and `code_server` (`error_handler.erl:85-98`, `182-198`; `code.erl:581-600`). While `main` waits for these loads, the terminal owner writes the restore sequence first. Measured (#428): pinned ERTS with the same modules loaded before `main` writes `{:ok, 1}` first in 6 of 6 runs, as PBeam does. In 5 of these 6 runs, `halt()` stops the node before the restore write arrives, so these runs show only that `main` writes first, not the order of the two writes. | ERTS cannot boot without `-root` (`init.erl:1226-1232`), so a run without `--root` keeps its start and starts no code server (#445). The pinned `code_server` reads only `erlang:pre_loaded/0` and `init:fetch_loaded/0` for its module database (`code_server.erl:95-102`), and the `pbeam` binary does not give the start file names to the boot. [#447](https://github.com/fahchen/pbeam/issues/447) runs `error_handler.beam` for an undefined function, and [#448](https://github.com/fahchen/pbeam/issues/448) runs the examples from a code path. PBeam has old code only after `erlang:delete_module/1`, and it has no code replacement (out of scope of #440). ERTS lets only its system process `erts_code_purger` call `erts_internal:purge_module/2` (`beam_bif_load.c:2090-2092`), and PBeam starts the purger as a normal process of the boot. PBeam has old code only during the purge of a failed `-on_load` function, so it does not implement the soft purge. ERTS runs a `check_process_code` request when the target process next runs (`erl_process.c:10859-10876`), and PBeam has no system task queue. The process of a pending request does not run between the request and the check, because PBeam has one scheduler, so the check sees the same frames. ERTS keys the fun entry of a lambda by the module checksum, the index, and the arity, and keeps the address of the loaded code in it (`erl_fun.c:79-85`; `jit/asm_load.c:1218-1240`), so a call of a fun enters that address and raises `error:{badfun, Fun}` when the entry holds no address (`beam_common.c:1626-1630, 1697-1706`). PBeam has no code replacement, so it keeps the index, the old unique value, and the label of each lambda that the loaded code makes a fun from and a call of a local fun compares the index and the old unique value of the fun with those entries of the code of its module name; the fun holds no module checksum (#740). A BEAM file whose `FunT` chunk a change gives the old unique value of another version of the module makes the call enter that code, where ERTS raises `error:{badfun, Fun}`. ERTS marks the funs of the code and suspends a process that calls one until the purge ends (`beam_bif_load.c:2150-2157`). The purge removes only code that no export names, so no resolved export target ([#628](https://github.com/fahchen/pbeam/issues/628)) names it. PBeam has no export state that calls the error handler for loaded code, so `true` after `false` stops the run. The start set has no boot script, so PBeam cannot run the `-on_load` function of a start module before the run. The resolved export target of each import ([#628](https://github.com/fahchen/pbeam/issues/628)) depends on this: the code table fills a target when a call finds the export and never empties it. A change that replaces or purges code must empty each target that names the old code, as the ERTS load changes the export entry (`export.c:204-242`). The PBeam loader cannot tell a corrupt chunk from a form that it does not decode yet. PBeam has no garbage collector for references, and its one scheduler commits code at once (#442). The PBeam loader does not decode `bs_match_string` yet. The re-scan of the Terra Counter gaps (#339) finds which of these modules the run calls. `erlang:delete_module/1` moves the current code to old code and keeps it in `by_id` until a purge, so `erlang:fun_info/2` gives the name of the function of a fun of that code (`erl_fun.c:37-46`); a call of such a fun stops the run with the runtime error `missing BEAM module M`, because `CodeTable::fun_frame` reads only the current code and the code that waits for `-on_load`. |
| Module information | `erlang:get_module_info/2`, which `Module:module_info/1` calls, gives the module for the key `module`. A module that is not an atom or not loaded, and a term that is not a key of ERTS, raise `badarg`. The keys `md5`, `exports`, `functions`, `nifs`, `attributes`, `compile`, `native_addresses`, and `native` stop the run with the runtime error `erlang:get_module_info/2 key K is not implemented`. `erlang:get_module_info/1`, which `Module:module_info/0` calls, gives the pair of the key `module` and stops the run with the runtime error `erlang:get_module_info/1 key exports is not implemented` at the key `exports`, the first key of its list that PBeam cannot give. | `get_module_info/3` gives the MD5 of the module, the exports, the functions, the NIFs, the attributes, the compile information, `[]` for `native_addresses`, and `false` for `native` (`erl_bif_info.c:5918-5945`). `erlang:get_module_info/1` gives a list of the keys `module`, `exports`, `attributes`, `compile`, and `md5` (`erl_bif_info.c:5946-5984`). | `code:do_start/0` calls only `M:module_info(module)` (`code.erl:1699-1712`). The other keys need the MD5 of the code chunks, the attribute and compile chunks as terms, and the ERTS export table order. Evidence: `tests/integration/module_info.rs`. (#445) |
| Process names | `erlang:register/2` gives a name to a process, and an exit frees the name. `erlang:whereis/1` returns the pid of a name or `undefined`, and raises `badarg` for a term that is not an atom. PBeam registers no port: `erlang:register/2` with a port returns a runtime error (see "Ports"), so a name never gives a port. `erlang:unregister/1` and `erlang:registered/0` return the runtime error `unresolved external MFA`. The `send` instruction gives a message to the process of a registered name and raises `badarg` for a name that no process has. | `whereis/1` returns the pid or port of a name or `undefined`, `unregister/1` frees a name, and `registered/0` returns all names. A send to a registered name delivers the message, and a send to a name that is not registered raises `badarg`. | The current fixtures register names, look up names, read `registered_name`, send messages to names, and free a name with an exit only. No fixture path calls `unregister/1` or `registered/0`. (#77, #108, #125) |
| ETS tables | `ets:new/2` makes `set`, `bag`, `duplicate_bag`, and `ordered_set` tables. An `ordered_set` table keeps the hash structure of PBeam and a sorted list of its keys in the ERTS term order. It compares keys with the arithmetic term order (`cmp_key_eq`, `erl_db_tree.c:758-761`): an exactly equal key finds the stored hash, and a binary search of the sorted keys finds the other equal keys, so `1` and `1.0` are one key also inside a tuple or a list, and a map key compares its keys exactly and its values arithmetically. An insert of a new key and a delete move the keys after it in the sorted list, so they cost time in proportion to the table size, where the AVL tree of ERTS costs time in proportion to its logarithm (`erl_db_tree.c`). `ets:first/1`, `ets:last/1`, `ets:next/2`, `ets:prev/2`, and their `_lookup` forms walk the keys in the term order, and a step from a key that is not in the table gives the next key (`erl_db_tree.c:635-757`, `3167-3282`), so `ets:foldl/3` and `ets:foldr/3` give the ERTS order. Two different alias references have no PBeam order (row "Term order"), so a walk of a table where they meet in a comparison returns a runtime error, and a key lookup then compares each object. On the other table types, `ets:last/1` acts as `ets:first/1` and `ets:prev/2` as `ets:next/2` (`erl_db_hash.c:853-856`). Its single-key operations (`ets:insert/2`, `ets:insert_new/2`, `ets:lookup/2`, `ets:lookup_element/3,4`, `ets:member/2`, `ets:delete/2`, `ets:take/2`, `ets:update_element/3,4`, `ets:info/1,2`) give the ERTS result and reductions, where `ets:internal_delete_all/2` charges one reduction for each object (`do_free_tree_continue`, `erl_db_tree.c:2877-2904`). `ets:delete/1` and the owner exit free one object for each reduction of the turn, and they trap to `ets:delete_trap/0` when the table holds more objects than the turn can free, as ERTS traps (`erl_db.c:3064-3077`), with no charge for a segment. `ets:select/1,2,3`, `ets:select_reverse/1,2,3`, `ets:match/2,3`, `ets:match_object/2`, `ets:tab2list/1`, and `ets:select_delete/2` walk it in key order with the ERTS results, chunks, continuations, and reductions (`erl_db_tree.c:1199-2215`): a bound key reads only its object, a partially bound key limits the walk to its key range (`analyze_pattern`, `erl_db_tree.c:2756-2875`), a turn reads 1000 objects and traps to `ets:select/1` or the select-delete trap, and a result with a limit traps to `ets:reverse/3`. The continuation `{Table, LastKey, EndKey, Limit, Program, Matches, Reverse, Got}` holds the last key, so a change of the table between two turns does not stop the walk, as in ERTS. `Program` is a new ordinary reference where ERTS holds a magic reference. The delete of a table drops its programs, so the continuation of a deleted named table raises `badarg` on a new table of the same name, where ERTS runs it on the new table (`erl_db_tree.c:1937`, `1960`). On the other table types, `ets:select_reverse/1,2,3` act as `ets:select/1,2,3` (`erl_db_hash.c:2308-2318`). It accepts each option that ERTS accepts, and it raises `badarg` for each call that raises `badarg` on ERTS. `[bag, set]` makes a `bag` table, as in ERTS. A heir that is a live process other than the caller returns a runtime error. A heir that is not alive or that is the caller acts as `none`, as in ERTS at the owner exit. `ets:insert/2`, `ets:insert_new/2`, `ets:lookup_element/4`, `ets:lookup/2`, `ets:member/2`, and `ets:delete/2` work on these tables with the ERTS access rules, object order, and reductions, and they raise `badarg` as ERTS does. A `bag` table keeps no second object that is exactly equal to an object of the table, and a `duplicate_bag` table keeps it. `ets:delete/1` deletes a table with the ERTS access rules and reductions, frees its name at once, gives no heir the table, and traps to `ets:delete_trap/0` when the segments use more reductions than the turn has left, as ERTS traps to `ets:delete_trap/1` (`erl_db.c:3006-3082`, `5378-5397`). When a trapped list insert uses the table, `ets:delete/1` returns a runtime error. `ets:delete/1` and the owner exit free the objects of each segment in the turn that charges the segment, as ERTS does (`erl_db_hash.c:3140-3149`), and keep the position of the next segment across a trap or a switch-out. `ets:info/2` gives the item `type` of each table, gives `undefined` for a free name, and raises `badarg` as ERTS does. Each other item that ERTS knows and the item `binary` return a runtime error. The reference of a deleted table gives `undefined`, and another reference raises `badarg`.
