A bundle ships each `.so` file of the `priv` directories of its release, and the launcher extracts it to its path. The NIF gate of `pbeam_bundle` opens a shipped library on the build host and checks it against its module, so a module that loads a library passes the build only when a shipped library takes the load (`docs/bundle-format.md`). | ERTS loads each NIF version from 2.0 to 2.18 except 2.5, exports each `enif_*` function, converts a UTF-8 string in `enif_get_string`, leaves the bytes of a new binary unset, calls the upgrade callback, replaces each function of the library in a module without `nif_start`, and keeps the private data for `enif_priv_data` (erl_nif.c:4799-4813, 4903-4940, 4956-4987). It calls `unload` when it purges the module, adds the module to the taint list (erl_nif.c:4794-4797), and prints the error text with the bytes of each name and the atom text of the module. | The first C ABI version (#865) runs the `add/2` library of `fixtures/nif/nif_add.c`, and the owner decision of 2026-10-21 allows only NIF version 2.18. Task #866 adds the `enif_*` functions and the dirty NIFs of `bcrypt_elixir`, and task #904 ships the library of `bcrypt_elixir` in a bundle. (#865, #866, #904, #937) |
| Input select | One node poll thread waits with `select`, at all times, for the read select of fd 0, for the pipes of the ports (see "Ports"), for the socket of each `tcp_inet` listen port with a caller in its accept queue and of each connected port with a receive that waits, and for write of the socket of each port with queued output (see "tcp_inet ports"). Each fd has one waiter at a time, as in ERTS (erl_check_io.c:90-111). When an fd is ready, the thread adds it with its direction to the ready list of the scheduler that serves it and writes the wake socket of that scheduler; the scheduler serves the list at its next scheduling step, and the thread leaves out each reported fd until the scheduler served it (one-shot). A port that waits for a new fd at the release of its lock wakes the thread. A scheduler with no process to run takes its own fds over from the thread and waits for them itself, until the first timer end, or without end when no timer is active, and then gives them back. The hand-off costs about 5 us for each TCP round trip against a scheduler that waits alone (#983). Thus the select message, the `inet_async` message, and the `inet_reply` message arrive while processes run, at a time that depends on the load of the host, as in ERTS. The fd 0 select, the port input, and the socket I/O are still handled on the serving scheduler, not on the poll thread or another scheduler (#985). When fd 0 has input, its end of file, or an error, the select ends, and its recipient gets `{select, TTY, Ref, ready_input}`, as in ERTS. When the reader of the TTY resource exits, its TTY monitor removes the select. When fd 0 is not open while it has a select, the wait reports no fd and runs again at once; ERTS ends the select without a message (erl_check_io.c:1422-1434). The boot does not select a closed fd 0. When a select of fd 0 stays after fd 0 closed, each `select` of the poll thread fails with `EBADF` for the whole set, so the thread waits again at once and uses one CPU, and its other fds wait for the idle wait of their scheduler; ERTS removes such an fd and sends no message (erl_check_io.c:1422-1434). No test reaches this case. The same wait also ends at a handled host signal (row "OS signals"). | `tty_select/2` and each `read_nif/3` that gives `{ok, _}` select fd 0 for read with the caller as the recipient (prim_tty_nif.c:660, 1227; erl_check_io.c:1236-1465). A poll thread waits for the select at all times (erl_process.c:3339-3372), and an idle scheduler also waits in its poll until the next timer end (erl_process.c:3536-3567). The select is one-shot: when fd 0 has input or end of file, the poll sends `{select, TTY, Ref, ready_input}` to the recipient and removes the select (erl_check_io.c:2121-2171). When the reader exits, its monitor stops the select (prim_tty_nif.c:1242-1244). `INET_REQ_ACCEPT` selects a listen socket for read when no connection is pending (inet_drv.c:12224). ERTS uses `kqueue` on macOS and `epoll` on Linux, with a `select` or `poll` set for each fd that the kernel poll does not accept (erl_poll.h:100-135, 214-225). Thus the select message can arrive while the selecting process runs, at a time that depends on the load of the host. With fd 0 `/dev/null`, the ERTS reference of `exit_owned_probe:ttys/2` gave a different result in 6 of 300 runs under load, so the reference test gives ERTS an fd 0 that stays empty and open (#323). | ERTS runs the I/O of a ready fd in a port task that any scheduler can run; PBeam keeps it on the serving scheduler until #985. The macOS `poll` gives `POLLNVAL` for a character device such as `/dev/null`, so PBeam uses `select`, which reads each fd type on macOS and Linux. (#115, #299, #983) |
| Ports | `open_port/2` runs only `{spawn_executable, Name}` with the options `binary`, `exit_status`, `use_stdio`, `nouse_stdio`, `stderr_to_stdout`, `{args, Args}`, `{packet, N}`, `eof`, and `overlapped_io`; a name and each argument is a binary, an atom, or a list file name. `{packet, N}` with N 1, 2, or 4 puts a header of N bytes with the size before the data of each `port_command/2`, and data that does not fit in a header of 1 or 2 bytes closes the port with `einval`. The port sends each complete packet of the program as one message, and the end of file drops a packet that is not complete. The input and output counts are the bytes of the data without the headers. N is a small integer whose low 32 bits are 1, 2, or 4, as ERTS keeps it in a C `int`; each other N gives `badopt`. With `eof`, the end of file sends `{Port, eof}`, after `{Port, {exit_status, Status}}` with `exit_status`, and the port stays open and reads nothing more. `overlapped_io` changes nothing. `erts_internal:open_port/2` reads the settings in order and gives `badopt` for each element that ERTS does not accept, then gives `badarg` for a name that ERTS does not accept, as ERTS does. Each other ERTS option (`{line, L}`, `line`, `{env, Env}`, `{arg0, A}`, `{cd, Dir}`, `{parallelism, B}`, `{busy_limits_port, L}`, `{busy_limits_msgq, L}`, `stream`, `in`, `out`, and `hide`) returns a runtime error, also when a later element is not valid. The port kinds `spawn` and `fd`, a `spawn_driver` port other than `tcp_inet` (see "tcp_inet ports"), a port without `binary`, an `erlang:port_command/3` option list other than `[]`, and a `send`, `erlang:send/2`, `erlang:send/3`, `erlang:exit/2`, or `erlang:register/2` with a port return a runtime error; `erlang:port_call/3`, `erlang:port_to_list/1`, `erlang:is_port/1`, and `erlang:ports/0` return the runtime error `unresolved external MFA`. `erlang:port_info/2` gives the items of `port_info/1`, and the items `registered_name`, `memory`, `monitors`, `monitored_by`, `parallelism`, `queue_size`, `locking`, and `queue_data` return a runtime error. `link/1` and `unlink/1` with a port change both sides of the link at once, as a port operation that runs at once does; ERTS sends a link or unlink signal and an unlink acknowledgment between the process and the port. `erlang:port_info(Port)` and `erlang:port_info(Port, links)` keep the links in the ERTS red-black tree (`ml_cmp_keys`, `erl_monitor_link.c:91-233`), walk it in post-order (`foreach_unordered__`, `erl_rbtree.h:1343-1458`), and cons each pid to the front of the list (`erl_bif_info.c:3676-3696`), so the item has the ERTS order. `erlang:port_connect/2` sends its link signal to the new owner, as ERTS does. PBeam starts the program with `posix_spawn` in a new session, with an empty signal mask, the environment of the process, and the pipes as fds 0 and 1, or 3 and 4. The program keeps each other fd of the PBeam process that is not close-on-exec, and each signal that is ignored in the PBeam process is ignored in it. With the start dispositions of the `pbeam` binary (row "OS signals"), these are `SIGFPE` and `SIGPIPE`, as in an ERTS port program (measured with `perl`: `SIGFPE` and `SIGPIPE` ignored, `SIGCHLD` default). A failed `posix_spawn`, for example of a directory, returns a runtime error. After `os:set_signal(sigchld, ignore)`, the host reaps each program, so the end of a program of a port with `exit_status` returns a runtime error. The pipes are read only when no process can run (see "Input select"). `erlang:port_control/3` with the command `0x04c76a00` on a port of the spawn driver returns a runtime error, and each other command gives `badarg`. A `port_command/2` with data that is not iodata writes no error report. `erlang:port_command/2` writes the data with a blocking write on the scheduler thread, so a program that does not read its input stops all processes until it reads. Each port operation runs at once. `port_info/1` gives the port number as `id`. | ERTS starts the program from its `erl_child_setup` process with `fork` and `execve` (erl_child_setup.c:280-374) and reports a failed `execve` as the exit status of a port that opened (erl_child_setup.c:369). `erl_child_setup` reaps the programs (erl_child_setup.c:396-420), so the disposition of `SIGCHLD` in the BEAM process does not change the exit status (measured: `exit 7` gives 7). It sends the report `Bad value on output port 'Name'` to the logger for a `port_command/2` with data that is not iodata (io.c:1267-1271). It reads the pipes in its poll thread while processes run, and queues port output that does not fit in the pipe and marks the port busy (sys_drivers.c:1140-1203). `erl_child_setup` closes each fd from 4 at its start, and its other fds are close-on-exec (erl_child_setup.c:466-492). The control of the spawn driver runs only the command `0x04c76a00` (sys.h:1467), which `erl_child_setup` sends at the exit of the program: it replies `[]` and sets the exit state of the port (sys_drivers.c:731-756). It can schedule a port operation as a port task (io.c:923-1002). `port_info/1` gives the index of the port in the port table as `id` (erl_bif_info.c:3668-3670). ERTS runs each option, port kind, and port BIF. ERTS reads `{packet, N}` input in reads of at most the rest of a packet, and allocates the data of a packet when its header arrives, and it fails the port with `ENOMEM` when the allocation fails (sys_drivers.c:1336-1440). PBeam keeps the bytes of a packet that is not complete and adds each read to them, so the messages are the same. `overlapped_io` has an effect only on Windows (erl_sys_driver.h:56; erl_bif_port.c:1001-1002), and `eof` sets `ERTS_PORT_SFLG_SOFT_EOF` (erl_bif_port.c:995-996; io.c:7204-7209). | The first consumer, the `stty` path of the Terra TTY backend, opens a `spawn_executable` port with `binary`, `exit_status`, `nouse_stdio`, and `args`, waits for the exit status, and calls `port_info/1` and `port_close/1` (docs/terra-target.md). `posix_spawn` runs no Rust code in the child, and it returns a failed `execve` to the parent. A thread of the PBeam process reaps each program with `waitpid`, which fails with `ECHILD` when `SIGCHLD` is ignored. PBeam has one scheduler thread. (#302) A `spawn_executable` program whose last name component is `inet_gethost` runs in a thread of the node, not as an external program, so a bundle needs no `erts/bin/inet_gethost` file (#1110). The thread uses the host calls of the program (`gethostbyname`, `gethostbyaddr`, `getaddrinfo`, and `getnameinfo`; inet_gethost.c:1791-1976) and gives the same replies, but it skips the `access` check of the file. It runs the worker pool and the request queues of the program (`inet_gethost.c:449-501`, `727-1043`) with one thread for each worker. A cancel of a running request leaves the thread to end its lookup and drops its reply, where the program kills the worker process (`inet_gethost.c:876-891`, `1149-1180`). The program also ignores the stream settings `stderr_to_stdout` and `nouse_stdio`. It writes no warning or fatal message to fd 2, and `port_info/2` gives the pid of the node as `os_pid`. |
| tcp_inet ports | `open_port({spawn_driver, Name}, [binary])` opens a port of the `tcp_inet` driver for `Name` `"tcp_inet"`, `<<"tcp_inet">>`, or `tcp_inet`, and of the `udp_inet` driver for the same forms of `udp_inet` (row "udp_inet ports"); each other driver name and each other setting returns a runtime error. In a run with a policy, the open by a runtime process returns a runtime error, because no capability kind permits a network effect yet. A boot process opens the port, because the boot authority passes each check (row "Boot authority"): the kernel start opens a `udp_inet` port in `inet_config:set_hostname/0`. `erlang:port_control/3` runs the requests `OPEN`, `PEER`, `NAME`, `BIND`, `SETOPTS`, `GETOPTS`, `GETSTAT`, `SUBSCRIBE`, `ACCEPT`, `LISTEN`, and `TCP_REQ_RECV` of an IPv4 TCP socket with the reply bytes of `inet_drv.c`, for the options `reuseaddr`, `keepalive`, `linger`, `priority`, `tos`, `nodelay`, `active` with `false`, `true`, and `once`, `mode`, `send_timeout`, `delay_send`, `send_timeout_close`, `recvtos`, `ttl`, and `recvttl`. `ACCEPT` keeps the callers in one queue with a monitor and a timer for each, makes a port owned by and linked to the caller for each connection, and sends the `inet_async` messages of ERTS for a connection, a timeout, an error of `accept`, and the close of the listen port. On a connected socket in passive mode with `{packet, raw}`, `TCP_REQ_RECV` with a length and a timeout, and the output of `prim_inet:send/2` with its header `term_to_binary(Mref, [local])`, give the `inet_async` and `inet_reply` messages, the `ealready`, `enomem`, `timeout`, `closed`, and `enotconn` results, and the `GETSTAT` counts of `inet_drv.c`. In active mode, the port sends `{tcp, S, Data}` for each read to its owner, and `once` becomes `false` after one message; a `SETOPTS` that changes `false` to `once` reads the socket, and a change to `true` delivers the bytes of the input buffer, before the reply. At the end of file or a failed read or write, the owner gets `{tcp_error, S, Reason}` for a failed read and then `{tcp_closed, S}` once, and the port unlinks its owner and closes with the reason `normal` or the error name, as `driver_exit` does. The port writes the output without a wait and queues the bytes that the socket does not take; a send while the queue holds 8192 bytes or more gets its reply when the queue holds 4096 bytes or fewer. Each other request (for example `CONNECT`), option, family (`inet6`, `local`), protocol (`mptcp`), and bind address tag (`any`, `loopback`), `{active, N}`, `{recvttl, true}`, a `linger` time with `linger` off, a `linger` time on macOS, a negative receive length, an output header other than the bytes of `term_to_binary(Ref, [local])`, an output with `delay_send`, an output to a port whose send waits for its queue (ERTS suspends the caller of a busy port), a send that would wait with a `send_timeout` other than `infinity` and 0 return a runtime error, as does `erlang:port_command/2` on a `udp_inet` port. `SUBSCRIBE` gives the size of the queue, and when the queue holds bytes, the caller gets `{empty_out_q, S}` once when the queue becomes empty, so `gen_tcp:close/1` waits for the queue (`prim_inet.erl:175-275`; `inet_drv.c:10795-10848`). `erlang:port_close/1` and a link exit signal for a reason other than `kill` put a port with queued output in the `CLOSING` state after its link exit signals and `DOWN` messages: the port writes its queue, sends no message, and terminates and closes its socket when the queue is empty or the connection ends (`erts_deliver_port_exit` and `flush_port`, `io.c:3939-3948`; `erl_port.h:807-810`). In this state, `erlang:port_info/1,2` and `erlang:port_control/3` find the port, the other port BIFs give `badarg`, and `link/1` and `monitor/2` give `noproc`. A failure of the port, as at the end of the connection in active mode, and a close with `linger` `{true, 0}` drop the queue (`driver_failure_term`, `io.c:7191-7225`; `tcp_inet_flush`, `inet_drv.c:12708-12730`). `GETOPTS` gives the `getsockopt` value of `reuseaddr`, `keepalive`, and `nodelay` as ERTS does: the flag bit of the option on macOS (4, 8, and 4) and 1 on Linux when they are on. It gives `linger` as on or off with its seconds, and `recvttl` as off, because PBeam never sets `IP_RECVTTL`. Without `SO_PRIORITY`, as on macOS, `priority` is 0 and its `SETOPTS` changes nothing; on Linux, each `SETOPTS` sets `priority` and `tos` again after the option, as ERTS does. The socket of a listen port with a caller in its accept queue, and the socket of a connected port with a receive that waits or in active mode, are in the read set of the idle wait, and the socket of a port with queued output is in its write set (row "Input select"). Under `pbeam_boot`, `gen_tcp:listen/2` on the `pbeam` binary runs with the `inet` option as on ERTS (`runs_the_gen_tcp_calls_of_thousand_island_as_pinned_erts`, `tests/feature/gen_tcp.rs`). Without a family option, `inet:tcp_module/1` reads the table of the `inet_db` server, which the kernel start runs: `gen_tcp:listen(0, [binary, {active, false}])` gives a listen socket on `{0,0,0,0}`, and `inet:tcp_module([])` gives `{inet_tcp,[]}`, on the `pbeam` binary and on `erl -boot start_clean` (measured). | `inet_drv.c` runs each request of `prim_inet` (inet_drv.c:10988-12400). `GETOPTS` gives the value of `getsockopt`, which macOS gives as the flag bit of the option, for example 4 for `SO_REUSEADDR` and `TCP_NODELAY` (measured: `[0,0,0,0,4]` for `reuseaddr`) (inet_drv.c:9727-9750). Kernel start runs the `inet_db` server (inet_db.erl:114-125, 939-965). | The listen socket and the acceptors of Thousand Island need only these requests (docs/tcp-socket-design.md). `socket2` gives these options only as a boolean, so PBeam maps an option that is on to the value of the host kernel (xnu `sogetoptlock` and `tcp_ctloutput`; Linux `sk_reuse`, `SOCK_KEEPOPEN`, and `TCP_NAGLE_OFF`). `socket2` has no `IP_RECVTTL` call, it sets `linger` off without its time, and on macOS it sets `linger` with `SO_LINGER_SEC`, so a different value would change the socket in a different way. Task #669 adds the close with queued output. Thousand Island sets `{active, once}` (`handler.ex:583-599`), so PBeam does not run `{active, N}` and its `tcp_passive` message yet. (#665, #666, #667, #668) |
| udp_inet ports | A `udp_inet` port runs the requests of row "tcp_inet ports" for an IPv4 UDP socket, and also `GETHOSTNAME`, with the reply bytes of `inet_drv.c`: `OPEN` with the datagram type and the default or UDP protocol, `SETOPTS`, `GETOPTS`, `BIND`, `NAME`, `PEER`, `SUBSCRIBE`, and `GETHOSTNAME`. `LISTEN` replies `exbadport`. `PEER` replies `enotconn`, because PBeam does not connect a `udp_inet` socket. `GETHOSTNAME` gives the `uname` node name. `OPEN` of a stream socket with the UDP protocol gives the errno of the host. The stream type with the default protocol, the seqpacket type, the SCTP protocol, and each other request (for example `ACCEPT`, `CONNECT`, `SENDTO`, and `RECV`) return a runtime error, and `erlang:port_command/2` on the port also. PBeam reads no datagram of the socket. The idle wait includes the socket of an active port, and a datagram on that socket stops the run with a runtime error. | `packet_inet_ctl` runs `OPEN` and `LISTEN` and gives each other request to `inet_ctl` (inet_drv.c:14564-14892). `GETHOSTNAME` calls `gethostname` (inet_drv.c:11219-11233). `PEER` of a socket that is not connected replies `enotconn` (inet_drv.c:11272-11300). An active socket reads its fd (`sock_select(desc, FD_READ, 1)`, inet_drv.c:14619-14621) and gets each datagram as a message `{udp, Socket, Address, Port, Data}`. | `inet_config:set_hostname/0` needs only these requests at the kernel start (inet_config.erl:223-231). macOS and Linux give `gethostname` and the node name of `uname` from the same kernel value. (#697) |
| Dirty NIFs | A static NIF binding and an extension binding have a job: `Normal`, `CpuBound`, or `IoBound`. A `Normal` NIF runs on the scheduler thread. A static binding has the job of the flag of its pinned-OTP `ErlNifFunc`: each bound `prim_file` NIF, `prim_tty:write_nif/2`, and `prim_tty:read_nif/3` are `IoBound`, and each other static binding is `Normal`. The dirty `zstd` functions, the dictionary loads and makes (zstd_nif.c:1086-1109), have no binding, so their flags have no use. A `CpuBound` call runs on the dirty CPU pool, which has one thread for each scheduler of the run, named `erts_dcpus_N`. An `IoBound` call runs on the dirty IO pool, which has 10 threads, named `erts_dios_N`. The pools belong to the node, so the schedulers of a run of `execute_on_schedulers` share them. The first call of a pool starts its threads, and each pool has one run queue: a thread takes the call of a `max` caller first, then of a `high` caller, then of the queue that `normal` and `low` callers share, as the run queue of a scheduler does (row "Scheduling"). The priority is the priority of the caller at the call. A static binding checks its arguments on the scheduler thread and gives the work of the call to the pool; an extension binding runs fully on the pool thread (row "Extension libraries"). The caller switches out at the call and does not run Erlang code until the call completes. A message to it does not put it in the run queue. A signal to it puts it at the end of the run queue, and its turn handles its signals for it: it answers a `process_info/2` request, adds a link or a monitor, and handles an exit signal. The turn does not add reductions to the reduction total of the caller and uses at most `CONTEXT_REDS` reductions; a caller with signals left goes to the end of the run queue again. An exit signal that ends the caller drops the claim of the caller on the call. The caller then goes to the end of the run queue and exits in its next turn while the call runs, with its link and monitor signals. A thread does not run a call whose claim is gone, so the call of a caller that exits while its call waits in the queue does not run. A call that runs continues until its work ends, and the scheduler drops the completion of a process that exits or exited. Process identifiers and call identifiers are not used again in a run, so a late completion does not find another process or another call. While a thread runs the call, `process_info/1` gives the status `running`, and while the call waits in the queue, the status `runnable`. A dirty thread sends each completion to the inbox of the scheduler of the call and writes one byte to its non-blocking wake socket, which ends the wait of an idle scheduler (row "Input select"). Before each scheduling step, the process of each completion goes to the end of the run queue, and in its next turn it returns the result, or it raises the exception with the NIF, its arguments, and `[]` as the first entry of the stack trace. When the run ends, it drops the claims of all processes and does not wait for a call that runs. When the node ends, the threads take the calls in the queue without a run, and a thread stops when the queue is empty. After the run, a call that runs continues on its thread until its work ends; the `pbeam` binary then exits, which ends the thread. `prim_tty:read_nif/3` is `IoBound` but runs on the scheduler thread, and `setupterm_nif/0` runs on the scheduler thread (row "NIF stubs"). A `prim_file` binding reads its arguments and its file resource on the scheduler thread, runs its host calls on the dirty IO pool, and makes its result on the scheduler thread in the next turn of the caller. When the scheduler drops a result that did not reach its caller, because the caller exited or the run ended, a dirty IO thread drops it, so the file of such an open closes on a dirty IO thread; after the node ends, the current thread drops it. A file resource has the four states of `efile_state_t`. An operation on an idle resource makes it busy until the operation ends. An operation or a close on a resource that is not idle gives `{error, einval}`, and then the operation does not check its other arguments. The exit of the owner closes an idle resource at once and a busy resource at the end of its operation. `crypto:pbkdf2_hmac_nif/5` runs on the scheduler thread, also for more than 100 iterations or a key of more than 64 bytes. | A NIF with `ERL_NIF_DIRTY_JOB_IO_BOUND` (prim_tty_nif.c:161, 163; prim_file_nif.c:179-218, except `get_handle_nif/1` at 215) switches its caller out to the dirty IO run queue (`static_schedule_dirty_nif`, erl_nif.c:3437-3477; erl_process.c:10244-10262). ERTS starts one dirty CPU scheduler for each scheduler and 10 dirty IO schedulers (erl_init.c:865-867), named `erts_dcpus_N` and `erts_dios_N` (erl_process.c:8956-8971). The `flags` of an `ErlNifFunc` select the job (erl_nif.h:239-243). A dirty IO scheduler calls the NIF (`erts_call_dirty_nif`, erl_nif.c:426-543), and the caller then goes back to a normal run queue, where `dirty_nif_finalizer` returns the result and `dirty_nif_exception` raises the exception with the MFA and the arguments of the NIF (erl_nif.c:3386-3435). A process goes to the dirty run queue of its priority (`select_enqueue_run_queue`, erl_process.c:6630-6660), and a dirty scheduler picks a process as a normal scheduler does (erl_process.c:9993-10024, 4039-4052): with `erl +S 1 +SDcpu 1:1`, `dirty_nif_probe:priorities_with/4` gives `[m1,m2,h1,n1,n2,l1,l2]`. `erts_process_state2status` gives `running` to a process that runs dirty and `runnable` to a process in a dirty run queue (erl_process.c:9319-9328). While the caller runs dirty or waits in the dirty run queue, a new signal sends its pid to the process `erts_dirty_process_signal_handler` (erl_proc_sig_queue.h:2062-2089, erl_proc_sig_queue.c:979-1008), which handles the signals of the caller with `erts_internal:dirty_process_handle_signals/1` and its own reductions, at most 5 calls for each pid message (erts_dirty_process_signal_handler.erl:52-89, erl_proc_sig_queue.c:8892-8963). An exit signal that ends the caller puts it in a normal run queue (erl_process.c:13484-13548), and a normal scheduler runs its exit while the NIF runs (erl_process.c:10092-10110). When the NIF returns, `erts_call_dirty_nif` drops the result of an exiting process (erl_nif.c:507-542). A process that exits before a dirty scheduler takes it does not run the NIF (erl_process.c:10252-10258). Measured with `erl +S 1` (`fixtures/boot/dirty_exit_probe.erl`): while the write of a `prim_tty` writer waits for a full stdout pipe, `process_info(W, message_queue_len)` gives 0 and then 1 after a message, a link from another process is added, `exit(W, normal)` is ignored, `exit(W, kill)` gives `{'DOWN', _, process, W, killed}` at once, and the linked process exits with `killed`. With 10 writes that wait, the 11th writer waits in the dirty run queue; after `exit(W11, kill)`, its bytes never arrive. `halt()` ends the node with status 0 while the write waits. Measured with `erts_debug:dirty_cpu(wait, 300)` as the call of `dirty_nif_probe:signals_with/3` and `kill_with/3` (`fixtures/dirty_nif/dirty_nif_probe.erl`): another process runs, the queue length is 0 and then 1, the status is `running`, `exit(C, normal)` is ignored, the result is `ok`; a kill gives `killed` to a monitor and to a linked process at once, and no late message comes. With `dirty_cpu_schedulers_online` 1, a second call that waits has the status `runnable`. `erts_debug:dirty_io(foo, bar)` raises `badarg` with `{erts_debug,dirty_io,[foo,bar],[]}` first. The ERTS poll set also holds the read end of the wake pipe (erl_poll.c:489-525). `halt` does not wait for a dirty NIF of a library without `ERTS_MOD_NIF_FLG_DELAY_HALT` (erl_nif.c:470-497). `file_handle_wrapper` changes the state of a resource from idle to busy and back, gives `{error, einval}` for each other state before the operation reads its arguments, and closes a resource whose owner exited during the operation (prim_file_nif.c:315-357, 377-424, prim_file_nif.h:73-78). `close_nif` closes only an idle resource (prim_file_nif.c:618-647). `create_ref_or_error_tuple` closes the file of an open whose caller exited on the dirty scheduler (prim_file_nif.c:547-553). `pbkdf2_hmac_nif` has no flag, but it schedules a dirty CPU call for more than 100 iterations or a key of more than 64 bytes (pbkdf2_hmac.c:86-96). Measured with `prim_file_dirty_probe:fifo/1` (`fixtures/prim_file_dirty/prim_file_dirty_probe.erl`): a reader that waits in `prim_file:read_file/1` for a writer of a FIFO has the status `running`, and its caller then opens the FIFO for write; the result is `{running,{ok,<<"dirty">>}}`. | The first consumer is the `prim_tty` writer, which waits only for its write, so the other processes run while stdout is full. `prim_tty:read_nif/3` runs only after a select message, so its `read` returns at once. The dirty CPU call of `pbkdf2_hmac_nif/5` waits for a consumer. PBeam terms and resources of a scheduler stay on its thread, so a static binding reads its resources before the switch out; ERTS checks the arguments on the dirty scheduler, and the result is the same. Each scheduler keeps its own completion inbox, so a completion goes to the thread that owns the caller. A dirty call holds both ends of the wake socket, so a completion after the run does not write to a closed socket. PBeam has no `erts_dirty_process_signal_handler` process: the turn of the caller does its work, so the order of the handling among the other processes and the reductions of the handling can differ. A program that calls `BeamProgram::execute` and continues after the run can see a call that runs end after the run. `read_nif/3` runs only after a select message, when a `read` returns at once (row "Input select"). (#19, #20, #847, #869) |
| OS signals | At the start, the `pbeam` binary sets `SIGFPE` to `SIG_IGN` and sets a handler for `SIGINT`, `SIGQUIT`, `SIGTERM`, and `SIGUSR1`, as ERTS does. It keeps `SIG_DFL` for `SIGCHLD`: a thread of the PBeam process reaps each port program, and with `SIG_IGN` the host reaps the program first, so the exit status of the port is lost (row "Ports"). `SIGINT` writes the ERTS break menu and reads one key of stdin as `do_break` does (`break.c:571-687`): `a`, `q`, and `*` abort the run with status 0, `c` and `m` continue it, and the end of the input aborts it. The crash dump of `A` and each information command give a runtime error that names the command. Thus a process that the PBeam process does not reap stays a zombie until the PBeam process exits. `os:set_signal/2` sets the host disposition of a signal to `SIG_IGN` for `ignore`, to `SIG_DFL` for `default`, and to the handler for `handle`, as ERTS does. The handler writes the signal number to a notify pipe. Before each scheduling step, the scheduler reads a flag that the handler sets, and it sends `{notify, Signal}` to the process registered as `erl_signal_server` for each signal in the pipe, in arrival order. When no process can run, the scheduler also waits for the pipe, so a signal wakes it. Thus a signal reaches `erl_signal_server` at the next scheduling step, not at once. The default `erl_signal_handler` runs as on ERTS: `sigquit` halts with status 0, and `sigterm` writes the info report and stops the node through `init:stop/0` with status 0 (see "Boot"). `sigusr1` calls `erlang:halt/1` with a slogan, which writes the slogan and stops the run with status 1 without a crash dump (see "Run end"). The write end of the pipe is non-blocking, so a signal that arrives when the pipe is full is dropped. The `badarg` of a mode that is not valid has the `error_info` cause `badopt` (`erl_bif_os.c:149-150`). | `os:set_signal/2` raises `badarg` with the `error_info` cause `badopt` for a mode that is not `ignore`, `default`, or `handle`, then `badarg` for a signal that is not valid (erl_bif_os.c:145-160). At emulator start, ERTS ignores `SIGFPE` (sys_float.c:31-37), installs handlers for `SIGINT` and `SIGQUIT` (sys.c:799-803), and installs handlers for `SIGTERM` and `SIGUSR1` and ignores `SIGCHLD`, so that an orphan process does not stay a zombie when the BEAM process is pid 1 (sys.c:812-823). `erl_child_setup`, a separate process, reaps the port programs (erl_child_setup.c:396-420). Each handler writes the signal number to a blocking pipe (sys.c:626-629, 749-752, 1105-1120). The signal dispatcher thread reads the pipe and, for each signal, sends `{notify, Signal}` to `erl_signal_server` at once, or runs the break handler for `SIGINT` (sys.c:589-607, 1122-1180). The break handler writes the break menu and reads a key from fd 0 (break.c:571-687); at the end of file of fd 0 it exits with status 0. `erl_signal_handler` halts for `sigquit`, calls `erlang:halt("Received SIGUSR1")` for `sigusr1`, and calls `init:stop/0` for `sigterm` (erl_signal_handler.erl:39-48). | PBeam has one thread, so the scheduler reads the pipe at its scheduling steps; a flag makes the check free of a system call when no signal arrived. A handler that waits for a full pipe that the same thread reads would wait without end. The crash dump is not implemented; the backlog entry "Run the crash dump of `SIGUSR1`" records it. PBeam does not model the `error_info` of an exception. PBeam has no separate reaper process for the port programs (#302). (#115, #301, #302) |
| OS environment | `os:getenv/1` and `os:env/0` read the copy of the environment of the run, which the first read makes from `environ`. `os:putenv/2` and `os:unsetenv/1` change that copy. As in ERTS, a key that is an atom, a binary (also inside a list), or an improper list raises `badarg`. A key that has the character 0, the character `=`, or a code point that the native encoding cannot hold also raises `badarg`. It converts the key, finds the entry, and converts the value with the ERTS rules: each entry splits at its first `=`, an entry without `=` is skipped, and the last entry of a key wins. On macOS, a value that is valid UTF-8 and has one of the 61 ERTS composition candidates returns the runtime error `unsupported composition of the native name code point N on macOS`. `prim_file:internal_native2name/1` gives the same runtime error for such a file name. It gives `{error, warning}` for bytes that are not valid UTF-8 in a UTF-8 encoding, because PBeam has no `+fn` emulator flag and `warning` is the ERTS default. For example, `ae\xcc\x81` returns this error for the code point 769. PBeam has no `erl` script and no `erlexec`. Thus `os:getenv/1` gives the host value or `false` for `ROOTDIR`, `BINDIR`, `EMU`, and `PROGNAME`, and `PATH` has no `BINDIR` prefix. | ERTS copies `environ` at emulator start (`sys_env.c:40-46`, `134-154`; `erl_osenv.c:360-385`), and `os:getenv/1` reads the copy (`erl_bif_os.c:94-110`). On macOS, the file name encoding is `ERL_FILENAME_UTF8_MAC` (`erl_sys_common_misc.c:120-124`), and ERTS composes the code points of a value with `compose_tab` (`erl_unicode.c:1418-1428`, `1569-1630`; `erl_unicode_normalize.h:1523-1690`). The composition is not Unicode NFC. Measured on ERTS 29.1.1: `ae\xcc\x81` gives `[97,233]`, `x\xcc\x81` gives `[120,769]`, and Hangul jamo stay decomposed. `prim_file_internal_native2name_1` composes in the same way, and for bytes that are not valid UTF-8 it gives `{error, Type}` with the type of the `+fn` flag, `warning` by default (`erl_unicode.c:2735-2824`). Measured on ERTS 29.1.1: `prim_file:internal_native2name(<<"ae",204,129>>)` gives `[97,233]`, and `<<255>>` gives `{error,warning}`. The `erl` script exports `EMU`, `ROOTDIR`, `BINDIR`, and `PROGNAME` (`erl.src.src:52-58`), and `erlexec` puts `BINDIR` and `ROOTDIR/bin` at the front of `PATH` (`erlexec.c:553-621`) before it starts the emulator. Measured on ERTS 29.1.1 with only `PATH` and `HOME` set: `ROOTDIR` and `BINDIR` give the install paths, `EMU` gives `"beam"`, `PROGNAME` gives `"erl"`, and `PATH` starts with `BINDIR`. | PBeam changes its own copy of the environment and not the `environ` array of the OS process, as ERTS on Unix does (`erts_sys_explicit_8bit_putenv`, `sys_env.c:66-80`). A child of a port starts with the `environ` array of the process, so it does not see a change of `os:putenv/2`. The `pbeam` binary starts without the `erl` script and `erlexec` (see "Boot"). The composition needs the 1040 entries of `compose_tab`. The start path reads only `OTP_TEST_FEATURES` (`erl_features.erl:466-472`), which is absent or ASCII. The OTP code path has only ASCII names. (#185, #443) |
| Terminal mode at exit | At the start of each run, `BeamProgram::execute` reads the mode of fd 0 when fd 0 is a terminal. At the end of the run, also after a runtime error or `erlang:halt/2`, it sets fd 0 to that mode when fds 0 and 1 are terminals, and ignores the result. When fd 0 has a read select, it then checks fd 0 once with a `select` of timeout 0, so a reset that turns `ICANON` on does not leave `PENDIN` set on macOS. PBeam does not set the mode when a signal or a Rust panic ends the OS process. When no `prim_tty:tty_select/2` ran in the run, PBeam also sets the mode at the run end, and it does not change the `O_NONBLOCK` flag of fd 0. A change of the mode to `ICANON` during the run, for example at the exit of a TTY owner, leaves `PENDIN` set on macOS until the scheduler next waits for fd 0. Each TTY resource stays until the end of the run, so its monitors stay too. | `erl_sys_init` reads the mode of fd 0 when fd 0 is a terminal (sys.c:428-432). At each emulator exit, also at `halt/0,1` and at the `init` crash halt, `erts_exit_vv` calls `sys_tty_reset` (erl_init.c:2730), which sets fd 0 to that mode with `tcsetattr` when fds 0 and 1 are terminals, but only after `prim_tty:tty_select/2` sets `using_oldshell` to 0 (prim_tty_nif.c:1224) or after `+Bc` replaces the interrupt character (sys.c:783-796). Else it only clears `O_NONBLOCK` of fd 0 (sys.c:182-190). Independently, when fds 0 and 1 are terminals at the start, the emulator sends the mode to `erl_child_setup` (sys_drivers.c:1670-1690), which sets fd 0 to it when the emulator closes its socket, also after `SIGKILL` (erl_child_setup.c:563-576). On macOS, the `kqueue` pollset of ERTS does not take fds 0, 1, and 2 and returns `ERTS_POLL_EV_NVAL` before it calls `kevent` (erl_poll.c:886-897), so ERTS keeps fd 0 with a read select in the fallback `select` set of its poll thread (erl_check_io.c:521-527; `check_io_debug` shows `fd=0 type=chr ... flags=FLBK`). A change of the mode to `ICANON` sets `PENDIN` in the macOS kernel and wakes each `select` of the terminal. The `select` then reads the input count with `ttnread`, which clears `PENDIN` (XNU `bsd/kern/tty.c`: `ttioctl_locked`, `ttyselect`, `ttnread`). The emulator calls `exit` at once after `sys_tty_reset` (erl_init.c:2730-2742), so `PENDIN` stays when the poll thread does not run between the two. Measured in a pseudo-terminal with `stty -g`, after `stty raw -echo` during the run: `main/0`, `halt/0`, and `crash/0` of `tty_restore_probe` end with the first mode plus `PENDIN` (`lflag` `200005cb` against `5cb`) in 5 of 110 runs, and in 63 of 150 runs with 10 CPU-bound processes on 10 cores. With `+S 1`, the result is the same (8 of 80 runs under load). With a `kqueue` registration of the terminal in the test process, which clears `PENDIN` at once, `PENDIN` stays in 0 of 40 runs under load. After `stty raw -echo icanon`, the reset does not turn `ICANON` on, and all runs end with the first mode (0 of 120 runs with `PENDIN` under load). With stdout a pipe, the raw mode stays. ERTS frees a TTY resource and its monitors when no term refers to it. | PBeam has no child setup process, no emulator signal handling, and no garbage collection of resources. A panic is a PBeam defect. The `pbeam_boot` run always starts the `prim_tty` reader of `user_drv`, which calls `tty_select/2`, and PBeam never sets `O_NONBLOCK` on fd 0. PBeam has no poll thread (see "Input select"). The select after the reset gives the end state of the ERTS run in which the poll thread runs before the exit, the result of most runs on a host without load. `halt/0` ends the run with the halt status, and the reset still runs (see "Run end"). The test `tty_restore` (`tests/feature/tty_restore.rs`) compares both runtimes after a raw mode that keeps `ICANON` on, and checks the `PENDIN` end state only on PBeam, because the ERTS result depends on the load of the host. (#115, #300, #329) |
| Run end | The run ends at the next scheduling step after the exit of the root process completes, also when another process can run or a timer is active. When the exit switches out at the end of the budget (row "Scheduling"), other processes run until the next turn of the exit, and the run ends after the exit sends its link and monitor signals and runs its TTY monitors. A root process that an exit signal ends exits when it handles the signal (row "Signal queue"). Then no other process runs. The `pbeam` binary exits with status 0 when the root process returns. A root that exits with the reason `normal` does not end the run, as on ERTS: other processes keep running until a halt or a host signal ends the run (row "OS signals"), and when no process can wake, the run ends with the error `root process exited with normal`. For each other root exit, also `kill` from another process, it writes `pbeam: root process exited with <Reason>` to stderr and exits with status 1. `<Reason>` is the `%T` text of `print_term` (erl_printf_term.c:352-811) with no length limit, and each byte of the text is one code point, as `erlang:halt/1` writes it. Thus `<Reason>` is the text in the parentheses of the ERTS line `Runtime terminating during boot (...)` when the halt text `Runtime terminating during boot (<Reason>)` is at most 1023 characters, that is, when `<Reason>` is at most 989 characters, and when `<Reason>` holds no pid, no reference, and no stack frame after the first frame (rows "Process and reference counters" and "Exception stack trace"). PBeam does not write the `Error! Failed to eval` line or the `Runtime terminating during boot` prefix. `erlang:halt/2` with a non-negative integer status stops the run at once, and the `pbeam` binary exits with the low 8 bits of the status code masked to `INT_MAX` and writes nothing. A flush with a flush timeout of 0 gives the status 255, as in ERTS. `erlang:halt/2` with `abort` gives a runtime error. With a crash dump slogan, it writes the UTF-8 bytes of the slogan and a newline to stderr, with `\r\n` for each `\n`, and stops the run with status 1 (bif.c:4372-4386, erl_init.c:2712-2713, 2738-2739, sys/unix/sys.c:343). PBeam writes no crash dump and no `Crash dump is being written` line, as ERTS does with `ERL_CRASH_DUMP_SECONDS=0`. When the root process waits and no process can run, no timer is active, fd 0 has no read select, no dirty IO call runs, and no signal handler is installed, the run ends with the runtime error `root process waits for a message that cannot arrive`. A run of `execute_on_schedulers` decides for the whole node: the last scheduler that becomes idle gives the error when each scheduler has no process that can run, no timer, no dirty call, no port that waits, no read select of fd 0, and no signal handler, and no delivery waits in the inbox of a scheduler. The decision depends on a property of PBeam: only a scheduler thread puts a delivery into the inbox of a scheduler, before it becomes idle, and a dirty call completes to the scheduler that started it. A process that a steal moves is such a delivery: the giver runs when it puts the process into the inbox of the hungry scheduler (row "Work stealing of a run with scheduler threads"). The `pbeam` binary installs the start handlers (row "OS signals"), so such a run waits for a signal, as on ERTS. | `halt()` after `-eval` stops the node with status 0 (erlang.erl:3357-3358), also while other processes wait for timers. `halt_2` checks the options first, then stops the node with the status masked to `INT_MAX` for a non-negative integer, aborts with a core dump for `abort`, and writes a crash dump with the slogan for a list (bif.c:4309-4398, big.c:3545-3565). `term_to_Uint32` truncates a flush timeout to 32 bits (big.c:3648-3663). While a halt flushes, no process runs (erl_process.c:15220-15255). A flush timeout that ends before the flush exits with status 255 (erl_process.c:15244-15248, erl_init.c:75, 2771-2774); a flush timeout of 0 gives 255 (measured 5 of 5). `elixir -e` runs the commands, then runs the `at_exit` callbacks, then calls `Logger.flush()` when the module `Logger` is loaded, and then calls `System.halt(status)` (kernel/cli.ex:58-71), which calls `:erlang.halt(status)` (system.ex:850-851). The default logger handler is async, so `erl -noshell -eval 'logger:warning("x"), halt().'` writes nothing, but `elixir -e 'require Logger; Logger.warning("x")'` writes the event (measured). When the `-eval` expression raises, `start_it/1` writes `Error! Failed to eval: <expression>\r\n\r\n` to stderr with `erlang:display_string/1` and raises again (init.erl:1552-1567). At a boot process exit that is not `normal`, `init` calls `crash("Runtime terminating during boot", [Reason])` (init.erl:710-712), which writes `Runtime terminating during boot (<reason>)\r\n` to stderr and halts with status 1 (init.erl:678-679). `crash/2` writes each item with `erts_internal:term_to_string(X, 32768)` (init.erl:665-672), which prints the term with `%T` and returns the bytes of the text as a list of integers (bif.c:4156-4190). `erlang:halt/2` with a string writes each list element as one Unicode code point, keeps at most 1023 characters, and exits with status 1 (bif.c:4372-4387, erl_init.c:2708-2742). Measured: the atom `'Ä'` gives the bytes `C3 83 C2 84`, the list `[160,255]` gives `"` `C2 A0 C3 BF` `"`, a reason of 40000 integers gives 1023 characters of halt text, and a string reason of 987 code points (989 characters of `<Reason>`) keeps the closing `)`, but one of 988 code points loses it. A kill of the boot process gives the same path without the `Error!` line. Without `halt()`, the boot process exits `normal`, `init` goes to its main loop (init.erl:700-704), and the node does not stop. `exit(normal)` in `-eval` gives the same result after the `Error!` line. Unless `ERL_CRASH_DUMP_SECONDS=0` is set, ERTS writes `erl_crash.dump` at the crash. | The `pbeam` binary runs one entry, as the reference commands run `-eval` before `halt()`. PBeam writes no core dump and no crash dump. Without the boot, no `init` process exists, and with `pbeam_boot` no `init` process watches the boot process. Thus the `pbeam` binary writes the reason in its own line, with the ERTS term text. A command-line user needs the full reason, so PBeam has no 1023-character limit. The form `pbeam erl` runs the pinned `init`, which writes the `Error!` line and the `init` halt text as ERTS does (row "Boot"). With `pbeam_boot` (see "Boot"), `init:stop/0` sends `{stop, stop}` to the `init` request process and returns `ok` (init.erl:536), and the `init` request process shuts down the kernel processes and halts with status 0. When the entry returns and the table `elixir_config` exists, that is, when the elixir application runs, the PBeam boot calls `'Elixir.Kernel.CLI':run/1` with a callback that does nothing, as `elixir -e` does after its commands: it runs the `at_exit` callbacks, calls `Logger.flush()` when the module `Logger` is loaded, and calls `System.halt(Status)` (kernel/cli.ex:58-71, 129-133). Thus a log event in the queue of the async default handler is written, as with `elixir -e` (`tests/feature/at_exit.rs`). Without the elixir application, the root process returns or raises as before, and a log event that is still in the queue at the root exit is lost, as with `halt()` after `-eval`. An exception of the entry goes through `run/1` with a callback that raises it again, so `exec_fun/2` writes the Elixir error and the status is 1, N for `exit({shutdown, N})`, or 0 for `exit(normal)` and `exit(shutdown)` (kernel/cli.ex:135-173). The error text holds no `erl_eval` and `Code` frames, because the PBeam boot calls the entry directly. The fixture `fixtures/io_fail` calls `logger_std_h:filesync(default)` before it writes its result, so the report of `user_drv` is written before the root exit on PBeam and before `halt()` on ERTS. The file control process replies to the call after it writes the report (logger_h_common.erl:262-266; logger_std_h.erl:601-623). Without the call, PBeam writes only `Failed to write log message to stdout, trying stderr` and the result (measured). (#169, #158) |
| Error report | An uncaught `error`, or an uncaught `throw` as `{nocatch, Reason}`, sends `{log, error, "Error in process ~p with exit value:~n~p~n", [Pid, {Reason, Stacktrace}], Metadata}` to the system logger; class `exit` sends nothing. The format never has `on node ~p`, because PBeam is never alive. PBeam sends the message at once when the exit starts. A root process sends no report, because it stands for the `-eval` process of `erl`, whose exception `init:start_it/1` catches (init.erl:1552-1567). With no process registered as `logger`, or when the sender is the registered `logger`, PBeam writes `(no logger present) unexpected logger message: ...` to stderr. A sender that is the system logger pid counts as a pid that does not exist, so `logger` becomes the system logger and takes the report. | ERTS sends the same message (beam_common.c:696-718, utils.c:770-858) and adds `on node ~p` when the node is alive. It queues the message to its system message dispatcher thread (erl_trace.c:2494-2497, 2780-2824). The dispatcher runs after the exit of a sender that is the registered `logger` removed its name, so that report also goes to stderr (erl_trace.c:2564-2605; measured). When the dispatcher runs before the exit marks the sender as exiting, the name lookup still finds the sender (register.c:356), and ERTS drops that report without a word (erl_trace.c:2817-2818). For the same reason, a sender that is the system logger pid counts as a pid that does not exist, and `logger` becomes the system logger again (erl_trace.c:2789-2795; measured). When the dispatcher runs before that sender exits, ERTS queues the report to the sender, and no other process gets it (erl_trace.c:2788-2790, 2803-2806). | PBeam has no distribution and no dispatcher thread. The report text is the same under `pbeam_boot` and `erl -noshell` (`tests/feature/crash_report.rs`). (#1023) |
| Initial group leader | `BeamProgram::execute` and the `pbeam` binary do not boot. The first process of a run is its own group leader, as the ERTS `init` process is, and no `user` process exists. When the `pbeam` binary runs `pbeam_boot:start(Module, Function)` of `examples/hello_world` (see "Boot"), the entry process has the registered `user` process as its group leader, as in ERTS. | A process started by `erl -eval` has the registered `user` process as its group leader (init.erl:1539-1546). | `BeamProgram::execute` runs fixtures that do not load the boot modules. The runtime library holds no program, so the boot is a BEAM module of the Hello World example (#139). (#72, #139) |
| Boot | The form `pbeam MODULE FUNCTION` of the `pbeam` binary has no built-in boot; the form `pbeam erl` runs the pinned `init` with `BeamProgram::boot` (rows "Node boot" and "Release boot"). The module `pbeam_boot` of `examples/hello_world` does the boot. It first starts the pinned `erts_code_purger`, which registers itself, as ERTS starts it as a system process next to `init` (`erl_init.c:2550-2557`). The start set of the boot has `erts_code_purger.beam` from the ebin directory of erts. The `pbeam` binary loads it with the other BEAM files and runs `pbeam_boot:start(Module, Function)` as the root process, or `pbeam_boot:start(Module, Function, Flags)` when it gets `--root` or `--pa` (row "Run arguments"). `Flags` has `{root, [Root]}` for `--root`, then `{bindir, [Root ++ "/erts-17.1/bin"]}`, as the `erl` script sets `BINDIR` to `$ROOTDIR/erts-%VSN%/bin` (`erl.src.src:52`, `erts/vsn.mk:23`) and `erlexec` gives `-root` and `-bindir` first (`erlexec.c:1152-1155`), one `{pa, [Dir]}` for each `--pa DIR` in the command line order, and `{mode, [Mode]}` for `--mode`, with each value as a binary of its bytes. The root process traps exits and registers an `init` request process with the flags `[{noshell, []} | Flags]`. It then needs the root flag, as `init` does: without `--root`, the root process exits with `no_or_multiple_root_variables`, so the `pbeam` binary writes `pbeam: root process exited with no_or_multiple_root_variables` and exits with status 1. It then starts the pinned `erl_prim_loader` with `erl_prim_loader:start/0`, sets its path to `Pa ++ Pz ++ ["."]`, with the last `pa` flag first, and gives its link to `init`. The loader gets `error` for `init:get_argument(loader)`, so it uses `efile`. It checks `prim_file:get_cwd/0` and puts the persistent term `erl_prim_loader` with the value `{efile, undefined}`. PBeam does not read a boot script file: `pbeam_boot` runs the commands of the pinned `start_clean.boot` in their order. It runs the last `path` command: it sets the path to `Pa ++ [Root ++ "/lib/kernel-11.0.4/ebin", Root ++ "/lib/stdlib-8.1/ebin"] ++ Pz`. `erl_prim_loader:get_file/1` and `erl_prim_loader:read_file/1` give the ERTS results for the files of the `pa` directories and of the current directory. `read_file/1` of an atom raises `error:function_clause` from the `func_info` instruction of `erl_prim_loader:archive_split/3`, as ERTS does (row "Exception raise sites"). The loader waits in `receive` with an idle timeout of 6 minutes, so a timer is always active, and a root process that waits for a message that cannot arrive waits as on ERTS (row "Run end"). It runs `logger_server:start_link/0` and then `application_controller:start/1` with the kernel application spec of the pinned `start_clean.boot`, and gives the link of each process to `init`. It then runs `application:load/1` with the `stdlib` application spec, `application:start_boot(kernel, permanent)`, `application:start_boot(stdlib, permanent)`, and `c:erlangrc/0`. It does not run `heart`, and it loads no module with `primLoad`: the `pbeam` binary loads the start BEAM files, and in interactive mode `error_handler` and `code_server` load each other module on demand. In embedded mode, the start BEAM files must hold each module of the kernel start. It starts no kernel process by hand: `kernel:start/2` starts `kernel_sup` and its children, as `kernel.app` and the `kernel` environment give them. `application:which_applications/0` and the id, type, and modules of each child of `supervisor:which_children(kernel_sup)` are the same as on the pinned ERTS with `-boot start_clean` (fixture `kernel_app_probe`). The `application_master` of `kernel` is the group leader of the `kernel_sup` processes. On Linux, the `inet_gethost_native` process that the `inet_db` start spawns reads the `bindir` flag (inet_gethost_native.erl:416) and opens the pinned `inet_gethost` port program from the directory with `overlapped_io`, `{args, Args}`, `{packet, 4}`, `eof`, and `binary` (inet_gethost_native.erl:412-421), as ERTS does (row "Ports"). The flags have no `progname` and no `home`, so `c:erlangrc/0` gives `{error, enoent}` and reads no file. The boot then sets the registered `user` process as its group leader, calls `pbeam_cap:serve/2` for `user` and `standard_error` and `pbeam_cap:enter_runtime(Module, Function)` (row "Boot authority"), and calls the entry. The `init` request process traps exits. It answers only the start of a kernel process, `get_arguments`, `{get_argument, Arg}`, `fetch_loaded` (with the modules that `{ensure_loaded, Module}` loaded, row "Code loading"), `{ensure_loaded, Module}`, and the `{stop, stop}` of `init:stop/0`. It gives each binary flag value of `get_arguments` and `{get_argument, Arg}` as a string, as `init` does. For `{stop, stop}`, it sends `{'EXIT', Boot, shutdown}` to each kernel process except `logger`, the last started first, and waits for its exit, as `init` does: `application_controller` stops `stdlib` and `kernel`, so `kernel_sup` shuts down its children. While it waits, it answers the requests above and ignores another stop request. It has no shutdown timer, as `init` without `-shutdown_time`. It then calls `halt()`, so the run ends with status 0. The halt stops `logger` and each other process, in place of the kill of `init`. Other requests (for example `get_plain_arguments` and `get_status`) and other messages get no answer and stay in its mailbox. A start that does not give `{ok, Pid}` gets no answer, so the boot waits. When a kernel process exits, the `init` request process runs `init:terminate/3`: after 500 ms, it halts with the halt text `Kernel pid terminated (Name) (Reason)`, which `erts_internal:term_to_string/2` makes, and status 1, as ERTS does without the crash dump (row "Run end"). Under `pbeam erl -noshell -eval`, an expression that fails writes `Error! Failed to eval: <Expression>` and `Runtime terminating during boot (<Reason>)` to stderr and exits with status 1, as ERTS does (init.erl:665-679, 710-712, 1552-1567). The `init` request process, `erl_prim_loader`, `logger`, and `application_controller` start before the root process sets its group leader. Thus they have the root process as their group leader. `ac_tab` holds the `kernel` environment, so `application:get_env(kernel, logger_level)` gives `{ok, notice}`. `logger:internal_init_logger/0` sets the primary level `notice`, and `logger:add_handlers(kernel)` replaces the `simple` handler with the `default` handler `logger_std_h`. The run ends when the root process exits (see "Run end"). In a locale whose codeset is not UTF-8, `prim_tty` writes in latin1 mode, and `prim_tty:to_latin1/2` runs `prim_tty:is_usascii/1` on each output binary (see "Binary matching"). ASCII output gives the ERTS output and status 0 (measured with `LC_ALL=C`, and with `TERM=dumb` and `LC_ALL`, `LANG`, and `LC_CTYPE` unset). Output that is not ASCII runs `binary_to_latin1/2`, which builds each character with `char_to_latin1/2` (prim_tty.erl:1323-1330). PBeam runs the `integer` segment of `bs_create_bin` that `char_to_latin1/2` uses for a character below 512 (#397). Thus the output of `é` to a pipe is the ERTS byte 233 with status 0. PBeam runs `erlang:integer_to_binary/2`, which `char_to_latin1/2` uses for a character of 512 or more, and for a character of 128 or more when its second argument is `true` (prim_tty.erl:1325-1328) (#399). Thus `io:put_chars/1` of the UTF-8 binary of `中`, with `LC_ALL=C` and `TERM=dumb`, to a pipe writes `\x{4E2D}` with status 0, as ERTS does (measured). `pbeam_boot` does not read the boot script, `sys.config`, or `vm.args` of a release. A Mix release example, such as `examples/plug_hello`, boots with its boot script through `pbeam erl` (row "Release boot", #855). | The `init` boot process runs the boot script (init.erl:1192-1217). Before the script, it starts `erl_prim_loader` with the path `Pa ++ Pz ++ ["."]` and gives its link to `init` (init.erl:1164-1184, 1196-1202, 1259-1262). The flags of `init` come from `init:parse_boot_args/1`, with each value a binary of the bytes of the argument (init.erl:1668-1709; erl_init.c:373-384). `init:get_arguments/0` and `init:get_argument/1` give each binary as a string (init.erl:1799-1819). `erl_prim_loader:start_it/1` registers the name, reads the `loader` flag, checks `prim_file:get_cwd/0`, and puts the persistent term `erl_prim_loader` (erl_prim_loader.erl:163-221). The loader waits in `receive` with the idle timeout of 6 minutes (erl_prim_loader.erl:117, 479-506). The `path` command of the boot script then sets the path to `Pa ++ BootPath ++ Pz`, with the `lib` directories of the boot script as `BootPath` (init.erl:1323-1332). Measured: `erl -noshell -pa d1 -pa d2` gives `{ok,["d2","d1",Kernel,Stdlib,Sasl]}` for `erl_prim_loader:get_path/0`, with the `ebin` directories of `kernel`, `stdlib`, and `sasl` of the pinned install. `erl_prim_loader:get_file/1` of a name without `/` reads the first file of the path that exists (erl_prim_loader.erl:595-627), and `read_file/1` reads the name from the current directory (erl_prim_loader.erl:298-299). Inside the loader, `?SAFE2` catches the `function_clause` of a bad `get_file/1` argument, so the call gives `error`. In the caller, `read_file/1` of an atom raises `error:function_clause` (erl_prim_loader.erl:413-422, 1538-1548). `start_clean.boot` runs the kernel processes `heart`, `logger`, and `application_controller` in the boot process (init.erl:1348-1350). `start_in_kernel/5` sends each start result to `init`, which links to the pid and replies (init.erl:1519-1528, 768-772). Without `-heart`, `heart:start/0` gives `ignore` (heart.erl:157-175). `init` gives `{Init, ignore}` for `ignore`, and halts the node with `Could not start kernel pid` for another result (init.erl:773-779). When a kernel process exits, `init` halts the node with `Kernel pid terminated` (init.erl:785-788, 1144-1145). The flags of `init` also have `root`, `bindir`, `progname`, and `home`. `init` answers `get_arguments`, `{get_argument, Arg}`, and the other requests of `do_handle_msg/2` (init.erl:804-854, 1799-1819). It sends another `io_request` to `user`, and logs another message as `init got unexpected` at the level `info` (init.erl:855-876). `application_controller:start/1` spawns and links the controller (application_controller.erl:198-202). The controller reads the flags with `init:get_arguments/0` and `init:get_argument/1` (application_controller.erl:535, 1362, 1681-1686, 1964-1968) and makes `ac_tab` (application_controller.erl:544-545). The boot then loads `stdlib` and runs `application:start_boot/2` for `kernel` and `stdlib`, and `c:erlangrc/0`. `kernel:start/2` runs `logger:internal_init_logger/0` and then `os:internal_init_cmd_shell/0` (kernel.erl:37-38). `os:internal_init_cmd_shell/0` reads the file info of `/bin/sh` with `file:read_file_info/2` and puts the shell in the persistent term `kernel_os_cmd_shell`, which only `os:cmd/1,2` reads (os.erl:602, 630-669). Without a `-master` flag, `kernel_sup` starts `standard_error`, `file_server_2`, `erl_signal_server`, `user_sup`, and `logger_sup` in this order (kernel.erl:117-168, 188-194, 222-225). `file_server:start_link/0` reads `init:get_argument(master)` and registers `file_server_2` (file_server.erl:61, 266-269). `kernel:start/2` then runs `erl_signal_handler:start/0` and `logger:add_handlers(kernel)` (kernel.erl:41-42). `logger:add_handler/3` removes the `simple` handler after it adds `default` (logger.erl:838-848). `logger:internal_init_logger/0` sets the primary level from the `env` `logger_level` `notice` of the kernel spec (logger.erl:1572-1576, 1748-1754). `kernel_sup` is `one_for_all` with intensity 0 (kernel.erl:92-94). When a child exits, `kernel_sup` writes a `child_terminated` report and a `reached_max_restart_intensity` report and stops (supervisor.erl:1287-1293, 1426-1427, 1472-1490). Then `application_controller` stops the node with `Kernel pid terminated` (application_controller.erl:1251). The `application_master` of `kernel` makes itself the group leader before it starts `kernel_sup` (application_master.erl:121-134). Thus `standard_error`, `erl_signal_server`, `user_drv`, and `user` have the `application_master` as their group leader (measured: `<0.46.0>`, initial call `{application_master,init,3}`). `user_sup:init([])` reads the flags with `init:get_arguments/0` (user_sup.erl:46-47). `code:start_link/0` runs `code:load_code_server_prerequisites/0` before it starts the code server, and that function runs `erl_features:enabled/0` (code.erl:1682-1684, 1699-1712). This puts the persistent terms of `erl_features` (erl_features.erl:405-472), so `erl_features:keywords/0` later only reads them (erl_features.erl:474-479, 498-500). `io_lib:quote_atom/2` calls `erl_scan:reserved_word/1`, which calls `erl_features:keywords/0` (io_lib.erl:979-980; erl_scan.erl:2278-2283). `logger_h_common:log/2` formats a log event in the process that logs it (logger_h_common.erl:178-183). The boot process traps exits (init.erl:1197) and sets `user` as the group leader before it runs `-eval` (init.erl:1539-1546). `ac_tab` holds the environment of each loaded application, which `application:get_env/2,3` reads (application_controller.erl:353-361). The node runs until `halt/0` or `init:stop/0`. For `{stop, stop}`, `init` shuts down each kernel process except `heart` and `logger`: `application_controller` stops the running applications, so `kernel_sup` shuts down its children in the reverse start order (supervisor.erl:1574-1656). `init` then kills the other processes and `logger`, and calls `halt()` (init.erl:788-789, 924-1000). In a locale whose codeset is not UTF-8, `prim_tty:to_latin1/2` matches the output with `is_usascii/1` (prim_tty.erl:1309-1321), and `erl -noshell` prints the output with status 0. A Mix release starts with `-mode embedded`, `-config` of `releases/<version>/sys`, and `-boot` of `releases/<version>/start`. `application_controller` reads the `-config` file with `erl_prim_loader:read_file/1`, `erl_scan`, and `erl_parse` (application_controller.erl:1964-2021, 2057-2075). The boot script loads each application, starts `kernel` and `stdlib`, calls `'Elixir.Config.Provider':boot/0`, and then runs `application:start_boot(App, permanent)` for each other application (`releases/0.1.0/start.script` of `examples/plug_hello`). `Config.Provider.boot/0` evaluates `runtime.exs` with `Config.Reader` and sets the result with `Application.put_all_env/2` (Elixir v1.20.4 `lib/elixir/lib/config/provider.ex:218-280`). | The owner decided on 2026-10-11 to start the kernel as OTP does (#449): a new kernel child needs no change of `pbeam_boot`. The default boot of the pinned install starts `sasl` too (measured: `application:which_applications/0` of `erl -noshell` has `sasl`), so the comparisons give `-boot start_clean`. Embedded mode needs the `primLoad` commands, which need `erl_prim_loader:get_modules/2` and `erlang:has_prepared_code_on_load/1`. The standard IO servers and the logger do not need the other `init` requests or `heart`. The `default` logger handler needs the `kernel` environment in `ac_tab` and the `logger_sup` processes. The IO servers and the logger do not use the shell of `os:cmd/1,2`, and no task needs `os:cmd/1,2` yet. The default SIGTERM handler needs `init:stop/0` to stop the node, and the shutdown of `logger_sup` makes the `default` logger handler write its pending events before the halt. A halt stops each other process, so the kill of the other processes has no observable result in a run without ports. Binary match instructions are an independent instruction feature. The code server of #445 reads the path of `erl_prim_loader` (code_server.erl:513-525), so the boot starts the loader with the `pa` flags. It runs only the last `path` command, because the boot loads no module with `primLoad` (#445). (#139, #179, #183, #186, #463, #444, #658, #449) |
| Node boot | `BeamProgram::boot(Arguments)` runs the pinned `erl_init:start(init, Arguments)` as the first process `<0.0.0>`, with each argument as a binary of its bytes, as the emulator does. `erl_init:start/2` runs the `on_load` functions of `zlib`, `erl_tracer`, `prim_buffer`, and `prim_file`, and of `prim_socket` and `prim_net` when `prim_socket` is in the start set (erl_init.erl:42-48), and then `init:boot(Arguments)`. The `init` process registers its name in the name table of the node, reads the boot file of its `-boot` flag with `erl_prim_loader`, and evaluates the boot script in its boot process. PBeam runs the `preLoaded`, `progress`, `path`, `primLoad`, `kernel_load_completed`, `kernelProcess`, and `apply` commands as the pinned `init` does. With `-boot start_clean`, the pinned `start_clean.boot` loads the kernel and stdlib modules that are not in the start set through `erl_prim_loader`, runs `heart:start/0`, which gives `ignore` without the `-heart` flag, starts `logger` and `application_controller` as kernel processes, and starts `kernel` and `stdlib`. A boot script without `primLoad` needs each module that it calls in the start set; no module loads through `init:ensure_loaded/1` when it is in the start set. `application_controller` is an ordinary process with its registered name, and the `application` API runs the pinned code. `init:stop/0,1` runs the pinned stop flow: `init` shuts down `application_controller`, which stops each application, kills each process that is not a system process, and halts with the status, so the run ends with `BeamError::Halt(Status)`. `erts_internal:is_system_process/1` reads the system processes of the current scheduler, so a system process of another scheduler thread gives `false`. On Linux, with a short host name and no domain, `inet_config:init/0` looks up the host name with `inet:gethostbyname/1`, which runs the pinned `inet_gethost_native` code: it opens the `inet_gethost` port program of `$ROOT/erts-*/bin` with the options `overlapped_io`, `{packet, 4}`, and `eof` (row "Ports"), and exchanges packets with it. A domain in an inetrc file, for example through `ERL_INETRC`, prevents the lookup. The `init` process is a system process. The process of `erts_internal:spawn_system_process/3` is a system process too, and the BIF stops the run with the runtime error `No function M:F/A`, with the terms as ERTS prints them, when it finds no export. The exit of a system process stops the run with the runtime error `System process <Pid> terminated: <Reason>`, and PBeam writes no crash dump. PBeam starts no other system process: `erts_code_purger`, `erts_literal_area_collector`, the three `erts_dirty_process_signal_handler` processes, and `erts_trace_cleaner` do not run, and no PBeam code needs them. `erlang:loaded/0` gives the modules of the run, the last loaded first, and the start modules load in the order of the program. The boot runs on one scheduler thread. The form `pbeam erl` of the `pbeam` binary runs this boot (row "Release boot"); the form `pbeam MODULE FUNCTION` runs the `pbeam_boot` module of row "Boot". | The emulator spawns `erl_init:start(init, BootArgs)` as a system process with the boot arguments as binaries (`erl_init.c:355-399`, `2540`), and then the system processes `erts_code_purger`, `erts_literal_area_collector`, three `erts_dirty_process_signal_handler`, and `erts_trace_cleaner` (`erl_init.c:2543-2600`). `erl_init:start/2` runs the `on_load` functions and `init:boot/1` (`erl_init.erl:35-50`, `62-80`). `init:boot/1` registers `init`, traps exits, and spawns the boot process (`init.erl:586-596`, `656-663`, `1192-1224`), which starts `erl_prim_loader`, reads the boot file (`init.erl:1164-1184`, `1256-1308`), and evaluates the script (`init.erl:1317-1358`). The status of `init` follows the `progress` commands, and `init` enters its main loop at the `normal` exit of the boot process (`init.erl:683-728`, `783-797`). Measured with `erl -noshell -boot fixtures/boot/init_boot_probe -pa fixtures/boot`: `init` is `<0.0.0>`, the steps see `{starting, init_kernel_started}` and `{starting, applications_loaded}`, and the status after the boot is `{started, started}`. `erts_internal:spawn_system_process/3` looks up the export first and stops the emulator with `No function M:F/A` (`erl_init.c:333-353`, `436-456`). The exit of a system process stops the emulator with `System process <Pid> terminated: <Reason>` and a crash dump (`erl_process.c:14186-14188`). `erlang:loaded/0` gives the modules of the module table, the last index first (`beam_bif_load.c:762-791`). | The task runs the pinned `init` code on the node as ERTS does (#860). BIF timers across scheduler threads are not supported (row "BIF timers of a run with scheduler threads"), so the boot runs on one scheduler. The kernel boot runs the pinned `start_clean.boot` (#861). `inet_config:init/0` looks up the host name on Linux only when the domain is empty (`inet_config.erl:110-133`), and `inet_gethost_native:do_open_port/2` gives these port options (`inet_gethost_native.erl:412-428`). Measured with `erl -noshell -boot start_clean -pa fixtures/boot -s kernel_boot_probe main`: the application list, the results of `application:start/1`, `ensure_all_started/1,2`, and `stop/1`, the stop of `kernel_boot_app` in the stop flow, and the exit status of `init:stop/0` and `init:stop(3)` are the same as on PBeam. Measured with `erl -noshell -boot start_clean -pa fixtures/boot -s kernel_boot_probe lookup` without `ERL_INETRC`, on macOS and on Debian 13 in a container with the domain `[]`: the domain after the boot and the native lookups of the host name and of `localhost` are the same as on PBeam (#883). The `plug_hello` runner moves to the node boot in #855. The other system processes have no consumer yet. The boot creates system processes only on the scheduler of the run, so no consumer needs the system processes of another scheduler thread. (#860, #861) |
| Release boot | `pbeam erl --root DIR --bindir DIR --preloaded DIR -- ERL_ARGUMENT...` boots the node as `erl` does. `BeamProgram::load_preloaded(Dir)` loads the 22 modules of `erlang:pre_loaded/0` from `Dir/<Module>.beam`, in the ERTS preload order, also `socket_registry`, `prim_socket`, and `prim_net`. The binary then runs `BeamProgram::boot` (row "Node boot") with `-root Root -bindir Bindir -progname erl -- -home Home --` and the `erl` arguments unchanged; without `HOME`, it gives no `-home`. Thus the arguments that `bin/<app> start` of a Mix release gives `erl` (`ELIXIR_CLI_DRY_RUN=1`), such as `-noshell -s elixir start_cli -mode embedded -config <rel>/releases/<vsn>/sys -boot <rel>/releases/<vsn>/start -boot_var RELEASE_LIB <rel>/lib -extra --no-halt`, boot the release. The fixture release `fixtures/release_boot` gives the same application list, application environment of `sys.config`, code mode `embedded`, plain arguments, loaded preloaded modules, and exit status 0 of `init:stop/0` as the pinned `erl`. Before `-extra`, the binary stops with the error `the erl option <Option> is not supported` for an argument that starts with `+` and for `-S`, `-args_file`, `-compile`, `-configfd`, `-detached`, `-emu_args`, `-emu_args_exit`, `-emu_flavor`, `-emu_name_exit`, `-emu_qouted_cmd_exit`, `-emu_type`, `-env`, `-epmd`, `-keep_window`, `-make`, `-man`, `-name`, `-no_epmd`, `-noinput`, `-proto_dist`, `-smp`, `-smpauto`, `-smpdisable`, `-smpenable`, `-sname`, `-start_epmd`, and `-version`. `-emu_type` and `-emu_flavor` select the emulator, `erlexec` checks the `-smp` options and `-no_epmd` (`erlexec.c:499-534`, `1118-1123`), and `-S` makes it rewrite the later arguments and add `-noshell` (`erlexec.c:1084-1090`, `2222-2238`). Each `erlexec` option with an operand is in this list, so the first `-extra` is the one that `erlexec` reads as an option. It stops with the error `the environment variable <Name> of erl is not supported` when `ERL_OTP29_FLAGS`, `ERL_AFLAGS`, `ERL_FLAGS`, or `ERL_ZFLAGS` is set and not empty, and when `ERL_EPMD_PORT` is set. `-setcookie` goes to `init` unchanged, as `erlexec` gives it. The preloaded modules are files of the `--preloaded` directory, not modules that are built into the binary. Before the boot, `pbeam erl` changes `PATH` of the environment copy of the run as `erlexec` does: the bin directory comes first, and `$ROOT/bin` follows it when `PATH` does not contain the root (`erlexec.c:556-620`). `scripts/example.sh` and the launcher of a release bundle boot `examples/plug_hello` with the arguments of `bin/plug_hello start` with `RELEASE_DISTRIBUTION=none`, without `-setcookie` and `-args_file`, and with `RELEASE_ROOT` and `RELEASE_SYS_CONFIG`, which the release boot reads (`Config.Provider` of `sys.config`); the release script also sets the other `RELEASE_*` variables, which the boot does not read. `GET /` gives the status line, the headers without `date`, and the body of the pinned ERTS run of the same arguments (`tests/feature/serve.rs`). `GET /` with `Accept-Encoding: zstd` gives the status line, the headers, and the compressed body of the pinned ERTS run, and the run serves the next request (row "NIF stubs"). | The `erl` script sets the root and `$ROOT/erts-<Vsn>/bin` as the bin directory and runs `erlexec`. `erlexec` gives the emulator `-root`, `-bindir`, `-progname`, and `-home` with the value of `HOME` when it is set (`erts/etc/common/erlexec.c:625-640`, `1151-1161`, `1746-1750`), adds the flags of `ERL_OTP29_FLAGS` and `ERL_AFLAGS` before the command line and those of `ERL_FLAGS` and `ERL_ZFLAGS` after it, and moves their `-extra` arguments to the end (`erlexec.c:2177-2260`), reads `-args_file` files (`erlexec.c:2122-2140`), and copies each argument after `-extra` unchanged (`erlexec.c:637-640`, `708-712`). It reads the options of the list before it starts the emulator: `+` flags go to the emulator (`erlexec.c:869-964`), `-name` and `-sname` start the distribution (`erlexec.c:774-785`, `812-818`), `-proto_dist` and `-start_epmd` configure it (`erlexec.c:801-806`, `835-850`), `ERL_EPMD_PORT` gives `-epmd_port` (`erlexec.c:633`, `1754-1760`), `-env` sets a variable, `-noinput` adds `-noshell`, and `-detached`, `-compile`, `-make`, `-man`, `-version`, and `-emu_*` change the start (`erlexec.c:661-861`). Each other argument goes to `init` unchanged. The emulator loads its built-in preloaded modules before the first process (`erts/emulator/Makefile.in:804-828`, `erl_init.c:459-484`). Measured: `erl -emu_args_exit -noshell -mode embedded -config c/sys -boot b/start -boot_var RELEASE_LIB lib -s m f -extra --no-halt a` gives `-root`, `-bindir`, `-progname erl`, `-home`, and then the arguments in their order. | PBeam has no `erlexec` and no distribution. A clear error for each `erlexec` option keeps a boot from running with other arguments than ERTS gives `init`. `-setcookie` is an `init` flag that only the `auth` server of the distribution reads (`auth.erl:348-352`), so without `-sname` or `-name` the result is the same as on ERTS. `scripts/example.sh` and the launcher of a release bundle omit `-args_file` because `pbeam erl` rejects it; the `vm.args` of `mix release` holds only comments, so `erlexec` reads no argument from it. (#889, #855) |
| Persistent terms | The node keeps one persistent term table, and each scheduler thread of the run reads it. Each run starts with an empty persistent term table of 8 slots. A key gets its slot from the ERTS internal hash (`src/term/hash.rs`). A pid key hashes the PBeam process number as its pid data. A reference key hashes the PBeam reference counter as its counter value, with scheduler number 1. A key with an atom that is not static hashes the PBeam atom index. A key with an external fun hashes the word `(module index << 40) \| (function index << 20) \| arity` in place of the address of the ERTS export entry. Thus `persistent_term:get/0` gives the ERTS slot order only for keys that contain no pid, no reference, no external fun, and no atom that is not static, and only when the ERTS table also starts empty, as after `erts_internal:erase_persistent_terms()`. `persistent_term:get/0` that traps, `persistent_term:put/2`, and `persistent_term:erase/1` take the update permission of the node, as ERTS does. A call that finds the permission with another process uses all its reductions and suspends, as ERTS does. The release of the permission, also at the exit of its holder, resumes each waiting process with a `Resume` signal, and the process tries its call again. A message or a signal does not wake a suspended process, and an exit signal still ends it. `persistent_term:put/2` of a new value and `persistent_term:erase/1` of a key with a value build the new table and always switch out and suspend the caller. In place of thread progress, the new table becomes visible after three scheduling steps of the scheduler of the caller, after the caller switched out; then the permission is released and the caller resumes.
